Classic ML

Classic machine learning questions.

    Block 1

  1. The supervised learning problem↗

    Machine learning "with a teacher" is called supervised learning. Its essence is that, for training, the model receives a special dataset in which it is marked in advance what the data means. That is, the model receives both the question and the answer it should give. It "looks" at large volumes of such data and thereby learns to answer correctly.

    Formally: we are given a sample of pairs $(x_i, y_i)$, where $x_i$ is the description of an object and $y_i$ is the correct answer. We need to build a function $a: X \to Y$ that gives correct answers on new objects that were not in the training data.

    In the simplest terms: it's learning from a workbook with an answer key. A student is given thousands of problems along with the correct answers, notices the patterns, and then takes the exam on problems that weren't in the workbook. The word "teacher" (supervisor) here means not a person standing nearby, but simply the fact that the data contains correct answers.

    We predict whether a customer will repay a loan.

    • Object $x_i$ — a table row: age 34, income 90,000, 5 years of work experience, 2 late payments.
    • Answer $y_i$ — what actually happened: repaid (0) or didn't repay (1). These labels come from the bank's history — they are already known because the events happened in the past.
    • Training — the model goes through 100,000 such rows and finds the dependency between the features and the outcome.
    • Inference — a new customer comes in, their outcome is unknown, and the model makes a prediction.

    The key point here is that the labels had to come from somewhere. For loans, history itself provides them, but in the task "is there a tumor in this image" they have to be obtained from doctors who label the images by hand.

    • Supervised: there are correct answers, and the model learns to reproduce them. Quality is measured objectively — by comparing predictions with the truth (accuracy, RMSE, etc.).
    • Unsupervised: there are no answers; the model looks for structure on its own — clustering, dimensionality reduction, anomaly detection. There is no single correct answer, so quality evaluation is far more subjective.
    • In-between options: semi-supervised learning (a small part of the data is labeled), self-supervised learning (labels are constructed from the data itself, as in language models, where the answer is the next word in the text).
    • Labels are neither free nor perfect. Labeling costs money and time, and labelers make mistakes and disagree with each other. The model can't be more accurate than the labels it was trained on: it will faithfully learn other people's mistakes too.
    • The label must be available at prediction time. If the training table contains a field that is filled in only after the event occurs, the model will learn "the future" from it and fall apart in production (this is data leakage).
    • Biased labeling gives a biased model. If only approved applications are labeled, the model knows nothing about those who were rejected — and its predictions for that part of the customer base will be unfounded.

    For such training to be possible, the training dataset must first be collected, reviewed by hand, and labeled. This must be done by a person or a group of people — the very "teacher" in question.

    1. What is the essence of supervised learning?

      The model is trained on a dataset where each object has a labeled answer. The task is to find a function $a: X \to Y$ that correctly predicts labels from the input data on new, unseen objects.

    2. What is a training set and what does it contain?

      A set of pairs $(x_i, y_i)$, where $x_i$ is the feature description of an object and $y_i$ is the correct answer. It is labeled by hand by a human expert, the "teacher".

    3. Which tasks are solved with supervised learning methods?

      Classification (discrete labels: spam/not spam, animal species) and regression (continuous values: apartment price, age from a photo).

    4. What is the main limitation of supervised learning?

      The need to label data by hand — expensive, slow, and requiring expertise. Model quality directly depends on labeling quality.

    5. How does supervised learning differ from unsupervised and self-supervised learning?

      In supervised learning there are ready-made correct answers, and quality is measured by comparing predictions with them. In unsupervised learning there are no answers; the model looks for structure on its own (clustering, dimensionality reduction), and there's no objective correctness metric. In self-supervised learning, labels are constructed from the data itself — for example, predicting the next word in a text — which makes it possible to learn from unlabeled corpora.

    6. Why can't a model be more accurate than the labels it was trained on?

      Training amounts to reproducing the labels: the model will learn systematic labeler errors as correct answers. So the quality ceiling is set by the consistency and correctness of the labeling, and improving it is often more cost-effective than making the model more complex.

  2. Datasets and splits

    A sample (dataset) is a subset of data used in the process of training and evaluating machine learning models. It is usually a table, an array, or a data structure containing input characteristics (features) and the corresponding output values (labels, targets).

    In the simplest terms: a dataset is your table of data. Rows are objects (customers, emails, images), columns are features, and a separate column holds the correct answer the model must learn to predict.

    Behind all of machine learning lies one unspoken hypothesis: the training data and future data come from the same distribution.

    Only under this condition does it make sense to learn from the past and apply what was learned to the future. If the assumption is violated — the distribution has changed — the model breaks, and no test metric will warn you about it.

    That's why data drift is monitored separately in production: the test set checks the model on data from the same period, while the model will have to work on data that doesn't exist yet.

    In machine learning it's customary to split the data into three types of sets:

    • Training set — used to build the model. The model "learns" to find patterns in this data.
    • Validation set — needed to tune the model's hyperparameters and prevent overfitting. The model doesn't see this data during training, but it is evaluated on it while the configuration is being chosen.
    • Test set — used only once, after the model's final tuning, to evaluate its quality on "unseen" data.

    Imagine a handwritten digit recognition task. You have a set of images of the digits 0 to 9:

    • 60,000 images go to the training set
    • 10,000 images to the validation set
    • 10,000 images to the test set

    The model is first trained on the first 60,000; during training the tuned model is checked on the validation set, and only then is the final model tested on the remaining 10,000.

    When training on the training set, the model adapts to the data and tries to tune its parameters, while on the validation set we compute quality metrics — how badly the model errs on data that doesn't take part in tuning the model's parameters. Next, on the test set we compute the final quality metrics, which show how the model will perform on new data. At this stage the model's generalization ability is checked.

    The test set must be completely isolated from the model until the final evaluation. If the model is fitted to the test set, this is considered a violation of the integrity of the experiment (data leakage).

    • Splitting before deduplication. Duplicates end up in both train and test, the model meets the very same rows in the test set — the metric soars, the usefulness is zero.
    • Random splitting with temporal dependence. The model learns from the future and predicts the past. The estimate comes out fantastic and completely unrealistic.
    • Related objects in different parts. Several images of the same patient, orders of the same customer, frames from the same video. You need a group split.
    • A test set that's too small. On 100 objects the metric has a spread of several percent, and comparing models with it is meaningless.
    • Reusing the test set many times. Every decision made based on the test metric gradually turns it into a validation metric.

    1. Into which three parts is a dataset split, and what is each one for?

      Train — for training the model (fitting the parameters). Validation — for tuning hyperparameters and monitoring overfitting during training. Test — the final quality evaluation, used only once.

    2. What is data leakage and why is it a serious problem?

      Data leakage is when information from the test (or validation) set influences model training. The result: inflated test metrics that don't reflect the real generalization ability.

    3. Why can't the test set be used for hyperparameter tuning?

      Repeated tuning on the test set causes the model to "adapt" to it. The test set should imitate new data — using it for decisions about the model means peeking into the "future".

    4. What is a model's generalization ability?

      The model's ability to work correctly on new, unseen data. It is evaluated on the test set. High quality on train with low quality on test is a sign of overfitting.

    5. What key assumption underlies splitting data into sets?

      That training data and future data come from the same distribution. Only then does test quality say anything about quality in production. If the distribution changes over time (data drift), the test estimate stops being relevant, and this is monitored separately after deployment.

    6. Why should duplicates be found before splitting?

      Otherwise copies of the same row will end up in both train and test. The model will meet objects in the test set that it literally memorized, the metric will be inflated, and you won't learn the real quality on new data.

    7. Why can't models be compared on a small test set?

      A metric on a hundred objects has a spread of several percent simply due to the random composition of the sample. A 1–2% difference between models is indistinguishable from noise in this situation. You need either a larger test set or cross-validation with an estimate of the spread across folds.

  3. What machine learning is and types of tasks↗

    Machine learning is the field concerned with creating algorithms that allow computers to improve their behavior based on data (experience) without being explicitly programmed.

    In the simplest terms: instead of writing rules, we show examples. In ordinary programming, a human formulates "if the amount is over 100,000 and the customer is new, review manually". In machine learning, we give the algorithm 100,000 past transactions labeled "fraud / not fraud", and it derives the rules itself.

    This is the most practical question of the topic, and the answer is not "always".

    ML is justified when rules exist but cannot be written down by hand:

    • there are too many of them (face recognition — you cannot describe in words how one face differs from another);
    • they change constantly (spam, fraud schemes);
    • they are not obvious even to an expert (which factors predict customer churn).

    ML is not needed when:

    • the rule is known and stable — "VAT is 20% of the amount" is one line of code and requires no training;
    • there is little data or it is unlabeled;
    • the cost of an error is such that guaranteed correctness is required, not statistical correctness.

    The key difference: an ordinary program gives a guaranteed correct answer according to a given rule, while a model gives a probably correct answer derived from data. A model is always wrong on some fraction of objects, and the system has to be designed with that in mind.

    In other words, we want to teach a machine/computer to notice cause-and-effect relationships based on some real-world data. In mathematics, such algorithms that solve real-world problems or give sufficiently accurate approximations to them are called models, or mathematical models.

    From here on, all our machine learning algorithms — whether classical algorithms from the Classic ML section, deep learning (DL), or any other field — will be called models or simply neural nets

    Suppose we are given:

    • A set of objects $X$ (this can be anything: for example, the set of all passengers on a bus. Each is described by some properties — age, sex, weight, ...), as well as
    • A set of labels $Y$ (also often called answers, ground truth — GT, or targets), and
    • An unknown (true) dependency $$y^*: X \to Y$$

    Our task is to find a model $a: X \to Y$ that predicts answers $Y$ from the input data $X$, i.e., approximates $y^*$.

    The two letters here are fundamentally different: $y^*$ is how the world actually works (it is not available to us; we only see its values on labeled objects), and $a$ is what we built from data. The model's prediction on object $x_i$ is denoted $\hat{y}_i = a(x_i)$: the "hat" always means an estimate of a quantity, not the quantity itself.

    For example, we want to predict the price of an apartment in central Moscow based on the information we have. Suppose we know

    • the number of rooms
    • the district
    • the crime rate in the district
    • access to the metro
    • neighborhood amenities
    • the presence of pharmacies/grocery stores
    • and so on

    All this information makes up the feature set (features), which we denote by $X$. And our model is some algorithm that predicts $Y$ from $X$: $$a: X \to Y$$ The goal of machine learning is to tune the model so that the prediction error is minimal. But we will talk about that below

    We have a training set

    $$\{(x_1, y_1), \ldots, (x_\ell, y_\ell)\}.$$

    We need to find an algorithm

    $$a: X \to Y,$$

    that approximates the true (unknown) dependency $y^*$ on the training set $X$.

    The explanation above says that the model learns to notice cause-and-effect relationships. This is a convenient metaphor, but it needs an important caveat: models find correlations, not causes.

    A classic example: a model discovers that customers who were called by the retention team churn more often. Does this mean the calls cause churn? No — the team called precisely those who were already about to leave. Cause and effect are reversed, but the correlation is strong and the model will use it.

    Practical consequences:

    • You can predict, but you cannot intervene. The model will correctly predict churn from the fact of a call, but banning calls will not reduce churn.
    • Features that are consequences of the target create leakage. They work great on historical data and fall apart in production.
    • Questions of the form "what happens if we do X" require experiments (A/B tests) or causal inference methods, not an ordinary predictive model.

    Each object is described by features $f_1, \ldots, f_n$, which map $X$ into the corresponding sets $D_1, \ldots, D_n$. Features can be:

    • Binary: $D = \{0,1\}$
    • Nominal (categorical): a finite set without order
    • Ordinal: a finite, ordered set
    • Numerical: $D = \mathbb{R}$

    Objects are often represented as an object–feature matrix.

    TaskGoal
    ClassificationPredicting an object's class
    RegressionPredicting a numerical value
    RankingOrdering objects by relevance
    ClusteringGrouping similar objects without supervision
    Learning methodDescription
    SupervisedLabels are known in advance.
    UnsupervisedLabels are unknown.
    Semi-supervisedSome objects are labeled.
    ReinforcementLearning through interaction with an environment.
    Online learningData arrives sequentially, and the model is updated on the fly.
    Active learningThe algorithm interacts with an oracle that labels data on request.

    The oracle can be a human expert who labels documents, images, or videos. Labeling can take time and money — for example, a chemical analysis of a substance.

    • Predicting mineral deposits: features — geological data; goal — identifying areas with a high probability of containing minerals.
    • Credit scoring: features — the borrower's application form and banking information; label — reliable / unreliable.
    • Medical diagnosis: features — symptoms, test results, medical history; goal — making a diagnosis, forecasting recovery, choosing treatment.
    • The classic iris problem (Fisher 1936): classifying flowers by features (petal length, sepal length, etc.).

    1. What is machine learning and what does "learning from data" mean?

      ML is the creation of algorithms that improve their behavior based on data without explicit programming. "Learning" means tuning the parameters of a model $a: X \to Y$ so that the error on the training set $\{(x_i, y_i)\}$ is minimal.

    2. What types of tasks exist in ML?

      Classification (predicting a class), regression (a numerical value), ranking (ordering objects by relevance), clustering (partitioning into groups without labels). Deep learning and various domains (CV, NLP) have more complex tasks, but we will not cover them for now

    3. How does supervised learning differ from unsupervised learning?

      In supervised learning, each object has a label $y_i$ and the model learns to predict it. In unsupervised learning there are no labels — the algorithm searches for structure in the data on its own (clustering, dimensionality reduction).

    4. What are an object's features and how are they classified?

      Features are numerical characteristics of an object that describe its properties. They can be binary $\{0,1\}$, nominal (categorical without order), ordinal (with an ordering), or numerical $\mathbb{R}$.

    5. When should a problem be solved with machine learning and when with ordinary code?

      ML is justified when rules exist but cannot be written down by hand: there are too many of them (face recognition), they change (spam, fraud), or they are not obvious even to an expert. Ordinary code is better when the rule is known and stable, there is little data, or guaranteed rather than statistical correctness is required.

    6. Why do people say models find correlations, not causes?

      A model uses any statistical relationship between the features and the target regardless of its direction. If the retention team called those who were already about to leave, the model will link calls to churn — but banning calls will not reduce churn. Questions of the form "what happens if we do X" require experiments or causal inference methods.

    7. What types of features are there and why does the distinction matter?

      Binary $\{0,1\}$, nominal (categories without order), ordinal (categories with an order), and numerical. The distinction determines preprocessing: nominal features require one-hot or target encoding, ordinal features can be encoded as numbers while preserving the order, and numerical features are scaled for distance-sensitive models.

  4. Definition of classification↗

    Classification is a machine learning task in which an object must be assigned to one of a set of classes known in advance.

    Formally: we need to build a function $a: X \to Y$, where the set of answers $Y$ is finite. The key words here are "known in advance" and "finite": the list of classes is fixed before training, and the model cannot invent a new class.

    In the simplest terms: it's sorting things into labeled boxes. The boxes are prepared in advance — "spam" and "not spam", "cat", "dog", "bird" — and each new object must be placed into one of them. The model's job is not to invent a box but to pick the right one.

    Possible answer sets:

    • Binary classification: $Y = \{-1, +1\}$ — two classes. Alternatively we can write $Y = \{0, 1\}$ (negative and positive classes). Depending on the context, one notation or the other is more convenient
    • Multiclass classification: $Y = \{1, \ldots, M\}$ — several non-overlapping classes.
    • Multilabel classification: one object can belong to several classes at once.

    These tasks are constantly confused, although the difference is simple:

    • Classification is supervised learning: the classes are known in advance and the training objects have correct labels. We teach the model to reproduce an existing partition.
    • Clustering is unsupervised learning: there are no labels, and the algorithm finds groups on its own. It doesn't know what they mean and can't call them "cats" and "dogs" — only "group 1" and "group 2".

    The test question: do you have labeled examples? If yes, it's classification; if not, it's clustering.

    For each object $x$ we need to predict a label $y$ from a finite set of classes. The model builds a decision boundary in feature space: objects on one side get one label, objects on the other side get another.

    The key difference from regression: the answer is discrete (class membership) rather than continuous. That is why the loss functions and quality metrics are fundamentally different: cross-entropy is used instead of MSE, and accuracy, F1, and AUC instead of RMSE.

    • Determining whether an email is spam.
    • Classifying images into cats and dogs.
    • Recognizing handwritten digits (MNIST).

    An important subtlety that almost all beginners miss: most classifiers output not a class but a number from 0 to 1 — an estimate of the probability of belonging to the positive class.

    The label is obtained afterwards by comparing with a threshold: 1 if p > 0.5, else 0. The 0.5 threshold is the default, but it is not part of the model and is almost never optimal: in anti-fraud it's reasonable to raise an alarm already at $p > 0.1$, while for sending an expensive offer, only at $p > 0.8$.

    The practical takeaway: you can change the threshold without retraining the model. It's the cheapest way to adapt a trained model to changing business requirements.

    • Assuming there are exactly as many classes as in training. In production, objects arrive that don't belong to any known class. The model will still output one of them — and confidently. For such cases, an "other" class or a separate novelty check is added.
    • Confusing multiclass and multilabel. In the first case the probabilities of all classes sum to one (softmax); in the second, each class is scored independently (a sigmoid on each output). These are different loss functions and different metrics.
    • Ignoring class imbalance. With 1% positive objects, a model that always answers "0" gets 99% accuracy. The metric must be chosen before training, based on the cost of errors.
    • Forcing regression into classification. Binning a continuous target into "cheap / medium / expensive" loses information and creates artificial boundaries: objects on opposite sides of a cutoff are nearly indistinguishable, yet the model is forced to separate them.
    • k-nearest neighbors (k-NN)
    • Logistic regression
    • Decision trees
    • Ensembles: Random Forest, Gradient Boosting, XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost
    • Naive Bayes classifier
    • Neural networks

    1. What is classification and how does it differ from regression?

      Classification predicts a discrete label from a finite set of classes. Regression predicts a continuous value. Classification uses cross-entropy as the loss function and accuracy/F1/AUC as quality metrics.

    2. How do binary, multiclass, and multilabel classification differ?

      Binary: $Y = \{0, 1\}$ (two classes). Multiclass: $Y = \{1, \ldots, M\}$ (one class out of several). Multilabel: an object can belong to several classes simultaneously.

    3. What is a decision boundary?

      A boundary in feature space: objects on one side belong to one class, objects on the other side to another. Model complexity determines the shape of this boundary: a linear model builds a hyperplane, neural networks build an arbitrary nonlinear surface.

    4. Name the main classification algorithms.

      k-NN (nearest neighbors), logistic regression, decision trees, random forest, gradient boosting (XGBoost, LightGBM, CatBoost), naive Bayes, SVM, neural networks.

    5. How does classification differ from clustering?

      Classification is supervised learning: the classes are known in advance and training objects have labels. Clustering is unsupervised learning: there are no labels, and the algorithm finds groups on its own without knowing what they mean. The test question is whether you have labeled examples.

    6. What does a classifier actually output, and what is a threshold?

      Most models output an estimate of the probability of belonging to the positive class — a number from 0 to 1. The label is obtained by comparing with a threshold, 0.5 by default. The threshold is not part of the model, so it can be changed without retraining, adjusting the precision/recall balance to the cost of errors.

    7. How does multiclass classification differ from multilabel in terms of model design?

      In a multiclass task the class probabilities are normalized with softmax and sum to one — the object belongs to exactly one class. In multilabel, each class is scored independently with a sigmoid, the probabilities don't sum to one, and an object can belong to several classes at once. These are different loss functions and different metrics.

  5. Definition of Regression↗

    Regression is the task of predicting a numeric (continuous) value from an object's set of features. Formally, the output space is $Y = \mathbb{R}$ or $Y = \mathbb{R}^m$.

    In the simplest terms: the answer is a number, not the name of a box. In classification we choose from a fixed list ("spam" / "not spam"), whereas here we name a quantity — 4,350,000 rubles, 23.7 degrees, 148 orders. The list of possible answers is infinite, and that changes everything else: loss functions, metrics, and how errors are evaluated.

    Three practical differences follow from the continuity of the answer:

    • An error has a magnitude. In classification, an answer is either right or wrong. In regression, missing by 100 rubles and missing by a million are fundamentally different events, and the loss function must tell them apart.
    • An exact hit is impossible. The probability of guessing a price down to the kopeck is zero, so "accuracy" makes no sense here — you measure the size of the deviation (MAE, RMSE).
    • The answer has units. Is an error of 5000 rubles a lot or a little? It depends on whether the apartment costs 3 million or 300 million. Hence percentage metrics (MAPE) and metrics relative to a baseline ($R^2$).

    The goal of regression is to build a function $a(x)$ that approximates the true relationship between inputs and outputs by minimizing the prediction error.

    To avoid confusion later, let's fix the notation:

    • $N$ is the number of objects in the dataset;
    • $x_i$ are the features of the $i$-th object, $i = 1, \ldots, N$;
    • $y_i$ is the true value of the target for this object (what's recorded in the data);
    • $a$ is the model itself, i.e., the function we built;
    • $\hat{y}_i$ (read "y-hat") is the predicted value the model outputs for object $x_i$.

    The last two are related by the equality $\hat{y}_i = a(x_i)$: these aren't two different concepts but two ways of writing the same number. The notation $a(x_i)$ emphasizes that the prediction is the result of the model's work; the notation $\hat{y}_i$ is shorter and convenient in formulas. That's why the mean squared error is written in its familiar form

    $$MSE = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(y_i - \hat{y}_i\right)^2 = \frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left(y_i - a(x_i)\right)^2$$

    In mathematics, a hat over a letter generally means "an estimate of the quantity, not the quantity itself": $\hat{y}$ is the model's estimate of $y$.

    There's no single notation in machine learning, and in the following sections (let alone in textbooks and papers) you'll see the same quantities written differently:

    • sample size — $N$, $n$, $m$, or $\ell$;
    • model prediction — $\hat{y}_i$, $a(x_i)$, or simply $a_i$;
    • true value — $y_i$ or $y(x_i)$.

    These aren't different concepts but different letters for the same things. A useful habit: in every new formula, first figure out which is the true value, which is the prediction, and what the sum runs over.

    Regression is used when you need to predict a value, for example: a price, a speed, a temperature, a sales volume, etc.

    A useful rule of thumb: if there's a meaningful midpoint between two possible answers, you're looking at regression. Between 100 and 200 rubles lies 150 — that's regression. Nothing lies between "cat" and "dog" — that's classification.

    A borderline case is ordered categories ("bad / okay / excellent," a rating from 1 to 5). Formally these are classes, but they have an order, and being off by two points is worse than by one. Such problems are solved either with ordinal regression or with ordinary regression followed by rounding.

    • Turning regression into classification. Splitting a price into "cheap / medium / expensive" seems convenient, but objects on either side of a boundary are nearly indistinguishable, and the model is forced to separate them. Information about magnitude is irretrievably lost.
    • Ignoring error asymmetry. MSE penalizes underestimation and overestimation equally, although in demand forecasting these events have completely different costs. You need an asymmetric loss function (pinball loss).
    • Forgetting the range of the answer. A model can predict a negative price or a negative number of orders. Log-transforming the target (with an inverse transform) or models that operate in the right range help.
    • Judging quality only by the average. MAE hides the tails: two models with the same MAE can behave completely differently on the worst percent of objects.
    • Predicting an apartment's price from its characteristics.
    • Forecasting a store's profit next month.
    • Estimating a person's age from a photo.
    • Linear regression
    • Polynomial regression
    • Neural networks

    1. What is regression and how does it differ from classification?

      Regression predicts a continuous numeric value ($Y \subseteq \mathbb{R}$). Classification predicts a discrete class. The key difference: in regression the answer is continuous, so MSE/MAE are used instead of cross-entropy.

    2. Give examples of regression tasks in ML.

      Predicting apartment prices, forecasting sales, estimating age from a photo, predicting temperature. Any task where the answer is a number.

    3. What does "approximating a relationship" mean in regression?

      Finding a function $a(x)$ that, given an object's features, outputs a number as close as possible to the true value $y$. The model's prediction is denoted $\hat{y}_i = a(x_i)$, and the true value $y_i$. "Closeness" is measured by a loss function — for example, MSE: $\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2$.

    4. What are the main methods for solving regression?

      Linear regression (linear relationship), polynomial regression (nonlinear via polynomials), decision trees and ensembles (nonlinear, with no assumptions about the shape of the relationship), neural networks (universal approximators).

    5. How can you quickly tell whether it's regression or classification?

      Check whether there's a meaningful midpoint between two possible answers. Between 100 and 200 rubles lies 150 — regression. Nothing lies between "cat" and "dog" — classification. A special case is ordered categories (a rating from 1 to 5), where being off by two points is worse than by one.

    6. Why isn't accuracy used in regression?

      The answer is continuous, so the probability of an exact hit is zero — the share of "correct" answers would always be zero. What's measured is not whether values match but the size of the deviation: MAE, RMSE, error quantiles.

    7. Why is binning a continuous target into categories a bad idea?

      Category boundaries are artificial: objects on either side of a threshold are nearly indistinguishable, yet the model must separate them, while within one category the difference in magnitude is completely lost. Information about how expensive an object is disappears, and quality usually drops.

    8. How does $\hat{y}_i$ differ from $a(x_i)$?

      It doesn't — they're the same number written two ways: $\hat{y}_i = a(x_i)$. $a$ denotes the model itself, and the "hat" over $y$ means "an estimate of the quantity, not the quantity itself": $\hat{y}_i$ is the model's prediction, and $y_i$ is the true value from the data. Their difference is exactly what sits inside MSE.

  6. Loss function and empirical risk↗

    A loss function is a mapping from an algorithm's output to a number that expresses the "cost" of the error it made on a single object. It depends on the prediction $a(x)$ and the true label $y(x)$.

    In the simplest terms: it is a way to tell the model what counts as an error. The model does not understand the words "predict well" — it needs a number to decrease. The loss function is that number, and all of training boils down to minimizing it.

    The key idea to grasp right away: the model optimizes exactly what you wrote into the loss function, not what you meant.

    Example: warehouse demand forecasting. You pick MSE — familiar and convenient. But MSE is symmetric: under-ordering by 10 units is penalized the same as over-ordering by 10. In reality these are completely different events: over-ordering means frozen money and warehouse space, while under-ordering means lost sales and a customer who went to a competitor.

    A model trained on MSE will dutifully err "equally in both directions" — because that is exactly what you asked it to do. To get different behavior you need an asymmetric loss function (e.g., pinball loss), not a cleverer model.

    They are regularly confused, although their roles differ:

    • The loss function is what is minimized during training. It must be convenient for optimization: smooth, differentiable, preferably convex.
    • The metric is what is used to evaluate the result and make decisions. It must be understandable to a human and reflect the business meaning.

    They often differ, and that is normal: you cannot train a model directly on accuracy or $F_1$ — these functions are piecewise constant, their gradient is zero almost everywhere, and gradient descent has nothing to grab onto. So you train on log loss and look at $F_1$.

    Function type Formula Explanation
    0-1 loss $L(a,x) = [a(x) \ne y(x)]$ No longer used in practice; for classification. Equals $1$ if $a(x) \ne y(x)$ (the prediction does not match the true value) and $0$ if $a(x) = y(x)$.
    Squared (MSE) $L(a,x) = (a(x) - y(x))^2$ Used for regression. $a(x), y(x) \in \mathbb{R}$. We look at the squared difference between the prediction and the true value.
    Log loss Let $a_i = a(x_i; w)$ be the output of the algorithm with parameters $w$ on object $x_i$ (the probability of class 1), and $y_i$ the true value; then:
    $$L(a_i,y_i) = -[y_i \log a_i + (1 - y_i) \log(1 - a_i)]$$     		Used for binary classification, popular
    Cross-entropy loss The multiclass case, $M$ classes. Let $a_i = a(x_i; w)$ be the output of the algorithm with parameters $w$ on object $x_i$ (the probabilities of class $k$, $k\in M$ — i.e., some class $k$ from the set of classes $M$), and $y_i$ the true value; then:
    $$L(a_i,y_i) = -\sum_{k=1}^M y_i \log a_i$$     		Used for multiclass classification, popular

    This is exactly the example from the previous block. The fraction of wrong answers is a perfectly understandable metric but a hopeless loss function:

    • it is piecewise constant: a small change in the weights almost never changes the label, so the loss does not change at all;
    • its gradient is zero wherever it exists — there is nothing to optimize;
    • it does not distinguish a confident error from a "hair's breadth" error: predictions of 0.51 and 0.99 with a true class of 0 are penalized equally.

    That is why it is replaced with smooth surrogates — log loss, hinge loss (in SVM), exponential loss (in AdaBoost). All of them are upper bounds on the 0-1 loss: by minimizing the surrogate, we also push the error rate toward zero, but in a differentiable way.

    Empirical risk is the algorithm's average error on the training set:

    $$Q(a, X_m) = \frac{1}{m} \sum_{x \in X} L(a, x),$$

    where $X_m$ is the training set and $m$ is its size.

    The main idea is to choose an algorithm $a^*$ that minimizes the average error on the training set:

    $$a^* = \arg\min_{a \in \mathcal{A}} Q(a, X_m)$$

    This is a simple and universal approach that often reduces to a numerical optimization problem. However, it can lead to overfitting — situations where the algorithm fits the training data too closely and loses its ability to generalize.

    For a regression task, the squared loss function can be used,

    $$L(a,x) = (a(x) - y(x))^2,$$

    and empirical risk minimization then corresponds to the least squares method:

    $$a^* = \arg\min_{a \in \mathcal{A}} \underbrace{\frac{1}{m} \sum_{x \in X_m} \underbrace{(a(x) - y(x))^2}_{L(a,x)}}_{Q(a, X_m)}.$$

    Empirical risk is computed on a finite training set, whereas what we really want to minimize is the error on all future data (called the true risk). The whole difficulty of machine learning lies between the two:

    • the training set contains noise, and a model driven to zero empirical risk usually learns that noise as well;
    • the richer the model class $\mathcal{A}$ we choose from, the lower the achievable empirical risk — and the larger the gap with the true risk.

    This is the source of all the practical techniques: regularization (a complexity penalty right in the loss function), early stopping, restricting the model class, and honest evaluation on held-out data. Formally, low empirical risk is a necessary but not sufficient condition for a good model.

    1. What is a loss function and why is it needed?

      A loss function measures the "cost" of the model's error on a single object — how far the prediction $a(x)$ diverges from the true $y(x)$. The choice of loss function determines what exactly the model optimizes.

    2. How does log loss differ from cross-entropy?

      Log loss is cross-entropy for binary classification (2 classes): $-[y\log a + (1-y)\log(1-a)]$. Cross-entropy generalizes it to $M$ classes: $-\sum_k y_k \log a_k$. For $M=2$ they coincide.

    3. What is empirical risk and what is the essence of its minimization?

      Empirical risk is the average loss over the training set: $Q(a, X_m) = \frac{1}{m}\sum L(a, x_i)$. Training is the search for an algorithm $a^*$ (some optimal algorithm) that minimizes $Q$.

    4. Why can empirical risk minimization lead to overfitting?

      The algorithm fits the noise in the training data, achieving minimal error on train, but loses its ability to generalize to new data. The remedies are regularization, cross-validation, early stopping, and other methods we will cover later (including in Deep Learning).

    5. How does a loss function differ from a metric?

      The loss function is minimized during training, so it must be smooth and differentiable. The metric evaluates the result and must be understandable to people and the business. They often differ: you train on log loss and look at $F_1$ or ROC-AUC.

    6. Why can't a model be trained directly on accuracy or the 0-1 loss?

      They are piecewise constant: a small change in the weights almost never changes the predicted label, so the loss value does not change and the gradient is zero almost everywhere. There is nothing to optimize. Instead, smooth surrogates are used — log loss, hinge loss, exponential loss — which are upper bounds on the error rate.

    7. How does empirical risk differ from true risk?

      Empirical risk is the average error on a finite training set; true risk is the expected error over the entire data distribution, including future objects. By minimizing the former we only approximate the latter; the gap between them grows with the richness of the model class and the amount of noise in the data.

  7. Regression↗

    Let $x_i$ be the input data, $y_i$ the target values, and $w$ (weights) and $b$ (bias) the model parameters. The linear regression model is:

    $$\hat{y}_i = w x_i + b,$$

    Let the model's prediction be $\hat{y}_i$.

    To train a linear regression model, we can use gradient descent on the MSE loss function:

    $$L(\hat{y}_i, y_i) = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2,$$

    where $N$ is the number of objects in the sample.

    Let's put the formula into words: the model multiplies the feature by a weight and adds a bias — this is the equation of a line, where $w$ controls the slope and $b$ the height. Training comes down to finding the pair $(w, b)$ for which the line passes as close as possible to all the points at once, with "closeness" measured by the mean squared vertical deviation.

    A natural question: why not just add up the deviations $y_i - \hat{y}_i$?

    • The signs would cancel out. A miss of $+10$ and a miss of $-10$ would sum to zero, and a useless model would look perfect. Squaring removes the sign.
    • The absolute value also removes the sign, but it isn't smooth. $|x|$ has no derivative at zero, which gets in the way of gradient methods. The square is differentiable everywhere.
    • There's an analytical solution. It is precisely the quadratic form that makes the optimal weights computable by the formula $w = (X^\top X)^{-1} X^\top y$ — without any iterative optimization.
    • Large misses are penalized more heavily. An error of 10 units contributes 100, while two errors of 5 contribute only 50. The model will prefer many small errors to one big one.

    The last property is a double-edged sword: MSE is very sensitive to outliers. A single anomalous object can drag the whole line. When this is a problem, people use MAE (robust, but not smooth at zero) or Huber loss (quadratic near zero and linear in the tails).

    Here we use MSE, but other loss functions can be used as well.

    Consider an example of solving a regression problem. Our data will be linear with a little noise:

    On the right of the GIF above is a plot that is a contour map of the loss function.

    Consider an example of solving a regression problem with cubic regression. We take the same data, but now instead of linear regression we use cubic regression:

    $$\hat{y}_i = w_1 x_i^3 + w_2 x_i^2 + w_3 x_i + b$$

    A common misconception: once you add $x^2$ and $x^3$, the model is no longer linear. In fact, it still is.

    Linearity here refers to the parameters, not the features. The model $\hat{y} = w_1 x^3 + w_2 x^2 + w_3 x + b$ is linear in $w_1, w_2, w_3, b$ — each weight appears to the first power. From the algorithm's point of view, this is ordinary linear regression fed three features: $x$, $x^2$, and $x^3$.

    The practical consequence: the same trick works with any transformations — logarithms, feature products ($x_1 \cdot x_2$), sines. A linear model captures nonlinear dependencies perfectly well if the right features are constructed for it. That's exactly what feature engineering was all about for many years.

    The polynomial degree is a direct, and very sharp, knob for model complexity:

    • Degree 1 — a straight line; may underfit curved data.
    • Degree 3–4 — usually enough for smooth dependencies.
    • Degree 15+ — the curve passes through literally every point of the training set and oscillates wildly between them. The train error is almost zero; on new data it's a disaster.

    An additional problem with high degrees is features on very different scales: if $x \approx 100$, then $x^3 \approx 10^6$. Without scaling, the optimization becomes ill-conditioned, and the weights on the higher powers become huge. That's why polynomial features almost always go together with standardization and regularization.

    1. Write down the linear regression formula and name its parameters.

      $\hat{y}_i = w x_i + b$, where $w$ is the weight (slope) and $b$ is the bias (intercept). In the multivariate case: $\hat{y}_i = \mathbf{w}^\top \mathbf{x}_i + b$.

    2. What is MSE and why is it used in regression?

      $\text{MSE} = \frac{1}{N}\sum(y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2$ is the mean squared error. It's differentiable and penalizes large errors more heavily (the square). This makes it possible to use gradient descent for optimization.

    3. What is a contour map of the loss function and what does it show?

      A projection of the loss surface onto the parameter plane. Each line is a set of parameters with the same loss value. The minimum is the point gradient descent "converges" to.

    4. How does polynomial regression differ from linear regression?

      It adds powers of the features ($x^2$, $x^3$...) as new features while remaining linear in the parameters ($w_1, w_2, ...$). It approximates nonlinear dependencies but increases the risk of overfitting at high degrees.

    5. Why are errors squared in MSE rather than taking the absolute value or the plain sum?

      A plain sum of deviations would cancel out due to sign compensation. The absolute value removes the sign but isn't differentiable at zero, which hampers gradient methods. The square is smooth everywhere, gives the analytical solution $w=(X^\top X)^{-1}X^\top y$, and penalizes large misses more heavily. The downside is sensitivity to outliers.

    6. Why is polynomial regression considered a linear model?

      Linearity refers to the parameters, not the features. In $\hat{y}=w_1x^3+w_2x^2+w_3x+b$ each weight appears to the first power, so from the algorithm's point of view it's ordinary linear regression fed three features: $x$, $x^2$, $x^3$.

    7. What are the dangers of high-degree polynomials?

      The curve passes through all training points and oscillates strongly between them — classic overfitting. In addition, the features end up on very different scales ($x \approx 100$ gives $x^3 \approx 10^6$), the optimization is ill-conditioned, and the weights on the higher powers are huge. Standardization and regularization are needed.

  8. Classification Loss Functions

    Commonly used in binary and multiclass classification. This function measures the discrepancy between the actual and predicted class probabilities:

    $$L = -\sum_{i=1}^n \textcolor{red}{y_i \log(p_i)}$$

    where $y_i$ is the true value, $p_i$ is the predicted probability of class $i$, and $n$ is the number of classes.

    In the simplest terms: the penalty equals minus the log of the probability the model assigned to the correct answer. The labels $y_i$ form a one-hot vector with a single one, so the whole sum reduces to a single term: $-\log(p_{\text{correct class}})$. The other classes are multiplied by zero and don't contribute.

    The model predicts the probability of the correct class, and we look at the penalty:

    $p$ (probability of the correct class)$-\log p$
    0.990.01
    0.90.11
    0.50.69
    0.12.30
    0.014.61
    0.0016.91

    The logarithm creates an asymmetric incentive: the reward for a confident correct answer is nearly zero (0.99 vs. 0.9 — a difference of just 0.1), but the penalty for a confident wrong answer grows without bound. It doesn't pay for the model to shout "I'm 99.9% sure" when it's wrong: that mistake costs about 7 units of loss versus 0.69 for an honest "I don't know, 50/50."

    This is exactly why cross-entropy teaches the model calibrated probabilities rather than just guessing the label: being confidently wrong is mathematically expensive.

    Like cross-entropy, it evaluates predicted class probabilities and is often used in binary classification. It is the special case of cross-entropy where the number of classes is 2:

    $$L = -\sum_{i=1}^2 \textcolor{red}{y_i \log(p_i)} = -[y \log(p) + (1 - y) \log(1 - p)]$$

    where $y$ is the true value, $p$ is the predicted probability of class $1$, and $1-p$ is the predicted probability of class $0$.

    Designed to improve training on problems with severe class imbalance by down-weighting the loss for well-classified examples:

    $$L = -\sum_{i=1}^n \alpha_i \underbrace{(1 - p_i)^{\gamma}}_{\text{amplified penalty}} \textcolor{red}{y_i \log(p_i)}$$

    where $\alpha_i$ is the weight of the $i$-th class, $\gamma$ is the focusing parameter for hard examples, and $n$ is the number of classes.

    In the simplest terms: the factor $(1 - p_i)^{\gamma}$ dampens the contribution of examples the model already solves confidently. The closer $p_i$ is to one, the closer the factor is to zero — an easy example barely affects the gradient anymore, and the model focuses on the hard ones.

    Take $\gamma = 2$ and look at the factor $(1-p)^2$:

    • $p = 0.9$ (easy example) $\to$ $(0.1)^2 = 0.01$, its contribution to the loss drops 100×;
    • $p = 0.6$ (medium) $\to$ $(0.4)^2 = 0.16$, its contribution drops about 6×;
    • $p = 0.1$ (hard) $\to$ $(0.9)^2 = 0.81$, its contribution barely changes.

    Why this matters is clear in object detection: a single image may contain a few dozen objects and hundreds of thousands of background regions. Each background region on its own is easy ($p \approx 0.99$) and yields a tiny loss, but there are so many of them that together they swamp the gradient, and the model simply learns to answer "background" everywhere. Focal Loss suppresses this avalanche.

    They are often confused, even though they fight different problems:

    • $\alpha$ is the class weight. It looks only at which class the object belongs to. It balances the contribution of rare and frequent classes.
    • $\gamma$ is the focus on hard examples. It looks only at how confidently the model answers, regardless of the class. A hard object from a frequent class keeps its weight too.

    They complement each other and are used together in the original paper: $\gamma = 2$, $\alpha = 0.25$.

    This is confusing: $\alpha$ is supposed to raise the weight of the rare class, yet in RetinaNet the rare class (objects) got $\alpha = 0.25$ and the background got $0.75$. So the rare class weighs less. This is not a mistake.

    Without the focusing factor ($\gamma = 0$), everything is as expected: the authors got the best result with $\alpha = 0.75$, i.e., weighted toward the rare class. But at $\gamma = 2$, the easy background examples are already suppressed by the factor $(1 - p)^{\gamma}$ by thousands of times, so there's no longer any need to boost objects further via $\alpha$ — the optimum shifts down. The paper states it directly: the larger $\gamma$, the smaller the optimal $\alpha$.

    Practical takeaway: tune $\alpha$ and $\gamma$ together. Copying $\alpha = 0.25$ into your own problem without re-tuning it for your $\gamma$ and your class ratio is a bad idea.

    Suppose the dataset has 4 classes and is imbalanced:

    ClassCount
    Class 1100
    Class 2700
    Class 33000
    Class 440

    Total objects in the dataset: $s = 100 + 700 + 3000 + 40 = 3840$. Class proportions:

    $$p_1 = \frac{100}{s} \approx 2.60\%; \quad p_2 = \frac{700}{s} \approx 18.23\%; \quad p_3 = \frac{3000}{s} \approx 78.13\%; \quad p_4 = \frac{40}{s} \approx 1.04\%$$

    The simplest way to set weights is to take inverse proportions, $\alpha_k = 1/p_k$. It's more convenient to compute directly from counts, because $1/p_k = 1 \big/ \frac{n_k}{s} = \frac{s}{n_k}$ (don't plug percentages into the formula — you'd get a number 100 times smaller):

    $$\alpha_1 = \frac{3840}{100} = 38.4; \quad \alpha_2 = \frac{3840}{700} \approx 5.49; \quad \alpha_3 = \frac{3840}{3000} = 1.28; \quad \alpha_4 = \frac{3840}{40} = 96$$

    The fewer objects a class has, the larger its weight: the rarest class 4 weighs 75 times more than the most frequent class 3 — exactly as many times as it is rarer ($3000 / 40 = 75$).

    The numbers 38.4 and 96 look scarily large, but they carry only one piece of information — how many times more important one class is than another. Multiplying all $\alpha_k$ by a common constant doesn't change these ratios.

    It's tempting to conclude "so the overall scale doesn't affect anything," but that's wrong — what actually happens depends on how training works:

    • torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss(weight=...) with reduction='mean' divides the weighted sum by the sum of weights, so the common factor cancels out and the loss doesn't change at all.
    • Plain SGD without regularization: the loss and all gradients are multiplied by the constant — exactly the same as scaling the learning rate by that factor.
    • Adam normalizes the step by the gradient scale and is therefore almost insensitive to a common factor.
    • With L2 regularization (weight decay in neural networks, the C parameter in scikit-learn), the scale really matters: the penalty on model weight magnitude doesn't scale along with the loss, so the fit-vs-regularization balance changes and you train a different model, not the same one with a different step size.

    Practical takeaway: choose the proportions deliberately, and pin the overall scale with normalization rather than leaving it arbitrary.

    The most common normalization makes the average per-object weight equal to one:

    $$\alpha_k = \frac{s}{K \cdot n_k}$$

    where $K$ is the number of classes, $n_k$ is the number of objects in class $k$, and $s$ is the dataset size. This is exactly what class_weight='balanced' does in scikit-learn. For our dataset we get $9.6$, $\approx 1.37$, $0.32$, $24$ — more modest numbers, but exactly the same proportions ($24 / 0.32 = 75$).

    The Focal Loss paper (Lin et al., 2017) does say $\alpha \in [0, 1]$, and people often conclude from this that a class weight greater than one is a mistake. It isn't.

    The paper analyzes the binary case, where the weight is a single number: $\alpha$ for the positive class and $1 - \alpha$ for the negative one. One degree of freedom — hence the interval $[0, 1]$. That's a parameterization, not a property of $\alpha$: the paper itself says right there that $\alpha$ can be set to the inverse class frequency.

    In the multiclass case, $\alpha_k$ is an ordinary multiplier on a loss term, and values above one are perfectly normal: class_weight='balanced' in scikit-learn routinely produces them, and torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss accepts any weight tensor via the weight argument. $\alpha$ is neither a probability nor a proportion.

    1. How are cross-entropy and log-loss related?

      Log-loss is the special case of cross-entropy for two classes: $-[y\log p + (1-y)\log(1-p)]$. Cross-entropy generalizes it to $K$ classes: $-\sum_{i=1}^K y_i \log p_i$. For $K=2$ the formulas coincide.

    2. What is Focal Loss and what problems is it for?

      Focal Loss adds the factor $(1-p_i)^\gamma$ to cross-entropy, which reduces the loss for "easy" (well-classified) examples. The model focuses on hard examples. It is used under severe class imbalance (e.g., object detection).

    3. How do the weights $\alpha_i$ in Focal Loss help with imbalance?

      $\alpha_i$ is a weight inversely proportional to the class share: rare classes get a larger weight in the loss function. This compensates for the fact that the model sees the rare class only a few times and would otherwise "ignore" it.

    4. Why does cross-entropy penalize confident wrong predictions more heavily?

      As $p_i \to 0$ for the true class, $-\log(p_i) \to \infty$. If the model is confident in a wrong answer, the penalty is huge. As $p_i \to 1$, the penalty tends to 0. This creates the right incentive for the model.

    5. Why does the sum over all classes in the cross-entropy formula reduce to a single term?

      The true labels $y_i$ form a one-hot vector: a one at the correct class and zeros elsewhere. All terms but one are multiplied by zero, leaving $-\log(p_{\text{correct class}})$.

    6. Why does cross-entropy teach the model calibrated probabilities?

      The penalty $-\log p$ grows without bound for a confident wrong answer (at $p=0.001$ it's 6.9) and barely decreases as confidence in the correct answer increases (0.9 $\to$ 0.99 saves only 0.1). Overstating confidence is mathematically unprofitable, so the model is better off outputting probabilities that match real frequencies.

    7. How does the parameter $\alpha$ in Focal Loss differ from $\gamma$?

      $\alpha$ is the class weight; it depends only on whether the object belongs to a rare or a frequent class. $\gamma$ is the degree of focusing; it depends only on how confidently the model answers on a specific object: the factor $(1-p)^\gamma$ suppresses easy examples regardless of their class. At $\gamma=2$, an example with $p=0.9$ loses 100× of its contribution. They must be tuned together: in the original paper, at $\gamma=0$ the optimum is $\alpha=0.75$ (weighted toward the rare class, as expected), but at $\gamma=2$ it's $\alpha=0.25$, because the easy examples of the frequent class are already suppressed by the factor, and there's no need to boost the rare class further.

    8. Can a class weight $\alpha$ be greater than one?

      Yes. The interval $[0, 1]$ in the original paper is a parameterization of the binary case ($\alpha$ for one class, $1-\alpha$ for the other), not a constraint. $\alpha_k$ is an ordinary multiplier on a loss term; class_weight='balanced' in scikit-learn routinely produces values above one, and torch.nn.CrossEntropyLoss accepts any tensor via weight. The proportions between classes carry the meaning, but you can't say the overall scale "doesn't affect anything": with L2 regularization (weight decay, the C parameter) it changes the balance between fit and penalty, i.e., a different model gets trained. That's why the scale is pinned by the normalization $\alpha_k = s / (K n_k)$.

  9. Regression loss functions

    The three formulas below differ in one thing — how heavily a large error is penalized. And the answer to that question is determined not by math but by your task.

    Ask yourself: is one error of 10 units worse than ten errors of 1 unit each?

    • Yes, worse (one badly misled customer is more dangerous than ten slightly dissatisfied ones) — use MSE/RMSE. Squaring makes a large miss disproportionately expensive: $10^2 = 100$ versus $10 \times 1^2 = 10$.
    • No, the same (what matters is the total magnitude of deviation, say, in rubles) — use MAE. For it, both scenarios cost exactly 10.

    This also explains the different behavior of models: MSE pulls the prediction toward the mean, and MAE toward the median. This is not a metaphor but an exact fact: the constant that minimizes MSE is the sample mean, and the one that minimizes MAE is the median.

    MSE

    The most common loss function for regression tasks. It measures the mean squared difference between predicted and actual values:

    $$L = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2$$

    where $y_i$ is the true value, $\hat{y}_i$ is the predicted value, and $N$ is the number of objects in the sample.

    MSE is more sensitive to outliers than MAE. A linear regression trained with MSE on data with outliers will fit them heavily, which makes it perform poorly on new data.

    Nine apartments cost about 5M, the tenth costs 50M (a penthouse that ended up in the sample by mistake).

    • A model trained on MSE will be forced to pull its predictions up: ignoring an object that produces an error of $45^2 = 2025$ is too expensive for it. As a result, all nine ordinary apartments will get an inflated estimate.
    • A model on MAE will notice that this object contributes only 45 — the same as 45 ordinary misses of one unit each. It is better off predicting the nine typical apartments well and accepting one big miss.

    The practical consequence: if the data contains genuine anomalies that should not determine the model's behavior, MSE will pull the solution toward them. But if the large values are not garbage but an important part of the task (e.g., you must predict expensive deals well), the sensitivity of MSE becomes an advantage.

    MAE

    It measures the mean absolute deviation between predicted and actual values:

    $$L = \frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N |y_i - \hat{y}_i|$$

    where $y_i$ is the true value, $\hat{y}_i$ is the predicted value, and $N$ is the number of objects in the sample.

    MAE is less sensitive to outliers than MSE. A linear regression trained with MAE on data with outliers will fit them less.

    RMSE

    It measures the mean squared difference between predicted and actual values, returning the result in the same units as the target:

    $$L = \sqrt{\frac{1}{N} \sum_{i=1}^N (y_i - \hat{y}_i)^2}$$

    where $y_i$ is the true value, $\hat{y}_i$ is the predicted value, and $N$ is the number of objects in the sample.

    RMSE is useful when you need to keep the units of the predicted values — MSE squares them, which makes the result harder to interpret.

    Often you want both the robustness of MAE and the smoothness of MSE. That is what Huber loss is for: it behaves quadratically near zero and linearly for large deviations.

    $$L_\delta(e) = \begin{cases} \frac{1}{2}e^2, & |e| \le \delta \\ \delta(|e| - \frac{1}{2}\delta), & |e| > \delta \end{cases}$$

    The threshold $\delta$ is the boundary between an "ordinary error" and an "outlier". This function is differentiable everywhere, including at zero (unlike MAE), and at the same time does not let individual anomalies drag the model away. A related variant is pinball loss for quantile regression, used when underestimation and overestimation have different costs.

    • RMSE and MSE rank models identically. The square root is a monotonic transformation, so a model with lower MSE always has lower RMSE too. The only difference is the interpretability of the units.
    • RMSE is always at least MAE, and the gap between them is an indicator: the more RMSE exceeds MAE, the heavier the tail of the error distribution and the more large misses there are in the data.
    • The metrics depend on the scale of the target. An RMSE of 5,000 rubles says nothing without context. For comparisons across tasks you need relative metrics (MAPE) or $R^2$ / $D^2$.

    All of these functions can also be used as metrics for evaluating model quality.

    1. What is the fundamental difference between MSE and MAE as loss functions?

      $MSE$ penalizes in proportion to the squared error — large errors (outliers) are penalized disproportionately heavily. $MAE$ penalizes linearly and is more robust to outliers. A linear regression on $MSE$ with outliers will be "pulled" toward them.

    2. Why do we need $RMSE$ if we already have $MSE$?

      $RMSE = \sqrt{MSE}$ returns the error in the original units of the target (not squared). This makes interpretation intuitive: "the model's average error is $X$ rubles" (rather than "$X^2$ $\text{rubles}^2$").

    3. Which loss function should be used when the target contains outliers?

      $MAE$ — it is robust to outliers, since it penalizes each unit of error equally regardless of the error's size. $MSE$ "blows up" because of large errors on outliers and generalizes worse.

    4. Can a loss function be used as a quality metric?

      Yes — $MSE$, $MAE$, and $RMSE$ are used both as loss functions during training and as metrics for comparing models on test data. This is convenient: the minimized function matches the measured quality.

    5. Why does MSE pull the prediction toward the mean and MAE toward the median?

      This is an exact mathematical fact: the constant that minimizes the sum of squared deviations is the sample mean, and the one that minimizes the sum of absolute deviations is the median. Hence the different behavior of models on skewed data with outliers: an MSE model shifts toward rare large values, an MAE model does not.

    6. What is Huber loss and why is it needed?

      A loss function that is quadratic for small errors and linear for large ones: $\frac{1}{2}e^2$ for $|e| \le \delta$ and $\delta(|e| - \frac{1}{2}\delta)$ otherwise. It combines the smoothness of MSE near zero (convenient for gradient methods) with the robustness of MAE to outliers. The threshold $\delta$ sets the boundary between an ordinary error and an anomaly.

    7. What does a large gap between RMSE and MAE mean?

      RMSE is always at least MAE, and the more it exceeds MAE, the heavier the tail of the error distribution: the data contains individual large misses that squaring amplifies. Close values mean the errors are distributed evenly.

  10. Classification metrics↗

    A metric is the definition of the word "good" for your task. Until it is chosen, the phrase "the model works well" is meaningless.

    And this choice is not technical but substantive: the same model can be excellent by accuracy and useless by recall. That is why the metric is chosen before training, based on what happens in reality with each type of error — not picked afterwards as whichever one makes the number look nicer.

    Definition

    The machine learning algorithms we've already met let us build a model that approximates this dependency. But how do we know how good the approximation is?

    Our model will almost certainly make mistakes on some objects: even if it were perfect, noise or outliers in the test data would spoil things. Meanwhile, different models will make mistakes on different objects and to different degrees. The machine learning specialist's job is to choose a suitable criterion that makes it possible to compare different models.

    Important: model quality must not be evaluated on the training set. At a minimum, it should be done on a held-out (test) set.

    In each specific case, a whole hierarchy of metrics can arise. Imagine we are talking about a music streaming service whose users we decided to delight with neural-network-generated tracks.

    LevelMetricComment
    1 (top)Future revenue of the serviceNot directly measurable, depends on many factors
    2Median session lengthMeasured online in production, an approximate indicator of user satisfaction
    3Share of satisfied assessorsOffline evaluation before launching the model, people's reaction to the quality of the music
    4 (bottom)Loss functionUsed when training the model, optimized directly

    The table above shows that the optimized loss function sits on the bottom level of the hierarchy, while the business goal is at the top. There is no direct equality between them, and this is fundamental:

    • The loss function must be convenient to optimize — smooth and differentiable. That's why we train on log loss rather than $F_1$: the gradient of $F_1$ is zero almost everywhere.
    • The metric must be understandable to humans and reflect the meaning of the task, but optimizing it directly is often impossible.

    Hence the typical combination in a real project: train on log loss, select models by PR-AUC, tune the threshold for recall at a given number of reviews, and make the launch decision based on money in an A/B test. All four quantities are different, and each has its place.

    The main trap of the hierarchy: the connection between levels is not guaranteed. Improving log loss by 3% doesn't mean session length will grow, let alone revenue.

    A classic example: a recommender model optimized for click-prediction accuracy starts promoting clickbait. The offline metric goes up, and users leave.

    The practical takeaway: the higher a metric is in the hierarchy, the more truthful it is and the more expensive it is to measure. Offline metrics are needed to quickly weed out obviously bad options, but the final decision is made based on an online experiment.

    Metrics can be offline or online. Online metrics are computed from data collected from a running system (e.g., median session length). Offline metrics can be measured before the model is put into production, for example on historical data or with the help of specially hired people, assessors.

    The latter is often used when the metric is a live human's reaction: for example, this is what search companies do when they ask people to rate the ranking quality of an experimental system before regular users see those results in the normal way. At the very bottom level of the hierarchy lie the loss functions optimized during training.

    Links

    LinkTimeDescription
    Classification and regression metrics (Rus)20-30 minBinary classification metrics (worked examples, pros and cons depending on the task): accuracy, confusion matrix ($TP$/$FP$/$TN$/$FN$), precision, recall, $F_1$, ROC/AUC, average precision (PR-AUC). Multiclass: micro- vs macro-averaging and the effect of imbalance. Separately — optimization via LogLoss + threshold tuning

    1. Why do you need a hierarchy of metrics in an ML project?

      Business metrics (revenue, retention) can't be optimized directly. They are linked by a chain: business $\to$ online metric $\to$ offline metric $\to$ loss function. An ML specialist optimizes the loss function while tracking its effect on the upper levels.

    2. How does an offline metric differ from an online metric?

      An offline metric is measured before launching to real users — on historical data or with assessors. An online metric is collected from a running system in production (A/B test, live users).

    3. Why can't model quality be evaluated on the training set?

      On train, the model can achieve zero error through overfitting (memorization). Real quality is the ability to predict on new, unseen data, which is measured only on the test set.

    4. What is the loss function in the context of the metrics hierarchy?

      The bottom level of the hierarchy — a mathematical quantity optimized by the learning algorithm. It should correlate with the offline metric, which in turn should predict online behavior.

    5. How does a metric differ from a loss function, and why are they separated?

      The loss function is minimized during training and must be smooth and differentiable, which is why we train on log loss. The metric evaluates the result and should reflect the meaning of the task, but it often can't be optimized directly: the gradient of accuracy and $F_1$ is zero almost everywhere. A typical combination is training on log loss, selecting models by PR-AUC, and setting the threshold for a business constraint.

    6. Why doesn't improving an offline metric guarantee better business results?

      The connection between levels of the hierarchy is not guaranteed. A classic example is a recommender model optimized for click probability that starts promoting clickbait: the offline metric grows while users leave. That's why the final decision is made based on an online experiment, and offline metrics are used to quickly weed out bad options.

    7. When should the metric be chosen, and why then?

      Before training, based on the real-world cost of each type of error. The metric is the definition of the word "good" for the task, and it determines the choice of loss function, validation scheme, and threshold. Choosing the metric after experiments means picking whichever one makes the number look better.

  11. Binary classification metrics: definitions

    The names seem confusing, but they follow a simple two-word rule:

    • the second word is what the model predicted (positive means "class 1", negative means "class 0");
    • the first word is whether it was right (true means yes, false means no).

    So a false negative means "the model said negative, and that was wrong", i.e., the object is actually positive and the model missed it. Read the name right to left and you will never confuse FP and FN.

    Note that for each object in the sample there are 4 possible situations:

    • We predicted the positive label and were right. We will put such objects in the true positive (TP) group. True because our prediction was correct, and positive because we predicted the positive label;
    • We predicted the positive label, but our prediction was wrong — false positive (FP). False because the prediction was incorrect;
    • We predicted the negative label and were right — true negative (TN);
    • And finally, we predicted the negative label but were wrong — false negative (FN).

    For convenience, these 4 numbers are arranged in a table called the confusion matrix:

    A test for a rare disease. 1000 people were examined, 50 of whom are sick. The model flagged 60 people as sick, of whom 40 are actually sick.

    Filling in the cells: $TP = 40$ (sick people found), $FP = 60 - 40 = 20$ (healthy people flagged as sick), $FN = 50 - 40 = 10$ (sick people missed), $TN = 1000 - 40 - 20 - 10 = 930$.

    Computing the metrics:

    • Accuracy $= (40 + 930)/1000 = 97\%$ — looks great;
    • Precision $= 40/60 = 67\%$ — one in every three "alarms" is false;
    • Recall $= 40/50 = 80\%$ — one in five sick people is missed;
    • $F_1$ $= 2 \cdot \frac{0.67 \cdot 0.8}{0.67 + 0.8} = 73\%$.

    97% accuracy and 80% recall are scores of the same model. The first number says nothing about the fact that one in five sick people went undiagnosed: accuracy is made up almost entirely of the 930 healthy people the model correctly left alone.

    The model outputs not a label but a probability, and the label is obtained by comparing it with a threshold (0.5 by default). By moving the threshold, we move both metrics — in opposite directions:

    • lower threshold $\to$ more objects are declared positive $\to$ we find more sick people (recall goes up), but there are also more false alarms (precision goes down);
    • higher threshold $\to$ we raise an alarm only when highly confident (precision goes up), but we miss more sick people (recall goes down).

    The extreme cases show this most clearly: declare everyone sick and you get recall = 100% with tiny precision; declare only the one person you are most confident about sick and you get precision = 100% with recall = 2%.

    That is why the question "which metric is better" has no answer outside the context of the task — first you decide which error is more costly.

    • FN is more costly — a miss is dangerous: cancer screening (a missed tumor), fraud detection, defect detection in manufacturing. We optimize recall and accept false alarms.
    • FP is more costly — a false alarm is expensive: blocking an honest customer's account, a spam filter (an important email in spam is worse than an ad in the inbox), sending out an expensive offer. We optimize precision.
    • $F_\beta$ lets you set this balance with a number: $\beta > 1$ (e.g., $F_2$) increases the weight of recall, $\beta < 1$ ($F_{0.5}$) increases the weight of precision.
    MetricFormulaExplanation
    Accuracy$\frac{TP + TN}{TP + TN + FP + FN}$Overall fraction of correctly classified objects
    Precision$\frac{TP}{TP + FP}$Fraction of correct positive predictions among all predicted positives
    Recall$\frac{TP}{TP + FN}$Fraction of correct positive predictions among all objects of the positive class
    $F_\beta$-score$F_\beta = (1 + \beta^2) \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{(\beta^2 \cdot \text{Precision}) + \text{Recall}}$A balanced metric that accounts for the relative importance of precision and recall. With $\beta=1$ it is the F1-score
    $F_1$-score$F_1 = 2 \cdot \frac{\text{Precision} \cdot \text{Recall}}{\text{Precision} + \text{Recall}}$Harmonic mean of precision and recall
    Recall@k, Precision@kRecall and Precision work well for the search task "document $d$ is relevant to query $q$", when out of the list of documents recommended by the algorithm we only care about the first one
    • Looking at accuracy under class imbalance. With 1% positives, an "always 0" model gives 99% accuracy and zero usefulness. This is not an edge case but the norm in anti-fraud and medicine.
    • Treating the 0.5 threshold as sacred. It's just the default and is almost never optimal. The threshold is tuned on validation for the desired precision/recall balance.
    • Forgetting that the metrics depend on the threshold, while ROC-AUC does not. Precision, recall, and $F_1$ are computed for a specific threshold; ROC-AUC and PR-AUC evaluate ranking quality across all thresholds at once.
    • Chasing $F_1$ blindly. $F_1$ weighs precision and recall equally, which is plainly wrong for medicine or anti-fraud — there you need $F_2$ or direct optimization for the business cost of errors.

    Practice problem on the platform

    1. What is a confusion matrix and what 4 cells does it contain?

      $TP$ (class 1 correctly predicted), $TN$ (class 0 correctly predicted), $FP$ (class 1 incorrectly predicted), $FN$ (class 0 incorrectly predicted). The $2\times 2$ matrix shows all outcomes of binary classification.

    2. What do Precision and Recall measure, and how do they differ?

      $Precision = \dfrac{TP}{TP+FP}$ is the fraction of true positives among those predicted positive. $Recall = \dfrac{TP}{TP+FN}$ is the fraction of positives found among all actual positives. Precision answers "how accurate are we?", Recall answers "how many did we find?".

    3. What is the $F_1$-score and why is the harmonic mean used?

      $F1 = 2\dfrac{PR}{P + R}$ is the harmonic mean of Precision ($P$) and Recall ($R$). The harmonic mean "penalizes" imbalance: if one of the metrics is close to zero, $F_1$ will also be small. This makes $F_1$ a strict metric that requires both $P$ and $R$ to be good at the same time.

    4. When is Accuracy not a suitable quality metric?

      Under class imbalance: if 95% of objects are class 0, a model that always predicts 0 gets Accuracy=95% but is completely useless. In such cases $F_1$, ROC AUC, or Precision/Recall are used.

    5. How do you remember the difference between FP and FN?

      Read the name right to left: the second word is what the model predicted (positive/negative), the first is whether it was right. False negative means "predicted negative and was wrong", i.e., an actually positive object was missed.

    6. Why can't precision and recall be maximized simultaneously?

      Both metrics depend on the threshold but move in opposite directions: lowering the threshold finds more positive objects (recall goes up) but adds false positives (precision goes down). In the extreme cases, "declare everything positive" gives 100% recall with tiny precision, and vice versa.

    7. In which tasks is FN more costly than FP, and what do you do about it?

      When a miss is more dangerous than a false alarm: cancer screening, fraud detection, defect detection. There you optimize recall — lower the classification threshold and use $F_\beta$ with $\beta > 1$ (e.g., $F_2$), which weights recall above precision.

  12. Multiclass classification metrics↗

    If we have $K$ classes, we can compute separate metrics for each of them. There are 2 ways to compute metrics for a multiclass task:

    MethodDescription
    Micro-averagingTake each class, fix it, and "merge all the others into one class" — this gives us $K$ binary classifiers.
