    6. Why can't you just sort features by importance once and cut off the tail?

      A feature's importance depends on the composition of the other features. Two mutually redundant strong features split the importance in half and both look weak — with a one-time sort, you might throw out both. RFE recomputes importances after each removal, so such effects are taken into account.

    7. How do you choose the number of features from an RFECV plot?

      Take the point at the start of the plateau, not the formal maximum of the metric: the difference between, say, 10 and 25 features usually lies within the confidence interval, and a model with fewer features is simpler, faster, and more stable in production.

    8. Why is permutation importance more reliable for RFECV with trees?

      The built-in feature_importances_ systematically overstates the importance of features with many unique values — they have more possible split thresholds. Because of this, a noisy high-cardinality feature may survive elimination undeservedly.

  36. Bayesian optimization

    Bayesian optimization is a hyperparameter tuning method for expensive objective functions. In ML, that function is often a cross-validation result:

    $$ f(\lambda) = \text{CV score of the model with hyperparameters } \lambda. $$

    We want to find good values of $\lambda$ while training as few models as possible.

    In the simplest terms: the method learns from its own attempts. Grid search and random search are blind — they try points without looking at previous results. Bayesian optimization updates its picture of where things might be good after every run and chooses the next point deliberately.

    The key word in the definition is expensive. A single evaluation of $f(\lambda)$ here means fully training a model with 5-fold cross-validation: anywhere from minutes to hours.

    When one check costs an hour, the difference between 200 and 30 runs is the difference between a week and a day. So it makes sense to spend a few seconds "thinking" about which point to check next: the time spent choosing is negligible compared to the cost of the check itself.

    Conversely, if the model trains in a second, thinking longer than computing makes no sense — random search wins there, simply running a thousand points while the Bayesian method is still building its models.

    Instead of blind search, Bayesian optimization builds an approximation of the objective function:

    • surrogate model — a model that predicts the score for hyperparameters that have not been tried yet;
    • acquisition function — a rule that selects the next point to evaluate.

    The method balances exploring new regions against refining regions where good scores are already visible.

    The mechanics are easiest to grasp through an analogy: searching for the deepest spot in a lake, where each measurement is a diver's descent.

    • Surrogate model — your map of the lake bottom, built from the measurements made so far. It gives not only the expected depth at each point but also the uncertainty: near the measurements the map is reliable, far from them almost nothing is known. Usually this is a Gaussian process or TPE.
    • Acquisition function — the rule for choosing the next point. It combines two things: "where the map expects it to be deep" (exploitation) and "where the map knows nothing" (exploration). A popular choice is Expected Improvement: the expected improvement over the best result found so far.

    This balance is essential. A method that only refines the best region found will get stuck in a local optimum; a method that only explores new ground degenerates into random search. The acquisition function keeps these two forces in equilibrium.

    • Grid search checks a predefined grid and scales poorly with the number of hyperparameters.
    • Random search is often more efficient than a grid but does not use the history of past runs.
    • Bayesian optimization uses the results of past runs to choose the next candidates more intelligently.

    Commonly used in practice:

    • Optuna;
    • Hyperopt;
    • scikit-optimize;
    • Ray Tune.

    Under the hood they may use Gaussian Processes, TPE, Random Forests, and other surrogate models.

    Bayesian optimization is not magic:

    • Overfitting to validation. The method deliberately searches for the maximum of the CV metric, which means it also chases that metric's noise. Hundreds of runs find a configuration that got lucky on a particular split — and the gain vanishes on the test set.
    • Noisy evaluation breaks the surrogate. If the same $\lambda$ gives a different CV score on a rerun, the map is built from contradictory measurements. Fixing random_state and using more folds helps.
    • Sequential nature hurts parallelism. By design, the method picks points one at a time; random search parallelizes across a hundred machines trivially, while here you need special schemes.
    • The search space matters more than the optimizer. If you set learning_rate in the range $[0.5, 1.0]$, no algorithm will save you. Ranges for "multiplicative" parameters (learning rate, regularization) are specified on a log scale, not a uniform one.

    1. What is Bayesian optimization?

      It is a sequential hyperparameter tuning approach that builds a model of the objective function and chooses the next runs based on the results obtained so far.

    2. How does it differ from grid search and random search?

      Grid search and random search do not use the run history to choose the next point. Bayesian optimization does, through a surrogate model.

    3. What is a surrogate model?

      It is an auxiliary model that approximates the unknown objective function: based on hyperparameters already tried, it tries to predict the score of new ones.

    4. What does the acquisition function do?

      It selects the next point to evaluate, balancing exploration and exploitation: exploring unknown regions or checking a region where a high score is expected.

    5. When should you not use Bayesian optimization?

      When the model trains very quickly, there are few hyperparameters, the search space is poorly specified, or the score estimate is too noisy. In those cases random search can be simpler and more reliable.

    6. Why does Bayesian optimization pay off only for expensive functions?

      The method spends time building the surrogate model and choosing the next point. That is justified when a single evaluation of $f(\lambda)$ is a full training run with cross-validation lasting minutes or hours. If the model trains in a second, the "thinking" costs more than the checks themselves, and random search will be more efficient.

    7. What are exploration and exploitation in an acquisition function?

      Exploitation means checking where the surrogate predicts high quality; exploration means checking where the surrogate is uncertain, i.e., where there have been no measurements yet. Pure exploitation gets stuck in a local optimum, pure exploration degenerates into random search. Expected Improvement and similar functions maintain the balance.

    8. Why are learning rate ranges specified on a log scale?

      Such parameters act multiplicatively: the difference between 0.001 and 0.01 matters far more than between 0.31 and 0.32. With a uniform grid, almost all points land in the region of large values, and the small orders of magnitude remain untested.

  37. Classic ML model pipeline

    A classic ML model pipeline is a reproducible sequence of steps from raw data to the final model evaluation: problem formulation, splitting, analysis, preprocessing, training, hyperparameter tuning, evaluation, and interpretation.

    In the simplest terms: it's a recipe you can repeat and get the same result. Not "I tinkered with something in a notebook and got 0.87", but a sequence of steps that a colleague, an automatic rerun a month later, and production can all reproduce.

    Note: data splitting comes second, before EDA and preprocessing. This is no accident, and getting it wrong is the most common beginner mistake.

    Everything you do while looking at the full dataset is a decision made with knowledge of the test set:

    • computed the mean for imputing missing values over the whole table $\to$ test statistics leaked into training;
    • selected features by their correlation with the target on all the data $\to$ the selection "peeked" at the test set;
    • spotted an outlier during EDA and removed it from the whole sample $\to$ you cleaned the test set too, which you won't be able to do in production.

    The rule: cut off the test set first and forget about it, and only then look, clean, encode, and train. Technically this is ensured by a Pipeline — all preprocessing inside it is refit from scratch on each fold.

    1. Formulate the problem and the target metric.
    2. Split the data into train/validation/test or set up cross-validation.
    3. Do EDA.
    4. Assemble preprocessing into a Pipeline/ColumnTransformer.
    5. Train a simple baseline.
    6. Compare several models.
    7. Tune hyperparameters on validation/CV.
    8. Evaluate the final model on test.
    9. Analyze errors and important features.

    A baseline is not a formality but a reference point without which any number is meaningless. A minimal baseline takes five minutes:

    • classification — always predict the majority class (DummyClassifier);
    • regression — always predict the median or mean (DummyRegressor);
    • time series — predict yesterday's value.

    The point is that a ROC-AUC of 0.78 may be an excellent result on a hard task — and a failure where an out-of-the-box logistic regression gives 0.85. Until you have a baseline, you don't know which case is yours.

    A baseline also has diagnostic value: if a complex model barely beats a constant, the problem is almost always the data or the problem formulation, not insufficiently tuned hyperparameters.

    Accuracy is usually looked at only as a rough starting metric. For imbalanced classes, what matters more is: Precision, Recall, $F_1$, ROC AUC, PR AUC, the confusion matrix, and choosing the threshold for the business task.

    People usually start with MAE/RMSE/$R^2$, but for practical tasks it is useful to also look at quantiles of the absolute error: $p_{50}$, $p_{75}$, $p_{95}$, $p_{99}$. They show not only the average quality but also the tails of the error distribution.

    Step 9 is usually skipped, yet it gives more value than yet another hyperparameter search. Error analysis answers not "how many errors" but "which ones exactly":

    • By segment. The model is good on average but completely falls apart on new customers or in one region. This is often fixed with a dedicated feature or a separate model rather than tuning.
    • By the biggest misses. Look at the 20 objects with the largest error. It regularly turns out that they are labeling errors, duplicates, or objects that physically lack the needed feature.
    • By the confusion matrix. Which classes get confused with each other is a direct hint about which data or features are missing.
    • By feature importance. If an identifier or an upload date is at the top, you've found a leak.

    The test set is used once — after the model, preprocessing, and hyperparameters have been chosen. If you keep adjusting the solution after the test evaluation, you need a new independent test set or an honest external check.

    1. Why do you need a unified ML pipeline?

      So that all steps from preprocessing to the model are executed identically on train, validation, and test, and the experiment is reproducible.

    2. Why do you need a baseline first?

      A baseline shows the minimum reasonable level of quality. Without it, it's unclear whether a complex model gives a real improvement.

    3. Where are hyperparameters tuned?

      On validation or via cross-validation. The test set is not used for tuning.

    4. What does error analysis mean?

      It's examining the objects where the model makes the biggest mistakes: by class, segment, feature values, rare categories, or tails of the distribution.

    5. Why is it important to fix random_state and library versions?

      So that results can be reproduced. Otherwise a change in the split or in the algorithm version can change the metrics.

    6. Why is the data split done before EDA and preprocessing?

      Any decision made while looking at all the data at once takes the test set into account: the mean for imputing missing values, feature selection by correlation with the target, outlier removal. This is leakage that makes the estimate inflated. The correct order is to cut off the test set, forget about it, and only then look, clean, and train.

    7. What should you use as a baseline and why is it needed?

      For classification — predicting the majority class (DummyClassifier); for regression — a constant (median or mean); for time series — yesterday's value. Without a reference point it's unclear whether a result is good: a ROC-AUC of 0.78 can be excellent on a hard task and a failure where logistic regression gives 0.85.

    8. What should you look for during error analysis?

      Segments where the model systematically fails; objects with the largest error (often labeling errors or duplicates); pairs of classes the model confuses; suspicious features at the top of the importance ranking — identifiers and dates there usually mean leakage.

    38. Block 3

  38. Logistic regression

    Judging by the name, you might think this is yet another regression algorithm, but it is not. Logistic regression is used for classification tasks.

    Let $x_i$ be the input data, $y_i$ the target values, and $w$ (weights) and $b$ (bias) the model parameters. Recall what the linear regression model looks like:

    $$\hat{y}_i = w x_i + b,$$

    Then the logistic regression model looks like this:

    $$\hat{y}_i = \sigma(w x_i + b),$$

    where $\sigma$ is the sigmoid.

    Whereas linear or polynomial regression predicts a real number $\mathbb{R}$, logistic regression predicts the probability that an object belongs to class $1$ (in the binary case). We use the sigmoid to transform a real number into a pseudo-probability lying in the range $[0, 1]$.

    Since the outputs of logistic regression are probabilities, we can again tune the classification threshold to get the best classification quality metrics (see optimizing classification metrics).

    The sigmoid is usually explained like this: we needed to squash a number into $[0,1]$, so we took a suitable function. That's true but incomplete — it has a precise origin.

    Introduce the odds — the ratio of the probability of an event to the probability of its absence: $\frac{p}{1-p}$. If $p = 0.8$, the odds are 4, i.e., "four to one". Odds lie in the range $[0, +\infty)$, and their logarithm — the logit — lies in $(-\infty, +\infty)$, exactly like the output of a linear model.

    Logistic regression postulates that a linear combination of features predicts precisely the logit:

    $$\log \frac{p}{1-p} = wx + b$$

    Solve this for $p$, and you get the sigmoid — it isn't chosen arbitrarily but derived from this equation.

    This also gives a meaningful interpretation of the coefficients: the weight $w_j$ shows how much the log-odds change when the feature increases by one, and $e^{w_j}$ shows by what factor the odds themselves change. A coefficient of 0.7 on the feature "has late payments" means that late payments roughly double the odds of default ($e^{0.7} \approx 2$). It's precisely for this interpretability that logistic regression is loved in medicine and credit scoring.

    The sigmoid is a function that maps any real number $\mathbb{R}$ to a value between $0$ and $1$:

    $$\sigma(x) = \frac{1}{1 + e^{-x}},$$

    The loss function for logistic regression is usually log-loss (= binary cross-entropy loss):

    $$L(y, \hat{y}) = -y \log(\hat{y}) - (1 - y) \log(1 - \hat{y}),$$

    where $y$ is the target value and $\hat{y}$ is the model's prediction.

    A natural question: since the model outputs a number, why not train it on the familiar squared error? There are two reasons, and both are serious.

    • MSE combined with the sigmoid gives a non-convex loss function. It has local minima, and gradient descent can get stuck. Log-loss with the sigmoid, on the other hand, is convex — it has a single minimum that is guaranteed to be found.
    • MSE gives almost no gradient on a confident error. The derivative of the sigmoid $\sigma'(z) = \sigma(z)(1-\sigma(z))$ vanishes at the extremes. If the model confidently answered 0.999 while the correct answer is 0, it made a gross mistake, but with MSE the gradient will be negligible and training will practically stall. With log-loss these factors cancel out, and the gradient is proportional to the error $(\hat{y} - y)$: the grosser the miss, the stronger the correction.

    The second point is the same argument for which cross-entropy is preferred in neural networks.

    Multiclass logistic regression

    For multiclass logistic regression, instead of the sigmoid we use the softmax function, which transforms the model's outputs into "probabilities" over all classes so that they sum to 1:

    $$\text{softmax}(z_i) = \frac{e^{z_i}}{\sum_{j=1}^K e^{z_j}}$$

    where $K$ is the number of classes.

    Suppose we have 4-class classification, and the model produced the following outputs:

    $$z = [0.3750,\ -1.5378,\ -0.6234,\ -0.9105]$$

    Pass them through softmax:

    $$\text{softmax}(z) = [\textcolor{red}{0.5578},\ 0.0824,\ 0.2055,\ 0.1543]$$

    The probabilities sum to 1. The first class has the highest probability, so as the model's final prediction for this object we choose class 1.

    Multiclass logistic regression uses the cross-entropy loss.

    Let $y$ be the true class (a one-hot vector) and $\hat{p}$ the predicted probabilities (the softmax output). Then the loss for a single object is:

    $$L(y, \hat{p}) = -\sum_{k=1}^K y_k \log \hat{p}_k$$

    where $K$ is the number of classes, $y_k = 1$ only for the correct class, and $\hat{p}_k$ is the probability predicted by the model for class $k$.

    Written for a single object with true class $c$:

    $$L = -\log \hat{p}_c$$

    Thus the model is penalized if the probability of the correct class is small.

    A one-hot vector is a vector of length $K$ (where $K$ is the number of classes) in which all elements are 0 except one, which equals 1 and indicates the correct class. For example, if we have 4 classes and the correct class is the second, the one-hot vector is:

    $$\begin{bmatrix} 0 \\ 1 \\ 0 \\ 0 \end{bmatrix}$$

    Such a vector is convenient for computing the cross-entropy loss, since only the probability of the correct class enters the loss function.

    • The model is linear in its decision boundary. $wx + b = 0$ defines a line (hyperplane), so logistic regression can't separate data where the boundary is curved. This is fixed by adding nonlinear features — products, polynomials, transformations.
    • Scaling is needed. By itself it doesn't affect the predictions, but with regularization (which is on by default in sklearn!) the penalty depends on the scale of the weights, and features in different units are penalized incomparably.
    • C in sklearn is $1/\lambda$. Smaller C means stronger regularization. The direction is counterintuitive, and this is a common source of errors.
    • Perfect class separation breaks training. If the classes are perfectly separable, the optimal weights go to infinity (the model keeps increasing its confidence indefinitely). Regularization prevents this — yet another reason not to turn it off.
    • Probabilities are well calibrated. This is a rare virtue: by construction, logistic regression gives probabilities close to the real frequencies, unlike boosting and naive Bayes.

    1. How does logistic regression differ from linear regression?

      Logistic regression is a classification algorithm, not a regression one. It applies the sigmoid to a linear prediction: $\hat{y} = \sigma(wx + b)$, transforming a real number into the probability of class membership in $[0, 1]$.

    2. What is softmax used for in multiclass classification?

      Softmax transforms a vector of logits (the model's outputs) into a vector of probabilities that sum to 1: $\text{softmax}(z_i) = e^{z_i} / \sum_j e^{z_j}$. The class with the highest probability is the model's prediction.

    3. Which loss function is used for logistic regression and why?

      Log-loss (binary cross-entropy): $L = -y\log\hat{y} - (1-y)\log(1-\hat{y})$. It penalizes the model in proportion to its confidence in a wrong answer. For multiclass classification — cross-entropy: $L = -\log\hat{p}_c$ (the log of the probability of the correct class).

    4. What is one-hot encoding and why is it used in classification?

      A one-hot vector: the class label is represented by a vector of zeros with a single one at the position of the correct class. It's used to compute the cross-entropy loss — it lets you compare the predicted probabilities with the "ideal" distribution (one 1, the rest 0).

    5. Where does the sigmoid come from, and how do you interpret the model's coefficients?

      The model postulates that a linear combination of features predicts the logit — the log-odds $\log\frac{p}{1-p}$. Solving for $p$ gives the sigmoid. Hence the interpretation: the weight $w_j$ shows the change in log-odds when the feature increases by one, and $e^{w_j}$ shows by what factor the odds themselves change.

    6. Why is logistic regression trained on log-loss rather than MSE?

      First, MSE combined with the sigmoid gives a non-convex loss function with local minima. Second, the derivative of the sigmoid vanishes at the extremes, so on a confident error the MSE gradient is negligible and training stops. With log-loss these factors cancel, and the gradient is proportional to the error $(\hat{y}-y)$.

    7. What happens with perfectly separable classes, and why?

      The optimal weights go to infinity: the model can keep increasing its confidence, continuing to reduce log-loss, since it reaches a perfect zero only in the limit. Training doesn't converge or yields huge coefficients. Regularization prevents this by bounding the magnitude of the weights.

    8. Why does logistic regression need scaling, even though by itself it's insensitive to it?

      Without regularization, scale affects only the magnitude of the coefficients, not the predictions. However, regularization penalizes the weights, and their magnitude depends on the feature's units, so features on different scales are penalized incomparably. In sklearn regularization is on by default, so you should always scale.

  39. Decision Trees↗

    A decision tree is a logical classification algorithm.

    In the simplest terms: it's a game of "Guess Who." The model asks a sequence of yes/no questions ("is age over 60?", "any late payments?") and narrows down the options after each one. When the questions run out, the object lands in a leaf that holds the answer.

    If the feature "age" > 60 and "the patient has had a heart attack before," then don't operate: the risk of a negative outcome is 60%.

    If "a phone number is provided in the application" and "salary > 2000$" and "loan amount < 5000$", then the loan can be issued: the default risk is 5%.

    The phrase "we choose the best split" explains nothing until you say what "best" means. And it means this: a split is good if the resulting groups have more order after it.

    The mechanics at each node:

    1. Iterate over all features and, for each, all reasonable thresholds (usually midpoints between adjacent values).
    2. For each option, mentally split the objects into two groups and compute how much the "heterogeneity" decreased (measured by a criterion — Gini, entropy, or MSE for regression).
    3. Take the option with the largest decrease and split the node on it.
    4. Repeat for each of the two resulting groups.

    Example: if a node has 50 objects of class 0 and 50 of class 1, and splitting on "income > 80k" yields groups of "45 vs. 5" and "5 vs. 45," disorder dropped sharply — an excellent split. But if you get "25 vs. 25" and "25 vs. 25," nothing changed, and the feature is useless.

    Note the word "repeat" in the description above: the tree makes a decision at each node separately, based only on the current step, and never revisits it.

    This makes the algorithm fast but doesn't guarantee optimality: finding the best tree overall is NP-complete, and in practice nobody solves it. The split with the best gain right now may close off a pair of excellent splits one level down.

    The classic example is an XOR relationship: the answer is 1 when exactly one of two binary features is 1. Neither feature on its own gives any gain, so the greedy algorithm sees no reason to split. But make one "useless" split, and at the next level the problem is solved perfectly. This is partly why post-pruning works better than early stopping — it builds the full tree and prunes afterward.

    Before training a logical classification algorithm, you need to go through several stages:

    • Choosing a family of rules for finding patterns
    • Choosing an informativeness criterion
    • Building the classifier

    Consider a feature $f_j(x)$ and values $a_j, b_j$. Common families of rules for finding patterns are:

    • Threshold condition (decision stump): $$f_j(x) \leq a_j$$ or $$a_j \leq f_j(x) \leq b_j$$

    • Conjunction of threshold conditions: $$\underbrace{[a_j \leq f_j(x) \leq b_j]}_{\text{first condition}} \land \underbrace{[a_k \leq f_k(x) \leq b_k]}_{\text{second condition}} \land \dots \land \underbrace{[a_l \leq f_l(x) \leq b_l]}_{\text{last condition}}$$

      or in shorthand: $$\bigwedge_{j=1}^{l} [a_j \leq f_j(x) \leq b_j]$$

    The idea is to cover each class separately with simple rules — to describe what properties objects have so that we can assign them to one class or another. Each such rule is fairly simple and can't single out an entire class on its own.

    A decision tree is a conjunction of threshold conditions.

    A leaf is more than just a class label. It stores the distribution of training objects that fell into that region, and everything else is derived from it:

    • class label — the most frequent class among them (the mode);
    • probability — the share of the class in the leaf. If 8 objects of class 1 and 2 objects of class 0 land in a leaf, predict_proba returns 0.8;
    • regression value — the mean (or median) of the targets of the leaf's objects.

    Incidentally, this shows why a single tree's probabilities are coarse: they take only a few discrete values, determined by the number of objects in the leaf. A leaf with two objects can only give 0, 0.5, and 1 — hardly calibrated probabilities, and one more reason to use ensembles.

    ResourceDescription
