    StatQuest: Decision Trees, Part 2 - Feature Selection and Missing DataAutomatic feature selection. Ways to handle missing values: filling with the mode, correlation with other features, using the mean, median, or linear regression.
    Regression Trees, Clearly Explained!!!Building regression trees to predict numbers: minimizing the sum of squared residuals (SSR) to find thresholds, fighting overfitting with node limits.
    Decision treesDefinition of a tree, the construction idea, and leaf labels. Classification, regression. Impurity measures (Gini, etc.). Feature importance. Handling missing values, surrogate splits. Categorical features. Good coverage of where trees apply and their pros and cons. Contains math formulas, but written clearly and systematically (in Russian).

    1. What is a decision tree and how is the class decision made?

      A tree is a chain of threshold conditions on features. Each internal node splits objects by a rule $f_j(x) \leq a_j$. An object goes down the branches to a leaf, where it gets the predicted class (or value, for regression).

    2. What is an informativeness criterion and how is it used when building a tree?

      The criterion (Gini, entropy, MSE for regression) measures the "heterogeneity" of a subset of objects. When building, the tree chooses the split that most reduces heterogeneity in the resulting subsets — it searches for the most informative feature and threshold.

    3. Why is a decision tree prone to overfitting?

      Without constraints, a tree grows until each leaf contains a single object — perfect memorization of the training data but zero generalization. Regularization: limiting depth, the minimum number of objects per leaf, pruning.

    4. What makes decision trees convenient compared to logistic regression?

      They don't require feature scaling, handle categorical features naturally, are robust to outliers, and are easy to interpret. But they're unstable (sensitive to small data changes) and prone to overfitting. Ensembles (Random Forest, Boosting) fix these weaknesses.

    5. How exactly does a tree choose a split at a node?

      It iterates over all features and all reasonable thresholds (usually midpoints between adjacent values), computes for each option how much heterogeneity decreases in the resulting groups according to the chosen criterion (Gini, entropy, MSE), and takes the option with the largest decrease. Then the procedure repeats for each resulting group.

    6. Why is tree building called greedy, and why is that bad?

      The decision is made at each node separately, based only on the current gain, and is never revisited. Finding the optimal tree overall is NP-complete. Because of greediness, the algorithm may miss a relationship like XOR, where neither feature alone gives any gain, yet together they give a perfect separation.

    7. What is stored in a tree leaf, and why are a single tree's probabilities coarse?

      A leaf holds the distribution of training objects that fell into it: the label is the mode, the probability is the class share, and for regression, the mean. So probabilities take only a few discrete values determined by the number of objects in the leaf: a leaf with two objects can only give 0, 0.5, and 1.

  40. Splitting Criteria

    How do you build a binary decision tree from a training set? To understand this process better, let's take a very simple example: splitting a sequence of red and blue balls into, well, red and blue.

    Here the feature vector has a single component, a number — the position of the ball. It's the only feature we can use to split this sequence. Let's denote it $x$, and the split threshold $t$. Every internal node of the binary decision tree will use a predicate of the form $x \leq t$:

    Why this particular way? In fact, other options are possible too. At each node, the threshold $t$ should be chosen so that one part contains as many representatives of a single class (balls of one color) as possible, and the other contains all the rest. That is, it's a common-sense criterion, a natural heuristic that reduces the depth of the tree.

    But why minimize the depth of a decision tree at all? Let it be whatever the algorithm makes it. For example, we could iterate over thresholds $t$ from $0$ to $19$ one by one and, sooner or later, we'd be guaranteed to split the sequence into red and blue.

    Yes, the tree would be much deeper — so what? However, we must always remember that this sequence is just the training set.

    We intend to use this tree for other similar sequences with a somewhat different distribution of blue and red balls. Otherwise, this wouldn't be a machine learning problem, just a partitioning of one specific sample. And notice that the shallower the decision tree, the fewer split thresholds it uses and the higher, on average, its generalization ability. That's exactly why people try to build trees of minimal depth.

    A splitting criterion is a function that measures how well a split separates the data by class. It helps choose which feature and which threshold to split on.

    In the simplest terms: it's a way to measure the disorder in a node with a number. As long as "a good split" is just words, the algorithm can't choose anything. Once disorder is expressed as a number, the problem becomes a straightforward search: try all the options and take the one where disorder dropped the most.

    In the example, at each internal node the threshold $t$ was chosen so that one part contained as many representatives of one class (balls of one color) as possible, and the other whatever was left — i.e., all the rest. That's a heuristic.

    Our goal is to give the algorithm roughly the same heuristic. Mathematicians turned to earlier work on a variety of formulas and offered several criteria for predicate quality. One of the popular ones is entropy:

    $$H(x) = -\sum_{i=1}^{n} p_i \log_2 p_i$$

    This quantity is known to characterize the disorder of a system. The more diversity, the higher the entropy; conversely, the more order, the lower it is. It can naturally be used to evaluate the current split at each node.

    • Suppose we have 6 balls: 4 red and 2 blue. The probability of drawing a red ball is $\frac{4}{6}$, and a blue one $\frac{2}{6}$. Then the entropy is: $$H(x) = -\left( \frac{4}{6} \log_2 \frac{4}{6} + \frac{2}{6} \log_2 \frac{2}{6} \right) = 0.918$$
    • Suppose we have 6 balls: 5 red and 1 blue. Then the entropy is: $$H(x) = -\left( \frac{5}{6} \log_2 \frac{5}{6} + \frac{1}{6} \log_2 \frac{1}{6} \right) = 0.65$$
    • Suppose we have 6 balls: 6 red and 0 blue. Then the entropy is: $$H(x) = -\left( \frac{6}{6} \log_2 \frac{6}{6} + \frac{0}{6} \log_2 \frac{0}{6} \right) = 0$$

    In decision trees, this characteristic is called impurity (uncertainty/mixedness/heterogeneity). The lower it is, the better (purer) the data in the node. Conversely, the higher it is, the more diverse (chaotic) the data in the node.

    Going back to the ball example, we have 11 red and 9 blue balls. The sequence consists of $N=2$ classes with the following probabilities:

    $$p_1 = \frac{11}{20}, \quad p_2 = \frac{9}{20}$$

    Therefore, the impurity (here, entropy) of the root node is:

    $$S_0 = -\left( \frac{11}{20} \log_2 \frac{11}{20} + \frac{9}{20} \log_2 \frac{9}{20} \right) = 0.993$$

    Next, we choose the predicate with threshold $t = 12$ ($x \leq 12$) and get two subsets with impurities (entropies):

    $$S_1 = -\frac{5}{13}\log_2\frac{5}{13} - \frac{8}{13}\log_2\frac{8}{13} = 0.96$$

    $$S_2 = -\frac{6}{7}\log_2\frac{6}{7} - \frac{1}{7}\log_2\frac{1}{7} = 0.6$$

    Remark: The value 0.6 is much lower than the initial value $0,993$, which means the second sequence has more order than the original one. That's very good. This is exactly what we're aiming for when choosing the threshold $t$.

    Now, from the values $S_0, S_1, S_2$, we need to form a single criterion (one number) to judge the quality of splitting the set into two subsets. This can also be done in different ways, but the following formula, called information gain, is commonly used:

    $$IG(Q) = S_0 - \sum_{i=1}^{q} \frac{N_i}{N} S_i,$$

    where $q$ is the number of groups (subsets) after the split; $N_i$ is the number of elements in the $i$-th group after the split; $Q$ is the split criterion (predicate). Let's compute the information gain for our example:

    $$IG(x \leq 12) = S_0 - \frac{13}{20}S_1 - \frac{7}{20}S_2 = 0.16$$

    Remark: If you compute this criterion for other thresholds $t$, it turns out that $t = 12$ gives the largest value. So it's the best split with respect to the chosen criteria: entropy and information gain.

    Mathematically, finding the best predicate at the current node can be written as:

    $$t^{*} = \arg\max_{t \in T} IG(t)$$

    Besides entropy, other criteria are used as impurity measures. For example, Gini impurity:

    $$G(x) = 1 - \sum_{i=1}^{n} p_i^2,$$

    where $p_i$ is the probability (frequency) of objects of the $i$-th class in the node.

    Both criteria measure the same thing and almost always produce identical trees, but each has its own interpretation:

    • Gini $= 1 - \sum p_i^2$ is the probability of making a mistake if you randomly pick an object from the node and assign it a random class according to the class proportions in that node. A purely probabilistic quantity.
    • Entropy $= -\sum p_i \log_2 p_i$ is the average number of bits needed to encode an object's class. A quantity from information theory.

    The practical differences are minimal:

    • for a binary problem, the maximum of Gini is 0.5 and of entropy is 1, but the curves are almost identical in shape;
    • entropy "punishes" heterogeneous nodes slightly more (because of the logarithm) and so occasionally builds slightly more balanced trees;
    • Gini is faster to compute — no logarithm, only multiplications, and the criterion is evaluated millions of times. That's why it's the default in sklearn.

    If you're asked in an interview which to choose, the honest answer is: the difference between them is almost always smaller than the effect of changing max_depth or min_samples_leaf, so those are what you should tune instead.

    An important flaw of the criterion worth knowing about.

    Information gain systematically prefers features with many unique values. The extreme case is a customer ID: splitting on it produces single-object leaves, each perfectly "pure," and the information gain is maximal. Formally the criterion did its job flawlessly; substantively, the model memorized the table and generalized nothing.

    The same effect, only milder, appears for any high-cardinality feature (zip code, product ID) and for continuous features with many unique values.

    Consequences:

    • there's a correction — Gain Ratio (in C4.5), which normalizes the gain by how fragmented the split is;
    • trees in sklearn are binary, which mitigates the problem but doesn't eliminate it;
    • this is also the source of the feature_importances_ bias discussed in other questions: high-cardinality features look more important than they are.

    In practice, the Gini criterion works almost the same as entropy. This is clearly visible from the plots of these criteria for binary classification.

    Trees built on training data may perform poorly on new data. Lack of generalization leads to overfitting and is a problem for decision trees. As a result, decision tree algorithms have many hyperparameters that can be tuned during training. Most of them concern when to stop training.

    DecisionTreeClassifier from sklearn has the max_depth hyperparameter, which controls how many splits to make before training stops.

    1. What is a splitting criterion and why is it needed?

      A splitting criterion is a function that evaluates the quality of a split at a tree node. It determines which feature and threshold to split on so as to get maximally "pure" subsets and build a tree of minimal depth.

    2. What is impurity and how does entropy measure it?

      Impurity is a measure of how mixed the class composition of a node is. Entropy $H(x) = -\sum_i p_i \log_2 p_i$: equals 0 for a homogeneous node (one class) and is maximal for a uniform distribution. The lower the impurity, the "purer" the split.

    3. What is information gain and how is it used to choose a threshold?

      $IG(Q) = S_0 - \sum_i \frac{N_i}{N} S_i$ is the reduction in impurity after the split. The best predicate: $t^* = \arg\max_t IG(t)$. All thresholds across all features are tried, and the one with the largest gain is chosen.

    4. How does the Gini criterion differ from entropy?

      Gini: $G(x) = 1 - \sum_i p_i^2$. Computationally simpler (no logarithm). In practice it gives nearly identical results to entropy — the curves practically coincide. sklearn uses Gini by default.

    5. Why do trees overfit and how is this fought?

      Without constraints, a tree memorizes the entire training set (depth up to the number of objects). This is fought with regularization: max_depth, min_samples_leaf, min_samples_split — these hyperparameters limit tree growth and improve generalization.

    6. How are Gini and entropy interpreted, and why is Gini the default?

      Gini is the probability of a mistake when assigning a random object in the node a random class according to class proportions. Entropy is the average number of bits needed to encode the class. The results almost coincide, but Gini doesn't require computing a logarithm and is therefore faster, and the criterion is evaluated millions of times during training.

    7. Why is information gain biased toward features with many unique values?

      Such a feature lets you slice the set into many small, perfectly pure groups: in the limit, splitting on a customer ID produces single-object leaves with zero impurity and maximal gain. Formally the criterion is optimal; substantively the model just memorized the table. The correction is Gain Ratio, which normalizes the gain by how fragmented the split is.

    8. Why do we aim to build trees of minimal depth?

      The training set is just a sample from the overall distribution, and a deep tree describes its particulars rather than the underlying pattern. The shallower the tree, the fewer split thresholds and the more objects each decision is based on — and therefore the higher the generalization ability.

  41. Regression trees

    A regression tree is a decision tree in which the predicted variable is continuous rather than categorical. Instead of trying to assign an object to one of the classes, a regression tree estimates a numerical value (e.g., price, age, income, time, etc.).

    In the simplest terms: the tree splits objects into groups and predicts the mean in each one. To all apartments that fall into the leaf "area > 80 and downtown", it will give the same price — the average over the training apartments in that leaf.

    This leads to the most important property of trees in regression: the prediction is piecewise constant. Within a single leaf the answer does not change at all, and at the leaf boundary it jumps in a step.

    This creates two practical limitations:

    • The tree approximates a smooth dependency with a staircase. It has to build a linear trend out of many small steps, spending depth on it, whereas linear regression would describe it with two numbers.
    • There is no extrapolation at all. Train a tree on apartments up to 100 m², ask about 300 m² — the object lands in the rightmost leaf and gets the same value as a 100 m² apartment. There will be no continuation of the trend: the tree can only average what it has seen.

    That is exactly why for tasks with a clear trend (time series, extrapolation), trees and their ensembles are used either with caution or together with a linear model that takes care of the trend.

    We want to predict an apartment's price from the features area, floor, and district. The regression tree will partition the feature space so that each leaf contains apartments with prices as similar as possible.

    The main idea is the same as for classification trees: the data is recursively split into subsets using threshold conditions. However, the leaves now contain a numerical prediction value — usually the mean of the target over all objects in the leaf:

    $$\hat{y}_L = \frac{1}{|L|} \sum_{i \in L} y_i$$

    The splitting criterion for regression trees is a function that measures the "homogeneity" of target values within a subsample. The most commonly used measures are:

    • Mean squared error (MSE): $$MSE = \frac{1}{|L|} \sum_{i \in L} (y_i - \bar{y}_L)^2$$
    • Mean absolute error (MAE): $$MAE = \frac{1}{|L|} \sum_{i \in L} |y_i - \tilde{y}_L|$$

    The smaller the error in the leaves, the better the split.

    Suppose we have data on car prices. The feature is the car's age, the target is the price. The algorithm will search for the age threshold at which the price spread in each subgroup is minimal:

    $$t^{*} = \arg\min_{t \in T} \left( \sum_{x_i \le t} (y_i - \bar{y}_{left})^2 + \sum_{x_i > t} (y_i - \bar{y}_{right})^2 \right)$$

    After the optimal threshold is chosen, the tree splits into two branches, and the process continues recursively until a stopping condition is met (e.g., a minimum number of objects in a leaf).

    Regression trees, like classification trees, are prone to overfitting. So the same regularization methods are used:

    • limiting the tree depth (max_depth)
    • the minimum number of objects in a leaf (min_samples_leaf)
    • the minimum error reduction per split (min_impurity_decrease)

    There is a nice internal logic here worth noticing: the criterion and the leaf value are consistent.

    If splits are evaluated by MSE, the optimal prediction within a leaf is the mean, since the mean is exactly what minimizes the sum of squared deviations. If the MAE criterion is used, the optimal value becomes the median.

    The practical takeaway: by choosing criterion='absolute_error', you get a tree that is robust to outliers on both fronts — in the choice of splits and in the leaf values. The price is speed: the MAE criterion is noticeably slower to compute than MSE.

    CriterionClassification treeRegression tree
    TargetCategorical (class: $0,1,\dots,M$)Continuous (numerical value)
    Splitting criterionEntropy, Gini, information gainMean squared error (MSE), MAE
    Leaf valueThe most probable classMean of the target
    Interpretation"Which class does the object belong to?""What numerical value should be predicted?"
    Example tasksEmail classification (spam/not spam), disease diagnosisForecasting price, income, temperature, time
    sklearn classDecisionTreeClassifierDecisionTreeRegressor

    Practice task on the platform

    1. How does a regression tree differ from a classification tree?

      The target is continuous rather than categorical. The leaves hold the mean $\hat{y}_L = \frac{1}{|L|}\sum_{i \in L} y_i$. The splitting criterion is MSE or MAE rather than entropy/Gini. The prediction is a number, not a class.

    2. How is the split threshold chosen in a regression tree?

      All features and thresholds are enumerated; the one that minimizes the weighted sum of MSE in the two children is chosen: $t^* = \arg\min_t \left(\sum_{x_i \leq t}(y_i - \bar{y}_{left})^2 + \sum_{x_i > t}(y_i - \bar{y}_{right})^2\right)$.

    3. How does a regression tree approximate a continuous function?

      With a piecewise constant function: in each region of the feature space, the prediction is the mean over the training samples in that region. Deep trees "memorize" every object — overfitting.

    4. Which regularization methods are applied to regression trees?

      max_depth limits depth, min_samples_leaf sets the minimum number of objects per leaf, min_impurity_decrease sets the minimum MSE reduction per split. The same as for classification — the logic is identical.

    5. Why can't a regression tree extrapolate?

      The prediction in a leaf is the mean over the training objects that fell into that region. Beyond the range of training values there are no new leaves; the object lands in the outermost one and gets the same value as the most extreme training object. A continuation of the trend never arises in principle — the tree can only average what it has seen.

    6. Why does the leaf hold the mean under the MSE criterion and the median under MAE?

      The leaf value is chosen to minimize the same criterion used to evaluate the split. The mean minimizes the sum of squared deviations, the median minimizes the sum of absolute deviations. So criterion='absolute_error' gives a tree that is robust to outliers both in choosing splits and in its predictions, but it is noticeably slower to compute.

  42. Pros and Cons of Decision Trees

    Almost all properties of a tree follow from one fact: a tree cuts the feature space into rectangular regions using axis-perpendicular thresholds of the form "feature > threshold", and outputs a constant in each region.

    This immediately implies:

    • feature scale doesn't matter — the threshold simply gets recomputed ($x > 5000$ rubles and $x > 5$ thousand rubles give the same split);
    • nonlinearity comes for free — just cut more pieces;
    • prediction beyond the training data is impossible — there are simply no pieces there, and the tree returns the constant of the nearest one;
    • on the other hand, the tree has to approximate a diagonal boundary with a "staircase" of many cuts, which is wasteful and unstable.
    • They capture nonlinear relationships between features and the target well.
    • Interpretable: a tree can be visualized, and the decision logic is easy to explain to a client.
    • They work with categorical features and missing values.
    • They don't require scaling or normalization of features.
    • Prone to overfitting, especially when the tree is deep.
    • Can't extrapolate — they predict only within the range of the training set.
    • Sensitive to hyperparameters: depth, minimum number of objects per leaf, etc.
    • A standalone tree is often inferior in quality to ensembles (bagging, boosting).

    Take a dataset and train a tree. Then remove one object and retrain.

    If the removed object influenced the choice of the root feature (and at the root, features often compete almost evenly — say, "income" gives a criterion gain of 0.0312 and "age" 0.0309), the root split will change. And along with the root, the entire tree below changes completely: different subsets, different thresholds, a different structure.

    This is high variance: the model depends heavily on the particular training set. A direct consequence is that trees are ideal base models for bagging: averaging many noisy but unbiased models sharply reduces variance, and that's exactly what Random Forest is built on.

    • "A tree handles missing values" — depends on the implementation. CatBoost, LightGBM, and XGBoost have mechanisms for this (a missing value is sent to whichever branch is more beneficial), but sklearn's DecisionTreeClassifier doesn't accept missing values and will fail with an error.
    • "Interpretable" is true only for shallow trees. A depth-20 tree can have up to a million leaves; you can formally trace a path, but you can no longer explain such logic to the business.
    • A tree's feature importance is biased. The built-in feature_importances_ inflates the contribution of features with many unique values, because they have more possible thresholds. For an honest estimate, use permutation importance or SHAP.
    • "Doesn't require scaling" doesn't mean "doesn't require preprocessing." Missing values, rare high-cardinality categories, and leakage remain problems.

    1. Why are decision trees interpretable?

      A tree is visualized as a chain of "if-then" conditions. You can trace the path from the root to a leaf and understand why the model made a particular decision. This is valuable in medicine, finance, and law.

    2. Why can't trees extrapolate?

      The prediction in a leaf is the mean or mode of the training set in that region. Beyond the training range of feature values, the tree returns the same value as in the outermost leaf — there's no extrapolation. Neural networks and linear models are better in this respect.

    3. Why is a single tree inferior to ensembles?

      A tree is an unstable model: small changes in the data greatly change its structure. High variance. Ensembles (Random Forest — bagging, XGBoost — boosting) average or sequentially correct errors, reducing variance or bias.

    4. What does "doesn't require scaling" mean?

      A tree divides the space with threshold conditions on each feature independently. Shifting or scaling a feature doesn't change the order of values, and therefore doesn't change the split. This isn't true for KNN, SVM, and linear models.

    5. Why can removing a single observation completely change a tree?

      At a node, features often compete almost evenly, and a small change in the sample changes the winner. If the root split changes, the whole structure below is built on different subsets — the tree changes entirely. This is the high variance that makes trees well suited as base models for bagging.

    6. Why can a tree's built-in feature_importances_ be misleading?

      It's based on the total criterion gain and systematically inflates the importance of features with many unique values: they have more possible thresholds and therefore more chances to give a good gain by accident. Permutation importance or SHAP are more reliable.

  43. How Can You Regularize a Decision Tree?

    Tree regularization refers to ways of limiting model complexity to prevent overfitting.

    In the simplest terms: don't let the tree grow until every leaf has a single object. An unconstrained tree will always train down to zero error on the training set — simply by giving each object its own personal leaf. That's not knowledge but memorization, and such a model is useless on new data.

    An unconstrained tree on a dataset of 1000 objects grows to 1000 leaves: each contains exactly one object, the training error is zero, and on the test set it's a disaster.

    This is clear from the "rules" it learns: instead of a meaningful "if income is above 80 thousand and there are no late payments, the loan will be repaid," you get "if income is between 81,200 and 81,400, age is 34, and tenure is 5 years, it will be repaid" — i.e., a description of one specific customer from the training set. A new customer will never fit such a rule.

    The constraint min_samples_leaf=20 physically forbids such rules: every statement the model makes must rest on at least 20 observations.

    They're conveniently divided into two kinds — pre-pruning (stops growth early) and post-pruning (trims an already-grown tree):

    • max_depth — a depth limit. The crudest and most intuitive knob: depth $d$ means at most $2^d$ leaves and at most $d$ conditions in any rule.
    • min_samples_leaf — the minimum number of objects per leaf. Directly forbids conclusions based on a couple of observations.
    • min_samples_split — the minimum size of a node that's allowed to be split at all.
    • min_impurity_decrease — don't split if the split barely improves the criterion. Cuts off "cosmetic" splits.
    • max_features — how many features to consider when choosing a split. In a single tree it's regularization; in a Random Forest it's the main source of tree diversity.
    • Pruning — grow the tree fully, then remove branches that don't pay for their complexity (ccp_alpha, cost-complexity pruning).

    At first glance both approaches do the same thing, but early stopping has a fundamental flaw: it's shortsighted.

    The algorithm builds the tree greedily, one split at a time. Sometimes the current split barely improves the criterion — and under the min_impurity_decrease rule growth stops there. But that very split might have opened the way to a pair of excellent splits one level down (the classic example is an XOR relationship: neither feature alone gives any gain, but together they give a perfect separation).

    Post-pruning doesn't have this problem: the tree first grows fully and sees all possible continuations, and only then are unnecessary branches cut off. The price is the time to build the full tree.

    How ccp_alpha works: it minimizes $R_\alpha(T) = R(T) + \alpha |T|$, where $R(T)$ is the tree's error, $|T|$ is the number of leaves, and $\alpha$ is the cost of one leaf. The larger $\alpha$, the more expensive complexity becomes and the more aggressive the pruning. The value of $\alpha$ is tuned by cross-validation.

    • Turning only one knob. People often limit max_depth but leave min_samples_leaf at 1 — and the tree still draws conclusions from a single object, just at a shallower depth.
    • Assuming the defaults are safe. sklearn's DecisionTreeClassifier has max_depth=None and min_samples_leaf=1, i.e., by default there's no regularization at all, and the tree is guaranteed to overfit.
    • Tuning parameters on train. Any constraint worsens the training metric by definition; only the validation curve is meaningful.
    • Carrying settings over between a single tree and an ensemble. In a Random Forest, trees are deliberately grown deep — overfitting there is cured by averaging, not pruning. In boosting, on the contrary, trees are kept shallow (depth 3–6).

    A task on the platform

    A task on the platform

    1. Why regularize a decision tree?

      Without constraints, a tree memorizes the training set completely — overfitting. Regularization limits model complexity and improves generalization.

    2. How do max_depth and min_samples_leaf affect a tree?

      max_depth limits depth — the smaller it is, the simpler the model. min_samples_leaf sets the minimum number of objects per leaf — preventing "tiny" leaves fitted to outliers.

    3. What is pruning and when is it used?

      Pruning is removing low-value branches after training the full tree. There's pre-pruning (stopping during training) and post-pruning (trimming afterward). sklearn uses ccp_alpha for cost-complexity pruning.

    4. What does max_features do, and why is it needed in Random Forest?

      max_features limits the number of features considered at each split. In a Random Forest, this creates diversity between trees — each tree "sees" a random subset of features, reducing the correlation between the ensemble's trees.

    5. Why does post-pruning usually work better than early stopping?

      The greedy algorithm is shortsighted: a split that barely improves the criterion may open the way to excellent splits one level down (the classic example is an XOR relationship). Early stopping would cut off that branch and never see what follows, whereas post-pruning first builds the whole tree and cuts off what's unnecessary already knowing all possible continuations.

    6. How does ccp_alpha work?

      It minimizes $R_\alpha(T) = R(T) + \alpha|T|$, where $R(T)$ is the tree's error, $|T|$ is the number of leaves, and $\alpha$ is the cost of one leaf. The larger $\alpha$, the more expensive complexity is and the more the tree is pruned. The value is tuned by cross-validation.

    7. Why aren't trees in a Random Forest regularized the same way as single trees?

      In bagging, an individual tree's overfitting is cured by averaging: you need unstable models with low bias, so the trees are grown deep. In boosting it's the opposite — the fight is against bias, and trees are kept shallow (depth 3–6).

  44. Ensembles: Bagging↗

    The idea of bagging (bootstrap aggregation) is as follows. Suppose the training set consists of $n$ objects. Draw $n$ examples from it uniformly at random, with replacement. We get a new sample $X_1$ in which some elements of the original set are missing and some may appear several times. Using some algorithm $b$, train a model on this sample: $b_1(x)=b(x,X_1)$. Repeat the procedure: form a second sample $X_2$ of $n$ elements with replacement and, using the same algorithm, train a model $b_2(x)=b(x,X_2)$ on it. Repeating the procedure $k$ times gives $k$ models trained on $k$ samples. To get a single prediction, average the predictions of all models:

    $$a(x) = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}b_i(x)$$

    In the simplest terms: it's polling a panel of experts instead of trusting one. Each model is trained on a slightly different version of the data, so they make different mistakes — some overestimate, some underestimate. When averaged, the random errors cancel out, while the common pattern everyone saw remains.

    Let each base model have variance $\sigma^2$, and let the models be independent. The variance of the mean of $k$ independent quantities is:

    $$\mathrm{Var}\left(\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k} b_i\right) = \frac{1}{k^2} \cdot k\sigma^2 = \frac{\sigma^2}{k}$$

    Hence the promised "variance $k$ times smaller." But nothing happens to the bias: the average of values each of which misses by $b$ on average also misses by $b$. Averaging removes the random component of the error and is powerless against the systematic one.

    In practice, however, the models are trained on overlapping subsamples and are therefore correlated. Accounting for correlation $\rho$, the formula becomes

    $$\rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1 - \rho}{k}\sigma^2$$

    The second term goes to zero as $k$ grows, but the first does not. So adding trees forever, we hit a ceiling of $\rho\sigma^2$: there's no further gain until we reduce the correlation between models. Random Forest grows directly out of this formula: it randomly selects features at each node precisely to reduce $\rho$.

    In bootstrapping, each subsample is drawn with replacement, so some objects don't make it in. Their share is easy to compute: the probability of not being chosen in $n$ draws is $(1 - 1/n)^n \to e^{-1} \approx 0.368$.

    So about 37% of objects don't participate in training each particular model — they're called out-of-bag (OOB). They can serve as a ready-made validation set: for each object, average the predictions of only those models that didn't see it. This gives an honest quality estimate without a separate holdout set and without cross-validation (in sklearn, the parameter oob_score=True).

    The process of generating subsamples by sampling with replacement is called bootstrapping, and the models $b_1(x), b_2(x), \dots, b_k(x)$ are often called base models/algorithms. The model $a(x)$ is called an ensemble of these models.

    • The bias of the ensemble equals the bias of a single algorithm.
    • The variance of the ensemble is $k$ times smaller than that of a single algorithm (with independent base models).
    • If the base model is stable. Averaging a hundred nearly identical linear regressions is pointless: they have low variance and high bias, and bagging cures exactly the former. It needs unstable models — deep trees.
    • If the model underfits. Averaging doesn't remove bias. An ensemble of a hundred depth-1 stumps stays just as crude — this calls for boosting, not bagging.
    • Interpretability is the price. You can show a single tree to a client; an average of 500, not so much.
    • Inference cost grows. Memory and prediction time increase $k$-fold, although training and prediction parallelize perfectly — unlike boosting.

    Let our target relationship $f(x)$ be given by

    $$f(x) = x \sin(x) + \varepsilon,$$

    where $\varepsilon$ is a normally distributed random variable.

    Let's look at the predictions of depth-7 decision trees and of bagging over such trees. Train decision trees 100 times on different random samples of size 20. Also take bagging over 10 depth-7 decision trees as base learners and likewise train it 100 times on random samples of size 20:

    1. What is bagging and how does it build an ensemble?

      Bootstrap Aggregation: from a sample of size $n$, $k$ bootstrap subsamples (with replacement) are drawn, and a base model $b_i(x)$ is trained on each. The final prediction is the average: $a(x) = \frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^k b_i(x)$.

    2. How does bagging affect bias and variance?

      The bias of the ensemble equals the bias of a single algorithm — it doesn't change. The variance of the ensemble is $k$ times smaller than that of a single algorithm. Bagging reduces the spread of predictions without touching the bias.

    3. Why does bagging need unstable base models?

      The point is for the base models to be diverse (less correlated). Deep trees are unstable — a small change in the sample changes the tree a lot. Averaging diverse predictions yields a gain in variance.

    4. How does a random forest differ from plain bagging?

      Random Forest is bagging over trees with extra randomization: at each node, the split is searched not over all features but over a random subset (usually $\sqrt{n}$). This reduces correlation between trees and further lowers variance.

    5. Why does bagging reduce variance but not bias?

      The variance of the mean of $k$ independent models is $\sigma^2/k$ — random deviations cancel out. The bias, however, is the same for all base models: if each misses by $b$ on average, their mean misses by exactly as much. Averaging fights the random component of the error and is powerless against the systematic one.

    6. Why does quality stop improving as the number of trees increases?

      The base models are trained on overlapping subsamples and are therefore correlated. Accounting for correlation $\rho$, the ensemble variance is $\rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1-\rho}{k}\sigma^2$: the second term tends to zero as $k$ grows, but the first remains. Further gain is only possible by reducing correlation — which is exactly what Random Forest does by selecting random features at each node.

    7. What is the out-of-bag estimate and why is it free?

      With bootstrapping, a fraction $(1-1/n)^n \approx 37\%$ of objects doesn't make it into a subsample. For each object, you can average the predictions of only those models that didn't see it and get an honest quality estimate without a separate holdout set or cross-validation (oob_score=True in sklearn).

    45. Block 4

  45. Random Forest↗

    In the previous section we assumed that the base algorithms are uncorrelated, and thanks to that we got a very strong reduction in the ensemble's variance relative to its base algorithms. In real life, however, this is hard to achieve: after all, the base algorithms learned the same relationship on overlapping samples. So it would be strange if the correlation were actually zero. But in practice it turns out that strictly satisfying this assumption isn't necessary. It's enough for the algorithms to be somewhat dissimilar from each other. This is the basis for developing the bagging idea for decision trees — the random forest.

    A random forest is an ensemble of decision trees built on random subsamples of the training data.

    Each tree in a random forest is typically very simple (shallow), and it's always trained on some small random subsample of the data. Each tree in this ensemble makes a prediction for the object we feed into the random forest, and the output is the averaged prediction (voting).

    In the name "random forest," the word "random" refers to two independent mechanisms at once, and they shouldn't be confused:

    • Random objects (bagging). Each tree is trained on its own bootstrap subsample — $n$ objects drawn with replacement. About 37% of objects don't make it in.
    • Random features (the random subspace method). At each node of the tree, the search runs not over all features but over a randomly chosen subset (by default $\sqrt{d}$ for classification).

    Plain bagging over trees uses only the first mechanism. Adding the second is exactly what turns it into a Random Forest.

    Bagging alone turns out to be insufficient, and here's why.

    Suppose the data has one very strong feature. Then any tree, whatever subsample it's trained on, will put it at the root: it wins almost every time. The trees end up similar to each other, their errors are correlated, and averaging similar errors doesn't reduce variance.

    Recall the formula for ensemble variance with correlation $\rho$:

    $$\rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1-\rho}{k}\sigma^2$$

    The second term goes to zero as the number of trees grows, but the first remains — and it's larger the more correlated the trees are. So you can hit the ceiling precisely because the trees are similar, not because there are too few of them.

    Random feature selection targets exactly this problem: at nodes where the strong feature didn't make it into the candidate set, the tree has to look for other patterns. The trees become more diverse, $\rho$ drops, and the ensemble wins — even though each individual tree becomes weaker. This is the key and counterintuitive point: we deliberately weaken the members to improve the ensemble.

    Individual trees are very confident in their predictions, even when they shouldn't be. By averaging several trees, we effectively "smooth" the boundaries between classes.

    How deep should the trees in a random forest be?

    A model's error consists of bias and variance. We reduce variance with the bagging procedure. Bagging doesn't affect bias, yet we want the forest's bias to be small. Therefore, the trees the ensemble is built from must themselves have low bias.

    • Shallow trees have few parameters: a tree can only memorize the high-level features of a subsample.
    • Conversely, deep trees have no trouble memorizing a subsample in detail.

    Shallow trees will be similar across all subsamples, but they won't describe the target relationship in much detail. So as the training subsample changes, the prediction for a test object will be stable but not accurate (low variance, high bias).

    The prediction of deep trees for a test object will vary more depending on the training subsample, but on average it will be close to the truth (high variance, low bias).

    How many features should a tree get for training?

    Take the square root of the total number of features ($\sqrt{d}$) for classification and a third of the features ($d/3$) for regression. In sklearn these are max_features='sqrt' and max_features=1/3, respectively.

    By limiting the number of features used to train a single tree, we also control the quality of the random forest. The more features, the higher the correlation between trees and the weaker the ensembling effect. The fewer features, the weaker the trees themselves.

    How many trees should a random forest have?

    You can't reduce variance forever (the sample is finite). So it makes sense to plot the error against the number of trees and cap the forest size at the point where the error stops decreasing significantly.

    A second practical limit on the number of trees may be the ensemble's running time. However, the random forest has a nice property: it can be built and applied in parallel.

    This reduces running time if we have several processors. But there are most likely far fewer processors than trees, and the trees themselves are usually deep. So with a large number of trees, a Random Forest may run longer than desired, and the number of trees can be reduced at a slight cost in quality.

    • A Random Forest doesn't overfit as the number of trees grows. Adding trees doesn't hurt quality — it plateaus. This is a fundamental difference from boosting, where extra iterations do harm. A forest can overfit, but for a different reason — trees that are too deep on a small sample.
    • Trees in a forest should be deep. Bagging reduces variance, not bias, so the base models must have low bias. There's no need to limit their depth as strongly as for a single tree (unlike boosting, where the trees are shallow).
    • Built-in feature importance is biased. feature_importances_ inflates the contribution of features with many unique values. For conclusions, use permutation importance or SHAP.
    • A forest doesn't extrapolate. Like a single tree, it predicts the mean over a leaf's training objects, so outside the range of training values it returns a constant. For time trends, this makes it inapplicable without special feature engineering.
    • Forest probabilities are coarse, but better than a single tree's. Averaging proportions across trees gives much smoother values than the few discrete levels of a single tree.

    1. What is a Random Forest and how does it reduce variance compared to a single tree?

      An ensemble of trees trained on random subsamples of the data (bagging), with a random subset of features selected at each split. Averaging the predictions of decorrelated trees reduces variance while keeping bias low.

    2. Why should the trees in a Random Forest be deep?

      A deep tree has low bias — it describes the relationship in detail. Bagging reduces its variance. A shallow tree has high bias — the ensemble can't fix that; it will only reduce the variance of already poor trees.

    3. How does the number of features per split affect forest quality?

      Fewer features — the trees are more diverse, the correlation between them is lower, and ensembling is more effective. More features — the trees are stronger but more alike. Recommendations: $\sqrt{n}$ features for classification, $n/3$ for regression.

    4. How do you determine the optimal number of trees in a Random Forest?

      From a plot of error vs. number of trees: keep adding trees as long as the error decreases significantly. Trees in an RF can be trained in parallel — an advantage over boosting, where trees are built sequentially.

    5. What two sources of randomness does a Random Forest have, and why is each needed?

      The first is a bootstrap subsample of objects for each tree (that's plain bagging). The second is a random subset of features considered at each node (the random subspace method). The second mechanism is what turns bagging into a Random Forest: without it, all trees would put the same strong feature at the root and end up similar.

    6. Why does weakening individual trees improve the ensemble?

      Ensemble variance equals $\rho\sigma^2 + \frac{1-\rho}{k}\sigma^2$, and the first term isn't removed by adding more trees — it's determined by the correlation between them. Random feature selection forces the trees to find different patterns, lowers $\rho$, and thereby reduces the ensemble's variance, even though each individual tree becomes weaker.

    7. Can a Random Forest overfit from increasing the number of trees?

      No. As the number of trees grows, quality plateaus and doesn't degrade — unlike boosting, where extra iterations do harm. A forest can overfit for a different reason: trees that are too deep on a small sample. The number of trees is capped for running-time reasons, not quality.

  46. Boosting↗

    Boosting is an ensemble method in which, as in the methods above, many base algorithms from the same family are built and then combined into a stronger model.

    In the simplest terms: it is learning from mistakes, put on an assembly line. The first simple model makes a prediction and gets a lot wrong. The second model does not learn to solve the task from scratch but to correct the errors of the first one. The third corrects what remains after the first two. Each one alone is weak, but the sum is strong.

    We are predicting an apartment's price. The true price is 10M.

    • Model 1 (a very simple tree) says 8M. Error (residual): $10 - 8 = 2$M.
    • Model 2 is trained to predict not the price but this residual. It says $+1.5$M. The ensemble prediction: $8 + 1.5 = 9.5$M, the new residual is 0.5M.
    • Model 3 learns to predict the 0.5M and says $+0.3$. Prediction: 9.8M.

    And so on, hundreds of times. In practice each step is multiplied by a small coefficient (learning_rate, typically 0.01–0.1), i.e., only part of the correction is added rather than the whole: with small steps the ensemble converges more stably and overfits less, although more models are needed.

    The difference is that in bagging and random forests the base algorithms are trained independently and in parallel, whereas in boosting they are trained sequentially.

    Each subsequent base algorithm in boosting is trained to reduce the overall error of all its predecessors. As a result, the final composition has lower bias than each individual base algorithm (although variance may also decrease).

    This leads to a consequence that is counterintuitive for beginners: boosting uses deliberately weak base models — trees of depth 3–6, "stumps". This is the exact opposite of bagging, where Random Forest grows deep, overfit trees.

    The logic is about which part of the error each method fixes:

    • Bagging fights variance: take models with low bias but high variance and average them — variance drops while bias stays low. So the base model must be complex.
    • Boosting fights bias: add up many crude models, each of which contributes a small correction. If the base model were complex, the very first one would learn the entire training set, the residuals would become zero, and there would be nothing left to correct — except noise.

    This is also the main risk of boosting: if you keep adding trees, sooner or later the ensemble starts fitting noise, and the validation error goes up. That is why the number of iterations is tuned on the validation set (early_stopping) rather than simply set "high".

    • Sensitive to outliers. An anomalous object produces a huge residual, and the following models will stubbornly fit precisely to it — especially with a squared loss. Robust loss functions (Huber, MAE) and data cleaning help.
    • Hard to parallelize. Trees are built strictly sequentially: the next one depends on the residuals of the previous one. There is parallelism within a single tree, but not between trees. Bagging, by contrast, is perfectly parallel.
    • The number of iterations is not "the more the better". Without early stopping, extra trees overfit the ensemble. learning_rate and the number of trees are tuned together: a smaller step means more trees.
    • Requires careful validation. Boosting easily "learns" data leakage all the way to perfect metrics — it is on boosting that splitting problems most often come to light.

    1. What is the fundamental difference between boosting and bagging?

      In bagging, base algorithms are trained independently and in parallel, and the results are averaged. In boosting, they are trained sequentially: each next model corrects the errors of the previous ones. Bagging reduces variance, boosting reduces bias.

    2. How does boosting reduce the ensemble error?

      Each new base algorithm is trained on the "residuals" — what the previous models have not yet explained correctly. The final model is a weighted sum of base algorithms that focuses on hard examples.

    3. How does boosting affect bias and variance?

      Boosting primarily reduces the ensemble's bias — the final model is more complex than each base algorithm. Variance can also decrease. The risk is overfitting with a large number of iterations.

    4. Which well-known algorithms implement boosting?

      AdaBoost reweights objects by their errors. Gradient Boosting builds the next model on the negative gradient of the loss function. XGBoost, LightGBM, and CatBoost are optimized implementations of gradient boosting that dominate tabular data tasks.

    5. Why does boosting use weak base models and bagging strong ones?

      Bagging reduces variance by averaging, so the base models must be complex and overfit but unbiased. Boosting reduces bias by adding many small corrections: if the base model were complex, the very first one would learn the entire training set and the following ones would have only noise left to fit. Hence depth 3–6 trees in boosting versus deep trees in Random Forest.

    6. Why does boosting need a learning_rate, and how is it related to the number of trees?

      It scales the contribution of each new tree, so the ensemble adds only part of the correction rather than all of it. Small steps make convergence more stable and reduce overfitting but require more trees: halve the learning_rate and roughly double the number of iterations.

    7. Why is boosting more sensitive to outliers than bagging?

      Each subsequent model is trained on the residuals of the previous ones, and an outlier's residual is huge — especially with a squared loss, where the error enters squared. The ensemble starts deliberately fitting anomalous objects. Robust loss functions (Huber, MAE) and preliminary data cleaning help.

  47. Gradient Boosting↗

    Gradient boosting (GB) is an ensemble method that combines several weak learners, usually decision trees. Unlike plain boosting, each subsequent base learner is trained using the gradient of the ensemble's error.

    The core idea of GB is to correct the mistakes made by the previous models. The process is iterative: each new model is trained on the residual errors of the previous models.

    In the simplest terms: each next tree learns to predict not the answer but the miss of the previous ones. The first tree makes a rough guess, the second refines where the first fell short, the third refines what's left after the first two — and so on hundreds of times, in small corrections.

    The name is misleading: there is no gradient descent over tree weights here. The descent happens over the predictions.

    In ordinary gradient descent we move the parameters: $w \leftarrow w - \eta \nabla_w L$. In boosting we move, in exactly the same way, the vector of the model's predictions on the training objects itself: $a \leftarrow a - \eta \nabla_a L$.

    One catch: the gradient is just a set of $\ell$ numbers, one per training object, and it can't be computed on new data. So boosting approximates this vector with a tree: it trains a tree to predict the antigradient. A tree can generalize to new objects; a vector of numbers cannot.

    This gives the precise meaning of the phrase "trained on residuals": with a squared loss, the antigradient $-\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{1}{2}(a-y)^2 = y - a$ is exactly the residual. For other loss functions it is no longer the residual but the antigradient proper: for MAE it's the sign of the error $\text{sign}(y - a)$, for log-loss it's the difference between the label and the predicted probability.

    That is exactly why boosting works with any differentiable loss function: change $L$, and what the trees predict changes, while all the rest of the machinery stays the same.

    Connection to gradient descent

    Consider base learners $b_t$, an object $x$, and an ensemble $a_T(x)$:

    $$a_T(x) = \sum_{t=1}^T \alpha_t b_t(x),$$

    where $\alpha_t \in \mathbb{R}$ is the weight of the $t$-th base learner and $b_t(x)$ is the $t$-th base learner. The idea: we will train $\alpha_T$ and $b_T$ with the previous ones fixed. We define the quality criterion with a smooth loss function:

    $$L\!\left(\underbrace{\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T-1} \alpha_t b_t(\textcolor{orange}{x})}_{a_{T-1}} + \alpha b(\textcolor{orange}{x})}_{a_{T}},\ y\right)$$

    Compute this quantity for each object, going over all objects $x_i$ in the sample $X^\ell$:

    $$L_i = L\!\left(\underbrace{\underbrace{\sum_{t=1}^{T-1} \alpha_t b_t(\textcolor{orange}{x_i})}_{a_{T-1,i}} + \alpha b(\textcolor{orange}{x_i})}_{a_{T,i}},\ y_i\right)$$

    Sum them all up and pose the problem of minimizing the quality criterion over the available parameters $\alpha$ and the learner $b$:

    $$Q(\alpha, b; X^\ell) = \sum_{i=1}^\ell L_i \to \min_{\alpha,\, b}$$

    For each object $x_i$ we get an error. From these we can form vectors:

    • The vector of the current approximation: $$a_{T-1} = (a_{T-1,i})_{i=1}^\ell = \begin{pmatrix} a_{T-1,1}\\ a_{T-1,2}\\ \vdots\\ a_{T-1,\ell} \end{pmatrix}$$
    • The vector of the next approximation: $$a_{T} = (a_{T,i})_{i=1}^\ell = \begin{pmatrix} a_{T,1}\\ a_{T,2}\\ \vdots\\ a_{T,\ell} \end{pmatrix}$$

    Recall gradient descent:

    • $a_{0}$ is the initial approximation,
    • $a_{T} = a_{T-1} - \textcolor{red}{\alpha g}$ is an optimization step,
    • $g$ is the gradient vector, $\alpha$ is the step size.

    This is very similar to adding one base learner to the ensemble:

    $$a_{T} = a_{T-1} + \textcolor{red}{\alpha b(x)}$$

    We look for a base learner $b_t$ such that the vector $(b_T(x_i))_{i=1}^\ell$ approximates the antigradient vector $(-g_i)_{i=1}^\ell$:

    $$b_T = \arg\min_{b} \sum_{i=1}^\ell L_i(b(x_i) + g_i)^2$$

    The idea is to minimize the error of all previous learners $a_{T-1}$ as quickly as possible: we add the learner $\alpha_T b_T$ to the ensemble so as to move in the direction closest to the antigradient.

    You can play with boosting interactively on this site.

    • Gradient Boosting over Decision Trees (GBDT) is a supervised learning method: it learns from labeled examples where the correct answer is already known for each object.
    • GBDT is primarily used for:
      • Regression
      • Classification
      • Ranking
    • In practice GBDT is especially well suited to tabular data, where an object is described by a set of features: age, income, number of purchases, activity history, product characteristics, customer attributes, etc.
    • It is one of the strongest go-to methods for tasks where you need to capture a complex nonlinear dependency between the features and the target, while the data still has an ordinary tabular structure.
    • GBDT is most often used as a strong baseline or directly as the main production algorithm in applied tasks: credit scoring, anti-fraud, churn prediction, CTR / conversion prediction, risk modeling, ranking.
    • That said, GBDT is not universally the best choice for all data: it is especially strong on structured tabular features, but is usually not the most natural method for raw images, long texts, audio, or video.
    • GBDT is poorly suited for tasks that require extrapolation, i.e., confident prediction beyond the range of values the model saw during training. Since it is built on trees with threshold splits, it's easier for it to partition already-known regions of feature space than to continue a dependency "further out".
    • GBDT requires more careful tuning than many simple baseline models. If you mindlessly increase the number of trees or make them too complex, the model easily starts fitting the noise in the data.
    • Compared to bagging methods, trees in boosting are trained sequentially, not independently. Because of this, training parallelizes less well.
    • GBDT lacks the transparency of a single decision tree or a linear model: the final model consists of a large number of small trees, so it can no longer be "read by eye" as a set of simple rules.
    • It usually has low bias compared to simple models and can deliver very high quality on tabular data.
    • By adding trees sequentially, it can gradually reduce the error and approach a very strong solution.
    • However, when the ensemble is too powerful, the risk of high variance and overfitting grows.
    • It performs best with careful complexity control: tree depth, learning rate, number of iterations, subsampling, regularization.

    We're predicting an apartment's price; the true value is 10 million. Let learning_rate = 0.5.

    StepWhat the tree doesEnsemble predictionResidual
    Startinitial approximation (sample mean)6.04.0
    Tree 1learns to predict residual 4.0, outputs 3.6$6.0 + 0.5 \cdot 3.6 = 7.8$2.2
    Tree 2learns to predict residual 2.2, outputs 2.0$7.8 + 0.5 \cdot 2.0 = 8.8$1.2
    Tree 3learns to predict residual 1.2, outputs 1.1$8.8 + 0.5 \cdot 1.1 = 9.35$0.65

    Two things are clearly visible. First, the ensemble does not rewrite the prediction but adds a correction to it. Second, learning_rate = 0.5 means only half of the found correction is taken: movement is more cautious, but more trees are needed. With typical values of 0.01–0.1 the steps are tiny, and hundreds or thousands of trees are required.

    This is a fundamental difference from Random Forest, and interviewers always ask about it.

    In a forest, trees are independent and averaged: adding new trees brings quality to a plateau and does no harm. In boosting, each tree is added to the prediction and keeps reducing the error on the training set — including by fitting the noise. The train error falls monotonically, while the validation error first falls and then starts to grow.

    Hence two practical conclusions:

    • Early stopping is mandatory. The number of trees is not set at random but determined on validation: training stops when the metric has stopped improving for a given number of iterations.
    • learning_rate and the number of trees are tuned together. They are inversely related: halve the step, and you'll need roughly twice as many trees. The common tactic: fix a small learning_rate (0.05) and let early stopping determine the number of iterations.
    • Number of iterations / trees — how many trees will be added to the ensemble. The more iterations, the longer the model keeps training the sequence of corrections to the current prediction.
    • Learning rate — how strong the contribution of each new tree will be. A small step makes training more cautious but usually requires more trees.
    • Row subsampling — what fraction of training objects to use when building each tree. This adds randomness, helps fight overfitting, and sometimes speeds up training.
    • Feature subsampling — what fraction of features may be considered when building a tree or a split. This also reduces the risk of overfitting and makes the ensemble less dependent on individual features.
    • Tree depth — controls how complex the rules a single tree can build are. The deeper the tree, the more detailed and local dependencies it can learn, but the higher the risk of overfitting.
    • Maximum number of leaves — another way to limit tree complexity.
    • Minimum leaf / child node size — prohibits leaves that are too small. This is one of the anti-overfitting parameters: the model stops splitting the data into pieces that are too fine.
    • Minimum split gain — sets a threshold below which a new split is not considered beneficial enough to make.
    • Early stopping — stops training if validation quality has stopped improving. It's one of the most useful tuning tools because it helps avoid building unnecessary trees.
    • Validation metric — the criterion by which iterations are compared and the best version of the model is chosen. This is especially important when one loss function is optimized but you want to track quality with a different metric.

    GBDT is less interpretable than a single decision tree, because the final model consists not of one set of rules but of a large number of small trees that sequentially correct the overall prediction. So such a model can no longer be "read by eye" as a whole and quickly understood as a single decision-making algorithm. Interpretation of GBDT is usually done not by inspecting the model's structure itself, but through dedicated analysis tools that help understand which features influence the predictions and how.

    At the global level you can analyze the behavior of the model as a whole rather than an individual prediction. For example:

    • Feature importance — shows which features the model considers most useful for decision-making on average.
    • Permutation importance — shows how much the model's quality drops if the values of a single feature are randomly shuffled. If quality degrades a lot, the feature was truly important.
    • Partial dependence — shows how the model's prediction changes on average as one feature changes, looking at the model as a whole.

    Thus, GBDT does have interpretability, but it is mostly tool-based: the model is explained through additional analysis methods rather than by directly reading its internal structure.

    Despite the successes of neural networks, boosting remains the first choice for tabular tasks, and there are clear reasons for this:

    • Tabular data is heterogeneous. Columns have different meanings, scales, and types, and there is no spatial or temporal structure between them that convolutions or attention could exploit. Trees don't care about heterogeneity.
    • Threshold splits are natural for tables. "Income above 80 thousand" is exactly the form of rule that describes real tabular dependencies, whereas a neural network approximates such a step with difficulty.
    • No need for scaling and encoding — fewer chances to make a mistake in preprocessing.
    • Works well on small datasets. Thousands of rows are enough, whereas neural networks usually require substantially more data.
    • Trains fast and is easy to tune relative to neural network architectures.

    The flip side: on raw unstructured data — images, audio, text — boosting loses to neural networks outright, because that's exactly where there is structure it can't exploit.

    • Compared to a single tree: much stronger in quality, but less transparent and heavier.
    • Compared to Random Forest: usually better at "squeezing out" quality, but requires more careful tuning and is more sensitive to hyperparameters.
    • Compared to linear models: a much richer class of dependencies, but worse in simplicity, speed, and transparency of coefficients.
    • Compared to neural networks: on tabular data often competitive or better, but loses on raw unstructured modalities.

    Links

    ResourceDescription
    Gradient boosting (Rus)GBDT from intuition (residuals $\to$ antigradient) to the formal treatment via expansion of the loss function. Covers: training the base learner, learning rate, feature importance, generalization to arbitrary differentiable losses, regularization. Tree shapes in LightGBM, XGBoost, and CatBoost. This time Yandex delivered. (You don't need to understand every formula while studying, e.g., the Taylor series part.)
    Gradient Boost Part 1 (of 4): Regression Main Ideas (Eng)An intuitive explanation of gradient boosting for regression: initial prediction (mean) $\to$ trees on residuals $\to$ scaling with the learning rate.     Gradient Boost Part 2 (of 4): Regression Details (Eng)A step-by-step walkthrough of the gradient boosting algorithm for regression: loss, initialization with the mean, the loop — pseudo-residuals $\to$ tree on residuals $\to$ computing leaf outputs (mean of residuals) $\to$ updating predictions with the learning rate.     Gradient Boost Part 3 (of 4): Classification (Eng)A step-by-step explanation of gradient boosting for binary classification: starting with log-odds, converting to probability via the logistic function, pseudo-residuals $y-p$, building trees on residuals, the formula for leaf values, learning rate, summing trees for the prediction.

    1. What is the essence of gradient boosting?

      An iterative ensemble in which each new tree is trained on the antigradient of the loss function — the direction that reduces the error of the previous ensemble. Each step: $a_T = a_{T-1} + \alpha b_T$, where $b_T$ approximates the antigradient.

    2. How does gradient boosting differ from Random Forest?

      In boosting, trees are built sequentially, each correcting the errors of the previous ones. In Random Forest they are built in parallel, independently of each other. Boosting reduces bias, RF reduces variance. Boosting is more sensitive to hyperparameters and overfitting.

    3. For which data types does GBDT work best?

      Structured tabular data with numerical and categorical features. For images, audio, and raw text it usually loses to neural networks. It is especially strong in credit scoring, anti-fraud, churn prediction, and CTR tasks.

    4. Name the main GBDT hyperparameters and how they affect quality.

      Learning rate (step size): smaller $\to$ more cautious, more trees needed. Number of trees: more $\to$ better, but with a risk of overfitting $\to$ use early stopping. Tree depth: deeper $\to$ more complex dependencies, higher risk of overfitting.

    5. Why is the method called gradient boosting if there is no gradient descent over tree weights?

      The descent is not over parameters but over predictions: the vector of the model's predictions on the training objects is shifted toward the antigradient of the loss function. Since the gradient itself is just a set of numbers for the training objects and can't be computed on new data, boosting approximates it with a tree that can generalize.

    6. Why do people say "trained on residuals", and is it always residuals?

      Only with a squared loss: the antigradient $-\frac{\partial}{\partial a}\frac{1}{2}(a-y)^2$ equals $y-a$, i.e., exactly the residual. For MAE the antigradient is the sign of the error; for log-loss, the difference between the label and the predicted probability. That is precisely why boosting works with any differentiable loss function.

    7. How does boosting's behavior as the number of trees grows differ from Random Forest?

      In a forest, trees are independent and averaged, so quality reaches a plateau and doesn't degrade. In boosting, each tree is added to the prediction and keeps reducing the training error, including by fitting the noise: the validation error first falls and then rises. That's why the number of iterations is determined by early stopping rather than set at random.

    8. Why does boosting remain the best choice for tabular data?

      Tabular features are heterogeneous in meaning and scale, and there is no spatial or temporal structure between them that convolutions or attention could exploit. Threshold splits naturally describe such dependencies, almost no preprocessing is needed, and the method works well on just thousands of rows. On images, audio, and text, where structure exists, it loses to neural networks.

  48. XGBoost, CatBoost, LightGBM↗

    XGBoost, CatBoost, and LightGBM are gradient boosting models.

    All three implement the same idea — building trees sequentially, each one correcting the errors of the previous ones. They differ in engineering decisions: how to search for splits, how to grow a tree, how to handle categories. It's exactly these details that determine which library is more convenient for a specific task.

    In the simplest terms: they are three different builds of the same engine. The algorithm is shared; what differs is the tuning for different conditions — speed, categorical features, robustness to overfitting.

    Before arguing about which one to choose, it's worth establishing a few facts:

    • The quality difference between them is usually small — a few percent, and often fractions of a percent. Features, validation, and tuning learning_rate together with the number of trees have a much bigger impact on the result.
    • All three support missing values natively and don't require imputation beforehand.
    • All three require early stopping. Without it any boosting will overfit, no matter how much regularization you set up.
    • None of them need feature scaling — they're based on trees.

    CatBoost stands out from other gradient boosting algorithms thanks to a number of key features:

    • Handles categorical data out of the box: it doesn't require preprocessing of categorical data, which makes analysts' lives much easier.
    • Minimized overfitting: it's harder to overfit than other algorithms.
    • High speed and efficiency: works well with large datasets.
    CharacteristicLightGBMXGBoostCatBoost
    Tree structureLeaf-wise (by the best leaf score)Level-wise (level by level, symmetric)Symmetric trees (the same predicates at each level)
    RegularizationDepth limit, feature fraction$L_1$/$L_2$ regularization, depth limitSymmetric structure + ordered boosting
    Training speedVery high, leaf-wise growthHigh, GPU supportModerate, due to ordered boosting
    OverfittingProne to it when grown too deepModerately robustLeast prone to overfitting
    FeaturesGrows fast, requires careful tuningA good trade-off between speed and accuracyBest handling of categorical features

    XGBoost uses a strategy of growing trees level by level, while LightGBM takes a leaf-wise growth approach.

    • Level-wise (XGBoost): trees grow level by level. This provides better control over overfitting, since each level balances the tree.
    • Leaf-wise (LightGBM): the focus is on growing the most promising leaves, which often results in deeper trees but can lead to overfitting on small datasets.

    Leaf-wise growth tends to find patterns in the features faster. When working with smaller and interpretable datasets, XGBoost's level-wise growth strategy may be more convenient and less prone to overfitting.

    If you need to state the essence of the differences in one block, it comes down to three points:

    • How the tree grows. LightGBM uses leaf-wise growth: at each step it splits the leaf that gives the largest gain, so the tree ends up asymmetric and deep. XGBoost grows level-wise, level by level. Leaf-wise finds strong patterns faster but overfits more easily on small data — it must be constrained via num_leaves, not just depth.
    • How splits are found. LightGBM uses GOSS (keeps objects with large gradients) and EFB (bundles mutually exclusive sparse features) — hence its speed on wide datasets. XGBoost and CatBoost use histogram-based search.
    • How categories are handled. CatBoost does it natively using ordered target statistics: for each object, the category is encoded using target statistics computed only on the preceding objects in a random permutation. This is exactly the technique that protects against the leakage typical of naive target encoding.
    • Many high-cardinality categorical features (cities, products, identifiers) — CatBoost. It spares you manual encoding and the leakage that comes with it.
    • Very large datasets where training speed matters — LightGBM.
    • You need a predictable, well-documented baseline with a rich ecosystem — XGBoost.
    • You don't want to pull in dependencies — HistGradientBoosting from sklearn.

    A sensible strategy: pick one of them, carry the pipeline through to the end, and only then, if needed, compare with the others on your own validation.

    • Training without early stopping. The number of trees isn't something to fix at random: it's tuned on validation.
    • Tuning learning_rate and the number of trees separately. They're inversely related: halve the step, and you'll need roughly twice as many trees.
    • Constraining LightGBM only by depth. With leaf-wise growth the key parameter is num_leaves, and its default value is too large for small datasets.
    • Feeding CatBoost already-encoded categories. Then the whole point of its built-in handling is lost — categorical columns should be passed as is, specifying cat_features.
    • Trusting built-in feature importance. It's biased toward features with many unique values; for conclusions use SHAP or permutation importance.

    Practice problem on the platform

    Practice problem on the platform

    Practice problem on the platform

    1. How does CatBoost differ from LightGBM and XGBoost?

      CatBoost handles categorical features natively without preprocessing and is less prone to overfitting thanks to ordered boosting and symmetric trees. LightGBM is the fastest (leaf-wise growth). XGBoost is a well-studied, level-wise, reliable baseline.

    2. What's the difference between leaf-wise and level-wise tree growth?

      Leaf-wise (LightGBM): grows the most promising leaf, the tree deepens quickly — higher risk of overfitting, better quality on large data. Level-wise (XGBoost): grows evenly level by level — more balanced, more reliable on small data.

    3. When should you choose LightGBM, and when CatBoost?

      LightGBM — large data, speed matters, numerical features. CatBoost — many categorical features, no time for preprocessing, robustness to overfitting matters.

    4. What is ordered boosting in CatBoost?

      A technique to fight target leakage when working with categorical features: predictions are built only on "historical" examples (those before the current object in the sort order). It prevents overfitting on categorical features.

    5. Why must LightGBM with leaf-wise growth be constrained by num_leaves and not just by depth?

      With leaf-wise growth the tree is asymmetric: the leaf giving the largest gain is split, so one branch can go very deep while others stay short. In such a scheme depth poorly reflects model complexity, whereas the number of leaves reflects it directly. The default num_leaves value is too large for small datasets and leads to overfitting.

    6. What are ordered target statistics in CatBoost and what problem do they solve?

      A category is encoded with target statistics computed only on the objects preceding the current one in a random permutation. This eliminates the leakage inherent in naive target encoding, where an object's own target participates in forming its own feature and leads to overfitting on high-cardinality categorical features.

    7. How much does the choice among the three libraries affect the final quality?

      Usually only slightly — the difference is a few percent, and often a fraction of a percent. Features, correct validation, and tuning learning_rate together with the number of trees affect the result much more than the choice of a specific implementation.

  49. Stacking, blending↗

    Stacking is an ensembling algorithm whose main differences from bagging and boosting are the following:

    • It can use algorithms of different types, not just from some fixed family.
    • The outputs of the base algorithms are combined by a trainable meta-model rather than by some standard aggregation method (summing or averaging).

    In the simplest terms: instead of averaging the experts' opinions, we hire one more expert who learns how to weigh them. The meta-model looks at which base algorithms are usually right on which objects and combines their predictions accordingly.

    An ensemble gains exactly as much as its members err differently. If all models err on the same objects, there's nothing to average out.

    That's why stacking takes algorithms from different families: they err in different places because they're built differently.

    • Gradient boosting excels at capturing complex feature interactions but extrapolates poorly beyond the training data.
    • A linear model extrapolates beautifully but doesn't see nonlinearities.
    • kNN is good where local structure matters and weak in sparse regions.

    The meta-model learns whom to trust in which situation, and that's its advantage over simple averaging: each member's weight isn't fixed but depends on the object.

    For instance, the base algorithms can be nearest neighbors, linear regression, or even neural networks.

    Training a stacking ensemble proceeds in several stages:

    1. The full dataset is split into training and test sets.
    2. The training set is divided into $n$ folds. These folds are then iterated over the same way as in cross-validation: at each step, $(n-1)$ folds are fixed for training the base algorithms and one for their predictions (computing meta-features). This approach lets us use the entire training set while keeping the base algorithms from overfitting.
