    3. The meta-model is trained on the resulting meta-features. Besides meta-features, it can also take features from the original dataset as input. The choice depends on the problem.

    If there's plenty of data, you can simply split the training data into two non-overlapping parts: one on which the base algorithms are trained, and one on which they make their predictions and the meta-model is trained. Using this simple split instead of cross-validation on the training data is sometimes called blending. If there's a lot of data, the test set can also be split into two parts: test and validation, and the latter used to tune the hyperparameters of the member models.

    This is the most important detail of stacking, and getting it wrong invalidates the whole construction.

    Imagine the base models are trained on the entire training set and then produce predictions for the meta-model on that same set. What will the meta-model see? That the overfit boosting model predicts perfectly — after all, it memorized these objects. It will draw the only logical conclusion: trust only the boosting model, with a weight of about one.

    On new data, the boosting model is far from perfect, but the meta-model doesn't know that, and the whole ensemble performs worse than the boosting model alone.

    That's why meta-features are built out-of-fold: the prediction for an object is made by the copy of the base model that didn't see this object. Then the meta-model observes an honest picture of each member's quality — exactly what it will be in production.

    • The meta-model should be simple. Usually logistic or linear regression, sometimes regularized. It has few features (one per base model) and little data, so a complex meta-model will overfit instantly. A powerful boosting model on top of boosting models is a typical mistake.
    • The gain is usually modest. In practice, stacking adds fractions of a percent over the best single model. In competitions that decides the outcome; in production it almost never pays for the complexity.
    • Cost grows multiplicatively. You have to train and maintain all the base models plus the meta-model, and prediction time is the sum of all members' times.

    Practical takeaway: first squeeze the most out of a single well-tuned boosting model, and only then think about stacking.

    1. What is stacking and how does it differ from bagging and boosting?

      Stacking combines models of different types (KNN, linear regression, neural networks) via a trainable meta-model rather than averaging. Bagging/boosting use a single model family with a fixed aggregation rule.

    2. How is stacking trained, and why does it need cross-validation inside?

      The training set is divided into $n$ folds. Base models are trained on $n-1$ folds and predict on the remaining one — this is how meta-features are built without data leakage. The meta-model is trained on these predictions.

    3. What is blending and how does it differ from stacking?

      Blending is simplified stacking: the dataset is split into two parts, base models are trained on the first and predict on the second, and those predictions are used to train the meta-model. No cross-validation — simpler, but requires more data.

    4. What is the role of the meta-model in stacking?

      The meta-model learns to combine the base algorithms' predictions, discovering which of them are strong on different subsets of the data. It's usually simple (logistic or linear regression) so as not to overfit on the small set of meta-features.

    5. Why must meta-features be built out-of-fold?

      If base models predict on the same data they were trained on, an overfit model will show nearly perfect predictions, and the meta-model will learn to trust only it. On new data this model performs worse, but the meta-model doesn't know that — and the ensemble ends up worse than a single model. The out-of-fold scheme shows the meta-model each member's honest quality.

    6. Why does stacking use models of different types?

      An ensemble gains as much as its members err differently. Models from different families err in different places: boosting captures interactions but extrapolates poorly; a linear model extrapolates but doesn't see nonlinearities; kNN is strong in dense regions. The meta-model learns whom to trust in which situation.

    7. Why should the meta-model be simple?

      It has few features (one per base model) and little training data, so a complex meta-model overfits on the meta-features almost instantly. Logistic or linear regression with regularization is the usual choice.

  50. HistGradientBoosting

    HistGradientBoosting is sklearn's implementation of gradient boosting that builds trees from feature histograms. Instead of iterating over all unique values of a feature, the algorithm splits the values into intervals, or bins, in advance and searches for splits over them.

    In the simplest terms: instead of "let's check all 500,000 possible income thresholds", the algorithm says "let's round income into 255 buckets and check 255 thresholds". Split accuracy barely suffers, and the amount of work drops by thousands of times.

    The "income" feature on a dataset of 1M customers has, say, 400,000 unique values.

    • Classic boosting, to find the best split on this feature, has to sort the values and try every possible threshold — on the order of 400,000 candidates, and it does this in every node of every tree.
    • Histogram-based boosting distributes the values into 255 bins once, before training. Now every node has exactly 254 candidates, and the feature itself is stored not as float64 but as uint8 — 8 times less memory.

    A natural question: won't we lose quality by coarsening the feature? In practice, hardly at all. The splits "income > 87,431" and "income > 87,500" separate almost the same objects, and the extra precision of the threshold is overfitting to the particular sample anyway. Discretization here also works as mild regularization.

    In ordinary boosting, finding the best split can be expensive: many possible thresholds have to be considered. The histogram-based approach first discretizes numerical features:

    $$ x_j \rightarrow \text{bin}(x_j). $$

    After that, the tree searches for a split not among all values but among a limited number of bins. This speeds up training and reduces memory consumption.

    sklearn offers:

    • HistGradientBoostingClassifier;
    • HistGradientBoostingRegressor.

    In modern versions of sklearn they support early stopping, monotonic constraints, and handling of missing values and categorical features.

    HistGradientBoosting is convenient as a strong sklearn baseline: it is well integrated into the sklearn API and pipelines. But XGBoost, LightGBM, and CatBoost often offer more options for production tuning, GPUs, categorical features, custom loss functions, and large datasets.

    1. What is histogram-based gradient boosting?

      It is gradient boosting in which continuous features are first split into bins, and trees search for splits over these bins rather than over all the original values.

    2. Why is HistGradientBoosting faster than classic GradientBoosting?

      Because it tries fewer split candidates and stores a more compact feature representation. This is especially noticeable on large tabular data.

    3. How does HistGradientBoosting handle missing values?

      The algorithm can account for missing values when building splits and choose which branch to send NaN to, instead of requiring imputation beforehand.

    4. How does it differ from XGBoost/LightGBM/CatBoost?

      It is an sklearn implementation with good integration into sklearn pipelines. The specialized libraries usually have richer settings and are often stronger on large production tasks.

    5. When should you choose HistGradientBoosting?

      When you need a strong tabular baseline, the sklearn API, a quick start, and fewer external dependencies. For maximum quality, compare it with XGBoost/LightGBM/CatBoost.

    6. Isn't quality lost by coarsening features into bins?

      In practice, hardly at all. Neighboring thresholds (e.g., "income > 87,431" and "income > 87,500") separate practically the same objects, and excessive threshold precision is overfitting to the particular sample. Discretization acts as mild extra regularization, while the feature is stored as uint8 instead of float64 — 8 times less memory.

    7. How many bins are used by default, and why that number?

      255 (the max_bins parameter), plus a separate bin for missing values. That is the maximum that fits into one byte, which is what gives the compact uint8 representation. Reducing the number of bins speeds up training and strengthens regularization; increasing it hardly improves quality.

    51. Block 5

  51. Clustering

    Clustering is an unsupervised learning task that consists of partitioning objects into groups (clusters) based on their similarity. The main goal is for objects within the same cluster to be as similar as possible, and for clusters to differ from one another.

    In the simplest terms: you are handed a box of parts and asked to sort them into piles without being told which piles should result. No labels, no "correct answer" — only the objects themselves and your notion of what "similar" means.

    Formally, let a sample $X = \{x_1, x_2, \dots, x_n\} \subset \mathbb{R}^d$ be given. Our goal is to partition the sample into $k$ clusters $C_1, \dots, C_k$ so that elements within a cluster are "similar" and elements of different clusters are "different".

    • Customer segmentation. Split the customer base into groups by behavior to make different offers. Nobody knows in advance how many groups there are or what they look like.
    • Compression and quantization. Reducing an image's million colors to 16 representative ones is KMeans over pixels.
    • Anomaly detection. Objects that do not fall into any dense cluster are anomaly candidates.
    • A starting point for labeling. Cluster unlabeled data, show an expert a few representatives of each group, and get labels at a reasonable cost.

    This is the fundamental difference from classification, and it causes almost all the difficulties:

    • No ground truth to compare against. There is nothing to compute accuracy against, so quality is assessed indirectly — through the geometry of the partition (silhouette, Calinski–Harabasz) or through usefulness for a downstream task.
    • Different algorithms give different and equally "correct" answers. KMeans finds spherical groups, DBSCAN finds dense regions of arbitrary shape, hierarchical clustering finds a nested structure. Arguing about which one is right is pointless: they answer different questions.
    • The result depends entirely on the distance metric and scaling. Change the scale of one feature and you get a different partition. That is exactly why features are almost always normalized before clustering.
    • Clusters will always be found. An algorithm will split even uniform noise into groups. Before interpreting the result, make sure there is actually structure in the data.

    How do we define the "similarity" of clusters? Intuitively, we need some way to measure the distance between clusters. There are several popular ways to do this, which generalize into a single formula: suppose we want to measure the distance $R(W,S)$ between the clusters

    $$\underbrace{W=U\cup V}_{\text{union of clusters } U, V}$$

    and

    $$\underbrace{S}_{\text{cluster}},$$

    knowing the distances $R(U,S)$, $R(V,S)$, $R(U,V)$ (these are easy to compute, e.g., with the Euclidean metric). Then we can write the Lance–Williams formula (1967):

    $$ \begin{aligned} R(U\cup V, S) = \underbrace{\alpha_U R(U,S)}_{\text{contribution of the distance between } U \text{ and } S} + \underbrace{\alpha_V R(V,S)}_{\text{contribution of the distance between } V \text{ and } S} + \\ \underbrace{\beta R(U,V)}_{\text{correction for the distance between } U, V} + \gamma |R(U,S)-R(V,S)|, \end{aligned} $$

    where $\alpha_U, \alpha_V, \beta, \gamma$ are numerical parameters that can be chosen.

    Let $\rho(w, s)$ be the distance between point $w$ and point $s$ (e.g., Euclidean distance). In practice, the following metrics are commonly used for clustering:

    • Nearest-neighbor distance (single linkage): $$R(W,S)=\min_{w\in W,\, s\in S} \rho(w, s), \quad \alpha_U=\alpha_V=\tfrac{1}{2},\ \beta=0,\ \gamma=-\tfrac{1}{2}$$

    • Farthest-neighbor distance (complete linkage): $$R(W,S)=\max_{w\in W,\, s\in S} \rho(w, s), \quad \alpha_U=\alpha_V=\tfrac{1}{2},\ \beta=0,\ \gamma=\tfrac{1}{2}$$

    • Group average distance (average linkage): $$R(W,S)=\frac{1}{|W||S|} \sum_{w\in W,\, s\in S} \rho(w, s), \quad \alpha_U=\frac{|U|}{|W|},\ \alpha_V=\frac{|V|}{|W|},\ \beta=\gamma=0$$

    • Distance between centroids (centroid linkage): $$R(W,S)=\rho^2\!\left(\sum_{w\in W}\frac{w}{|W|},\sum_{s\in S}\frac{s}{|S|}\right), \quad \alpha_U=\frac{|U|}{|W|},\ \alpha_V=\frac{|V|}{|W|},\ \beta=-\alpha_U\alpha_V,\ \gamma=0$$

    • Ward's distance (Ward's method): $$R(W,S)=\frac{|S||W|}{|S|+|W|}\rho^2\!\left(\sum_{w\in W}\frac{w}{|W|},\sum_{s\in S}\frac{s}{|S|}\right)$$ $$\alpha_U=\frac{|S|+|U|}{|S|+|W|},\ \alpha_V=\frac{|S|+|V|}{|S|+|W|},\ \beta=-\frac{|S|}{|S|+|W|},\ \gamma=0$$

    You do not need all this formula drudgery, and you definitely do not need to memorize it: it is given here to show that all the distance variants can be generalized by a single formula, with the coefficients $\alpha_U, \alpha_V, \beta, \gamma$ changing depending on the context.

    The choice of these parameters determines how exactly we interpret the "similarity" of clusters — whether we consider them close by their nearest points (single linkage), by their farthest points (complete linkage), by the average distance (average linkage), by their centers of mass, or taking within-cluster variance into account (Ward's method).

    1. What is clustering and how does it differ from classification?

      Clustering is unsupervised learning: partitioning objects into groups by similarity without predefined labels. In classification, labels are known at training time. In clustering there is no correct answer — only criteria for the quality of the partition.

    2. What is the formal goal of clustering?

      To partition the sample $X \subset \mathbb{R}^d$ into $k$ clusters $C_1, \dots, C_k$ so that objects within a cluster are similar (small distance) and objects in different clusters are different (large distance).

    3. What is the Lance–Williams formula and why is it needed?

      A single formula $R(U \cup V, S) = \alpha_U R(U,S) + \alpha_V R(V,S) + \beta R(U,V) + \gamma |R(U,S)-R(V,S)|$ that generalizes all popular inter-cluster distance metrics. Different $\alpha_U, \alpha_V, \beta, \gamma$ yield single/complete/average linkage, centroid linkage, and Ward's method.

    4. How does Ward's method differ from single linkage?

      Single linkage measures distance by the nearest points and tends to produce "chains". Ward's method minimizes the increase in within-cluster variance on merging and yields compact clusters of similar size. In practice, Ward's method more often gives better results.

    5. Why can't you compute accuracy for clustering?

      Accuracy requires true labels, and clustering works without them — there is simply no ground truth to compare against. Quality is assessed either indirectly, through the geometry of the partition (silhouette, Calinski–Harabasz, Davies–Bouldin), or by usefulness for a downstream task.

    6. Why are features almost always normalized before clustering?

      The algorithms rely on distances, and a distance sums the contributions of all features in their own units. A feature with a large spread (income in dollars) will completely overwhelm a feature with a small spread (age in years), and the partition will effectively be based on a single feature.

    7. What does it mean that clusters are always found?

      Any algorithm will split even uniform random noise into groups — it optimizes its objective regardless of whether there is real structure in the data. So the result is checked before interpretation: look at the silhouette, the stability of the partition across runs, and whether the clusters make sense for the task.

  52. Clustering metrics

    Clustering has no ground-truth labels, so there's nothing to compute accuracy or $F_1$ against. We have to evaluate the geometry of the partition itself, and all the metrics below check the same pair of properties:

    • compactness — how close the points within a cluster are to each other;
    • separation — how far apart the clusters are from each other.

    A good partition is one where the first is small and the second is large. The metrics differ only in how exactly they measure this and how they combine it into a single number.

    Such metrics are called internal: they use only the data itself. There are also external metrics (ARI, NMI) — they compare the partition with a known reference labeling, but are applicable only when labels do exist, for example when testing an algorithm on a toy dataset.

    The quality of a clustering can be evaluated in different ways, for example: Silhouette Score, Calinski–Harabasz Index, and Davies–Bouldin Index.

    For each point $x_i$ define:

    • The mean intra-cluster distance: $$\textcolor{blue}{a(i)}=\frac{1}{|C_{x_i}|-1}\sum_{x\in C_{x_i},\,x\neq x_i}\|x_i-x\|$$ — the mean distance from $x_i$ to the other points of its own cluster.
    • The best (minimum) mean distance to another cluster: $$\textcolor{red}{b(i)}=\min_{C\neq C_{x_i}}\frac{1}{|C|}\sum_{x\in C}\|x_i-x\|$$

    Then the silhouette of a point is:

    $$s(i)=\frac{\textcolor{red}{b(i)}-\textcolor{blue}{a(i)}}{\max\{\textcolor{red}{b(i)},\,\textcolor{blue}{a(i)}\}}$$

    The overall silhouette:

    $$S=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}s(i)\in[-1,1]$$

    Interpretation: $S$ closer to $1$ is good (dense and well-separated clusters), around $0$ means the clusters overlap, and negative values mean points are "in the wrong" clusters.

    Note on complexity: requires pairwise distances; naively $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$, batching/approximations are possible for large $n$.

    Let $c$ be the centroid of the whole dataset, $c_j$ the centroid of cluster $j$, and $k$ the total number of clusters. Define the between-cluster and within-cluster dispersions:

    $$ \underbrace{\textcolor{teal}{\mathrm{tr}(B_k)}=\sum_{j=1}^{k} n_j\|c_j-c\|^2}_{\text{between-cluster dispersion}}, \qquad \underbrace{\textcolor{orange}{\mathrm{tr}(W_k)}=\sum_{j=1}^{k}\sum_{x\in C_j}\|x-c_j\|^2}_{\text{within-cluster dispersion}} $$

    The Calinski–Harabász index:

    $$\mathrm{CH}=\frac{\textcolor{teal}{\mathrm{tr}(B_k)}/(k-1)}{\textcolor{orange}{\mathrm{tr}(W_k)}/(n-k)}$$

    Interpretation: the larger $\mathrm{CH}$, the better (larger between-cluster dispersion and smaller within-cluster dispersion). Good for convex clusters; sensitive to feature scaling and the choice of $k$.

    For cluster $i$, let

    $$\textcolor{blue}{s_i}=\frac{1}{|C_i|}\sum_{x\in C_i}\|x-c_i\|$$

    be the mean intra-cluster distance (spread in the $L_2$ sense), and

    $$d_{ij}=\|c_i-c_j\|$$

    the distance between the centroids of clusters $i$ and $j$. Then

    $$R_{ij}=\frac{\textcolor{blue}{s_i}+\textcolor{blue}{s_j}}{d_{ij}},\qquad\mathrm{DB}=\frac{1}{k}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\max_{j\neq i}R_{ij}$$

    Interpretation: lower is better (we want low within-cluster "spread" and large between-cluster distances). Robust to different cluster scales, but sensitive to the distance metric.

    • Silhouette: interpretable range $[-1,1]$, but expensive for large $n$ (pairwise distances).
    • Calinski–Harabász: often works well for convex clusters; higher is better.
    • Davies–Bouldin: minimized; accounts for the "density" of clusters and the separation of centroids.

    In practice it's useful to look at the metrics together and validate across several values of $k$.

    A practical scenario: we don't know how many clusters are in the data. We run KMeans with $k = 2, 3, \ldots, 10$ and compute the mean silhouette for each.

    $k$Mean silhouette
    20.42
    30.59
    40.47
    50.40

    The maximum is at $k = 3$ — that's the one we take. An important advantage over the elbow method: the silhouette has a real maximum, whereas inertia decreases monotonically and you have to eyeball the "elbow", whose position is debatable.

    Even more useful than the mean is the per-object silhouette plot: it shows whether there are clusters in which a significant fraction of points have a negative silhouette. Such clusters are "foreign" even to their own points — a sure sign that $k$ was chosen poorly.

    • They assume convex, compact clusters. Silhouette and CH are based on distances to centers and within groups, so on nested rings or elongated arcs they will underrate a good DBSCAN partition and overrate a meaningless KMeans partition. You can't use them to compare algorithms with different geometry.
    • They know nothing about meaning. A partition can be geometrically perfect and completely useless for the business. The final criterion is the interpretability of the clusters and their usefulness in the downstream task.
    • They depend on scaling. Change the scale of a feature, and you change both the partition and the metric. Comparing values obtained with different preprocessing is meaningless.
    • Silhouette is expensive. Naively $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ in distances; on large datasets it is computed on a subsample.

    1. How is the Silhouette Score interpreted?

      $S \in [-1, 1]$: closer to 1 means the clusters are dense and well separated, around 0 means they overlap, negative means points ended up in the wrong cluster. Formula: $s(i) = (b(i) - a(i)) / \max(a(i), b(i))$, where $a(i)$ is the mean distance within the cluster and $b(i)$ is the mean distance to the nearest other cluster.

    2. What does the Calinski–Harabász Index measure and how is it interpreted?

      CH = between-cluster dispersion / within-cluster dispersion (normalized by $k-1$ and $n-k$). Higher is better: compact clusters with centroids far apart. Works well for convex clusters.

    3. What does the Davies–Bouldin Index minimize?

      DB = the mean of $\max_{j \neq i} (s_i + s_j) / d_{ij}$ over all clusters $i$. Lower is better: we look for clusters with small internal "spread" and large distances between centroids. More robust to different scales than CH.

    4. Why does clustering need special metrics rather than accuracy?

      Accuracy requires ground-truth class labels — clustering is unsupervised, so there are no labels. Silhouette, CH, and DB measure geometric properties of the partition: the compactness and separation of clusters, without comparing to a reference.

    5. How do internal clustering metrics differ from external ones?

      Internal metrics (Silhouette, Calinski–Harabasz, Davies–Bouldin) use only the data itself and evaluate the geometry of the partition — compactness and separation. External metrics (ARI, NMI) compare the partition with a known reference labeling and are applicable only when labels exist, for example when testing an algorithm on a toy dataset.

    6. How does the silhouette help choose the number of clusters, and why is it better than the elbow method?

      Compute the mean silhouette for several values of $k$ and take the maximum. Unlike inertia, which decreases monotonically and requires a subjective search for the "elbow", the silhouette has a real maximum. The per-object silhouette plot is even more informative: it reveals clusters with many points having a negative silhouette.

    7. Why can't these metrics be used to compare KMeans and DBSCAN?

      Silhouette and CH are built on distances to centers and assume convex, compact clusters. On data like nested rings, they will underrate the semantically correct DBSCAN partition and overrate the geometrically "tidy" but meaningless KMeans partition.

  53. KMeans

    KMeans is an algorithm that partitions data into $k$ clusters by minimizing within-cluster distance (usually the sum of squared distances to the cluster centers).

    In the simplest terms: place $k$ flags and repeatedly move each one to the middle of whatever has gathered around it. Each point is assigned to its nearest flag, then each flag moves to the center of mass of its group, and so on around the loop until the flags stop moving.

    The K-means algorithm minimizes the total within-cluster distance:

    $$\min_{C_1,\dots,C_k} \sum_{i=1}^k \sum_{x \in C_i} \|x - \mu_i\|^2$$

    where $\mu_i$ is the center of cluster $C_i$:

    $$\mu_i = \frac{1}{|C_i|} \sum_{x \in C_i} x$$

    Algorithm:

    • Initialize $k$ centers $\mu_1, \dots, \mu_k$ (randomly or via KMeans++).
    • Repeat until convergence:
      • Assign each point $x_j$ to its nearest center $\mu_i$.
      • Update the cluster centers using the formula above.

    Each of the two steps is guaranteed not to increase the objective: reassigning a point to a closer center reduces its contribution, and the mean is exactly the point that minimizes the sum of squared distances within a cluster. The objective decreases monotonically and is bounded below by zero, so the algorithm always converges in a finite number of steps. But it converges to a local minimum, not the global one: the problem is non-convex, and which minimum you reach depends on the starting point.

    It's often said that KMeans "assumes spherical clusters of equal size." This isn't an arbitrary requirement but a direct consequence of the objective.

    • A point belongs to the center with the smaller Euclidean distance. The set of points equidistant from two centers is a line (in general, a hyperplane). So KMeans cluster boundaries are always straight, and the clusters themselves are convex polytopes.
    • Minimizing the sum of squares makes the algorithm sensitive to distant points and inclined to split the data into groups of comparable "radius."

    Hence the typical failures: nested rings (boundaries aren't straight), tilted elongated ellipses, clusters of very different density and size. For such data, use DBSCAN, spectral clustering, or Gaussian mixtures (GMM), where a cluster's covariance can be arbitrary.

    The algorithm requires $k$ upfront, but the data doesn't tell you what it is. Standard approaches:

    • Elbow method. Plot inertia (the value of the objective) against $k$. It decreases monotonically — at $k = n$ it drops to zero — so you look not for a minimum but for a kink: the point after which adding a cluster barely improves the result.
    • Silhouette. For each point, compute how much closer it is to its own cluster than to the neighboring one; the value is averaged over the dataset and lies in $[-1, 1]$. Choose the $k$ with the highest silhouette. Unlike the elbow, the silhouette has a real maximum rather than a subjective "kink."
    • Problem context. Often $k$ is dictated by the business: you need exactly 5 customer segments because marketing is ready to run five different campaigns.
    • Forgetting to scale. KMeans computes Euclidean distances, so a feature with a large spread (income in rubles) completely overwhelms one with a small spread (age). Without StandardScaler, the clusters will be driven by a single feature.
    • Applying it to categorical features after one-hot. The mean of binary columns yields fractional "half-categories," and the Euclidean distance between one-hot vectors is nearly uninformative. For mixed data, use k-prototypes or other metrics.
    • Treating inertia as a quality metric across different $k$. It always decreases as $k$ grows, so comparing partitions with different numbers of clusters by inertia is meaningless — that's what the silhouette is for.
    • Running it only once. A single run can land in a bad local minimum; n_init=10 and init="k-means++" are the bare minimum.

    1. What does KMeans minimize, and what does the objective look like?

      KMeans minimizes the total within-cluster distance: $\sum_{i=1}^k \sum_{x \in C_i} \|x - \mu_i\|^2$, where $\mu_i$ is the cluster center. It's the sum of squared distances from each point to the center of its cluster.

    2. Describe the KMeans algorithm step by step.

      1. Initialize $k$ centers (randomly or via KMeans++). 2. Assign each point to its nearest center. 3. Recompute the centers as cluster means. 4. Repeat until convergence (assignments stop changing).

    3. What are the main limitations of KMeans?

      The number of clusters $k$ must be set in advance. It's sensitive to initialization and outliers. It assumes spherical clusters of equal size. It works poorly on data with non-Euclidean geometry or irregularly shaped clusters.

    4. What is KMeans used for in ML beyond clustering?

      Feature quantization (Bag of Visual Words in CV), centroid initialization for other methods, data compression, preprocessing before training. It's also used in VQ-VAE for discrete encoding of the latent space.

    5. Why does KMeans always converge, and why not to the global minimum?

      Neither step increases the objective: reassigning a point to its nearest center reduces its contribution, and the mean minimizes the sum of squares within a cluster. The function decreases monotonically and is bounded below, so the algorithm stops in a finite number of steps. But the problem is non-convex with many local minima, and which one is found is determined by initialization.

    6. Why is KMeans said to assume spherical clusters?

      A point belongs to the nearest center by Euclidean distance, so the boundary between clusters is always straight (a hyperplane), and the clusters are convex and of comparable radius. Nested rings, tilted elongated structures, and clusters of very different density can't be described by such a model — they call for DBSCAN, spectral clustering, or GMM.

    7. How is the number of clusters $k$ chosen?

      The elbow method (look for the kink in the plot of inertia vs. $k$, since inertia itself decreases monotonically and has no minimum), the silhouette coefficient (which has a real maximum), or direct problem requirements. You can't compare partitions with different $k$ by inertia — it's always lower for larger $k$.

  54. KMedoids

    K-Medoids is a variant of K-Means in which the cluster center is an actual object from the dataset (the medoid) rather than the arithmetic mean of its points. The medoid is the object in the cluster with the minimal total distance to all other objects in the cluster:

    $$m_C = \arg\min_{x \in C} \sum_{y \in C} d(x, y)$$

    In the simplest terms: K-Means looks for the "center of mass," while K-Medoids picks the "most typical representative." The center of mass is a point that may not exist in the data; the typical representative is always a specific row of your dataset.

    A cluster of five customers with incomes (thousand rubles): $50, 55, 60, 65, 3000$. The last one is a millionaire who got into the sample by mistake or is just a rare case.

    • K-Means: center = mean = $(50+55+60+65+3000)/5 = 646$. No such customer exists: the center of a "typical" cluster has drifted six times higher than any normal member, and on the next iteration completely different people will start being pulled toward it.
    • K-Medoids: try all five as candidates and take the one with the minimal sum of distances to the others. That's the customer with income $60$ (sum $\approx 2955$), not the millionaire (sum $\approx 11\,770$). The outlier stays in the cluster but can't determine its position.

    The reason lies in the formula itself: the mean minimizes the sum of squared deviations, so a distant point influences it quadratically. The medoid minimizes the sum of plain distances, where a single outlier is just one large term among many.

    K-Medoids works with any distance metric, not just Euclidean — a matrix of pairwise distances is enough. This opens up problems where a "mean" isn't even defined:

    • clustering texts by cosine distance or strings by Levenshtein distance (the average of two words doesn't exist);
    • categorical data with Jaccard distance;
    • precomputed distance matrices where you simply don't have the original coordinates.

    K-Means is inapplicable in such problems: it must be able to average coordinates.

    • Computational cost. To find the medoid, you have to try every candidate within the cluster and compute sums of distances: $O(k(n-k)^2)$ per iteration for the classic PAM algorithm versus $O(nkd)$ for K-Means. On datasets beyond tens of thousands of objects, approximate versions are used — CLARA (works on subsamples) or CLARANS.
    • Memory for the distance matrix. If the metric is non-standard, the $n \times n$ pairwise distance matrix has to be stored in full: at $n = 50\,000$ that's about 10 GB.
    • The same limitations on cluster shape. Like K-Means, the method gravitates toward compact "ball-shaped" groups and requires $k$ to be set in advance. Robustness to outliers is the only thing it changes; it doesn't fix cluster geometry.

    1. How does KMedoids differ from KMeans?

      KMeans uses the mean of all points in a cluster as the centroid — which may be a point outside the dataset. KMedoids picks the centroid (medoid) as an actual point from the data. This makes the algorithm more robust to outliers.

    2. Why is KMedoids more robust to outliers?

      The mean (the KMeans centroid) is "pulled" toward outliers — a single anomalous point shifts the cluster center. The medoid is an actual point chosen to minimize the total distance to all points in the cluster — an outlier can't become the medoid.

    3. What is the downside of KMedoids compared to KMeans?

      It's computationally more expensive: at each iteration, every point in the cluster has to be tried as a medoid candidate. Complexity $O(k \cdot n^2)$ versus $O(k \cdot n \cdot d)$ for KMeans. For large datasets this is noticeable.

    4. Why is the mean sensitive to outliers but the medoid isn't?

      The mean minimizes the sum of squared deviations, so a distant point influences it quadratically and drags the center along. The medoid minimizes the sum of plain distances and must be an actual object from the dataset: an outlier becomes just one large term and can't become the center as long as there's a dense group of points nearby.

    5. What advantage does K-Medoids offer besides robustness to outliers?

      It works with an arbitrary metric — a matrix of pairwise distances is enough. This lets you cluster texts by Levenshtein distance, categorical data by Jaccard, or work with a precomputed distance matrix where a "mean" isn't defined in principle. K-Means requires the ability to average coordinates and is inapplicable in such problems.

  55. KMeans++

    K-Means++ is a way to choose the initial centroids for K-Means so that they are far apart from the very beginning. The first center is picked at random, and each subsequent one is also picked at random, but with probability proportional to the squared distance to the nearest already chosen center:

    $$P(x) = \frac{D(x)^2}{\sum_{x' \in X} D(x')^2}$$

    where $D(x)$ is the distance from point $x$ to the nearest of the already chosen centers.

    In the simplest terms: the farther a point is from everything we have already chosen, the higher its chance of becoming the next center. This is not a greedy pick of the farthest point (that role is often played by an outlier) but a lottery in which distant points get more tickets.

    One of the key questions when using K-Means is the choice of initial centroids, since both the quality and the convergence speed of the algorithm depend on them. There are several ways to initialize centroids:

    • Random selection: $k$ random points from the data are chosen as initial centroids. This method is simple but can lead to poor results.
    • K-Means++: the first centroid is chosen at random, and then the remaining centroids are chosen with probability proportional to the squared distance to the nearest already chosen centroid. It requires extra computation.
    • Greedy K-Means++ — a modification of K-Means++ that speeds up convergence and improves clustering quality: at each step, when choosing a cluster center, several attempts are made and the best one is chosen (the one that minimizes the total squared deviation of points from the cluster centers).

    K-Means minimizes the sum of squared distances to the centers, and this problem is non-convex: it has many local minima, and the algorithm is only guaranteed to converge to the nearest one. Where it ends up is determined by the starting point.

    The classic failure of random initialization: the data forms three dense, widely separated clouds, and two initial centers happen to land in the same cloud. The algorithm will then dutifully split that cloud in half and merge the other two clouds into a single cluster — and stop, because further iterations improve nothing. The partition is stable but meaningless.

    K-Means++ targets exactly this problem: after the first center is chosen, all points in distant clouds get huge $D(x)^2$, and the chance that the next center lands near the first one becomes negligible. Formally this gives a guarantee: the expected quality of the partition is at most $O(\log k)$ times worse than the optimum — even before the first iteration of K-Means itself.

    Suppose the first center has been chosen, and the distances from four points to it are $1, 2, 3, 10$.

    • With selection proportional to distance, the odds would be $1:2:3:10$ — the far point gets 62%.
    • With selection proportional to squared distance, the odds become $1:4:9:100$ — the far point now gets 88%.

    It is the squaring that sharply shifts the choice toward remote regions: nearby points almost stop competing. At the same time, the choice remains random, so a lone outlier at distance 100 is not automatically made a center — it just gets many "tickets", but it will lose if there is a whole cloud of points nearby with comparable distances.

    • K-Means++ is only a starting point, not a guarantee. It makes a bad result unlikely but not impossible. That is why sklearn defaults to init="k-means++" and n_init=10: the algorithm is run several times and the partition with the lowest inertia is kept.
    • Initialization takes time. It requires $k$ passes over the data to recompute distances. On large datasets this is noticeable, which is why k-means|| exists — a parallel version that picks centers in batches.
    • It does not solve the "wrong $k$" problem. If there are actually five clusters and you ask for three, no initialization will save you — choosing $k$ is a separate topic (elbow method, silhouette).

    1. Why is centroid initialization important in KMeans?

      The initial centroids determine which local minimum the algorithm converges to. Random initialization can produce a poor partition. KMeans++ reduces this sensitivity by choosing well-spread initial centers.

    2. How does KMeans++ initialization work?

      1. The first centroid is a random point. 2. Each subsequent one is chosen with probability proportional to $d^2$ — the squared distance to the nearest already chosen centroid. This spreads the centers across the data.

    3. What is the idea behind Greedy KMeans++?

      At each step, several attempts are made to choose the next centroid, and the one that minimizes the total squared deviation is kept. This increases the computational cost of initialization but gives a better start and faster convergence.

    4. Why is the probability proportional to the squared distance rather than the distance itself?

      Squaring sharply amplifies the difference: with distances $1, 2, 3, 10$, the far point's odds rise from 62% to 88%. This reliably pushes the next center into a region not yet covered. At the same time, the choice remains random, so a lone outlier does not automatically become a center, as it would with a greedy "take the farthest point".

    5. Does K-Means++ provide quality guarantees, and why use n_init > 1 then?

      Yes, K-Means++ guarantees that the expected value of the objective is at most $O(\log k)$ times worse than the optimum. But this is an average-case bound, not one for a specific run, so in practice the algorithm is still run several times (n_init=10 in sklearn) and the partition with the lowest inertia is chosen.

  56. DBSCAN

    DBSCAN is an algorithm that finds clusters of arbitrary shape based on point density. It automatically excludes outliers.

    In the simplest terms: a cluster is whatever "sticks together". The algorithm walks through the points and asks each one: "do you have many neighbors nearby?" If so, the point is inside a dense region and all its neighbors join the same cluster; the cluster grows in a chain until the density runs out. Points with few neighbors and no dense region nearby are declared noise.

    The classic test is two nested rings of points (a "target") or two interleaved arcs ("half-moons").

    • KMeans looks for spherical clusters around centers, so it cuts the target in half with a vertical line: half of the outer ring goes with half of the inner one. Formally the objective is minimized, but substantively it is nonsense, since the center of the outer ring coincides with the center of the inner one.
    • DBSCAN follows density: the points of the outer ring are connected to each other by a chain of neighbors but separated from the inner ring by an empty gap wider than $\varepsilon$. The rings are recognized as two distinct clusters — their shape does not matter to the algorithm.

    That is exactly why DBSCAN is used for geodata (roads, clusters of buildings), trajectories, and any elongated structures: there, "cluster center" is a useless concept.

    Formally, a cluster is defined as a region with a high density of points.

    Parameters:

    • $\varepsilon$ — the neighbor search radius
    • $\text{minPts}$ — the minimum number of points in a neighborhood

    Definitions:

    • A point $p$ is a core point if $$|\{q : \|p - q\| \leq \varepsilon\}| \geq \text{minPts}$$ that is, if you center a ball at $p$, its neighborhood of radius $\varepsilon$ contains at least $\text{minPts}$ points $q$.
    • A point $q$ is directly reachable from $p$ if $p$ is a core point and $\|p - q\| \leq \varepsilon$.
    • A point $q$ is density-reachable from $p$ if there is a chain $p = p_1, p_2, \dots, p_n = q$ in which each $p_{i+1}$ is directly reachable from $p_i$. All points of the chain, except possibly the last one, are core points.
    • A cluster is a maximal set of points in which any two are connected through some core point: both are density-reachable from the same core point.

    The $\varepsilon$-neighborhood of a single core point is a small circle, and it will not cover a whole cluster of arbitrary shape. Take a ring: a circle around any of its points captures only a short arc.

    A cluster is assembled as a chain: from a core point to a neighboring core point in its neighborhood, from that one to the next, and so on around the whole ring, like falling dominoes. Each step is no longer than $\varepsilon$, but the chain itself can be arbitrarily long and winding. So reachability is a path, not a single jump, and that is exactly what lets DBSCAN find clusters of any shape.

    DBSCAN splits all points into three categories, which makes its behavior predictable:

    • Core — at least $\text{minPts}$ points in the $\varepsilon$-neighborhood. This is the "body" of the cluster, from which it grows.
    • Border — fewer neighbors than the threshold, but the point falls within the neighborhood of some core point. It is included in the cluster but does not extend it further.
    • Noise — neither of the above. Such points do not belong to any cluster, and this is an expected result, not a failure.

    The ability to honestly say "this point does not belong to anything" is a fundamental difference from KMeans, where every point must be assigned to the nearest center, even if it sits in the middle of nowhere.

    Both parameters look abstract, but they are chosen by clear rules:

    • $\text{minPts}$ defines what counts as "density". A rule of thumb is $\text{minPts} \geq d + 1$, more often $2d$, where $d$ is the number of features. The larger the value, the stricter the algorithm and the more points end up as noise.
    • $\varepsilon$ is chosen using the k-distance plot: for each point, compute the distance to its $k$-th neighbor (where $k = \text{minPts} - 1$), sort these distances in ascending order, and plot them. The plot shows an "elbow" — a sharp bend: before it, distances grow slowly (points inside dense regions); after it, they jump (points in empty space). The value at the bend is taken as $\varepsilon$.

    The logic is the same as the elbow method in KMeans: we look for the scale at which the data naturally splits into "dense" and "sparse".

    It is suitable for clustering data with noise or poorly defined boundaries, for example, geodata or images.

    • Clusters of different density. DBSCAN has a single global $\varepsilon$ for the entire dataset. If one group of points is dense and another is sparse, no single radius works: with a small $\varepsilon$ the sparse cluster goes entirely to noise, with a large one the dense clusters merge into one. This is exactly the problem solved by HDBSCAN, which works with a hierarchy of densities.
    • Curse of dimensionality. The method relies on distances, and in high-dimensional spaces all distances become nearly equal, so the notion of a "dense neighborhood" loses meaning. With hundreds of features, DBSCAN first requires dimensionality reduction (PCA, UMAP).
    • Mandatory feature scaling. $\varepsilon$ is one number for all axes. If age is measured in tens and income in hundreds of thousands, the neighborhood is effectively determined by income alone. Features must be brought to comparable scales.
    • No predict for new points in the classic form: to assign a new object to a cluster, you need either a recomputation or a separate model on top of the result.

    1. What is DBSCAN and how does it fundamentally differ from KMeans?

      DBSCAN is an algorithm that finds clusters by point density. Unlike KMeans, it does not require specifying the number of clusters $k$, finds clusters of arbitrary shape, and automatically marks outliers as noise.

    2. What is a core point in DBSCAN?

      A point $p$ is a core point if its $\varepsilon$-neighborhood contains at least $\text{minPts}$ points: $|\{q : \|p-q\| \leq \varepsilon\}| \geq \text{minPts}$. Core points form the "body" of a cluster.

    3. How does DBSCAN handle outliers?

      Points that are not core points and are not reachable from any core point are labeled as noise (outliers). This makes DBSCAN robust to outliers — they do not "spoil" the clusters as they do in KMeans.

    4. How do the parameters $\varepsilon$ and $\text{minPts}$ affect the result?

      A large $\varepsilon$ merges clusters and yields less noise. A small $\varepsilon$ yields more noise and clusters fall apart. $\text{minPts}$ sets the "minimum density": higher means a stricter requirement for core points. Typically $\text{minPts} \geq d+1$, and $\varepsilon$ is chosen from the k-distance plot.

    5. Which three types of points does DBSCAN distinguish?

      Core points (at least $\text{minPts}$ neighbors in the $\varepsilon$-neighborhood) form the body of the cluster and extend it; border points (few neighbors, but within a core point's neighborhood) belong to the cluster but do not extend it; noise is everything else — such points belong to no cluster, and that is an expected result.

    6. How is $\varepsilon$ chosen in practice?

      Using the k-distance plot: for each point, compute the distance to its $k$-th neighbor ($k = \text{minPts}-1$), sort in ascending order, and look for the "elbow" — the point of a sharp bend. Before the bend distances grow slowly (dense regions), after it they jump (empty space); the value at the bend is taken as $\varepsilon$.

    7. Why does DBSCAN work poorly with clusters of different density?

      There is a single radius $\varepsilon$ for the entire dataset, so no single value works for both dense and sparse groups: with a small $\varepsilon$ the sparse cluster becomes noise, with a large one the dense clusters merge. HDBSCAN solves this problem by considering a hierarchy of density levels.

  57. Hierarchical

    Hierarchical (agglomerative) clustering builds a tree of clusters (a dendrogram) that can be cut at the desired level. It comes in agglomerative (bottom-up) and divisive (top-down) variants.

    In the simplest terms: the agglomerative variant works like a knockout tournament in reverse — everyone merges. At first, each object is its own cluster. At each step, we find the two closest groups and merge them into one. We repeat until everything is gathered into a single cluster. The history of merges is the dendrogram — a tree that can be "cut" at any height to get the desired number of clusters.

    The distance between two points is clear. But what is the distance between two groups of points? The answer to this question is called linkage, and it is exactly what determines which clusters you get:

    • Single linkage (nearest neighbor) — the distance between the closest pair of points from the two clusters. It can find elongated clusters of arbitrary shape but suffers from the chaining effect: two dense clouds will merge if a chain of a few points happens to lie between them.
    • Complete linkage (farthest neighbor) — the distance between the farthest pair. It yields compact, spherical clusters of roughly equal size and does not suffer from chaining, but it easily splits an elongated cluster in two.
    • Average linkage — the average over all pairs, a compromise between the first two.
    • Ward — at each step, merges the pair that increases within-cluster variance the least. In spirit, it is the hierarchical analog of KMeans; in practice it is used most often and yields clusters of comparable size.

    The same dataset can produce completely different partitions under single and complete linkage — which is why interviewers ask about linkage more often than about the algorithm itself.

    In a dendrogram, objects are laid out horizontally, and the height of a crossbar is the distance at which the merge happened. A low crossbar means very similar objects were merged; a high one means two groups that were already far apart were joined.

    This gives the rule for choosing the number of clusters: cut where the vertical lines are longest. A long segment without merges means the next merge happened only at a substantially larger distance — that is, there is a real gap between these groups. The number of vertical lines crossed by the cut line gives the number of clusters.

    This is convenient because $k$ does not need to be fixed in advance as in KMeans: a single construction gives the whole hierarchy at once, and different cuts give different numbers of clusters — from large segments to small subgroups.

    • Complexity. A naive implementation is $O(n^3)$ in time and $O(n^2)$ in memory: it needs the pairwise distance matrix. With 100,000 objects that is already 40 GB for the matrix alone, so the method is applied to datasets of up to tens of thousands of objects.
    • Merges are irreversible. Once it merges two clusters, the algorithm never revisits that decision — an early mistake propagates all the way to the top. KMeans, by contrast, reassigns points at every iteration.
    • A practical note. Hierarchical clustering rarely comes in handy, and understanding it takes a long time if you are not used to it. So there is no need to dig deep — it is enough to understand the idea of a dendrogram and the difference between linkage methods.

    1. What is hierarchical clustering and what types are there?

      Agglomerative (bottom-up): each object starts as a separate cluster, then the closest ones are merged. Divisive (top-down): start with one cluster and recursively split it. The result is a dendrogram (a tree of clusters).

    2. How does hierarchical clustering differ from KMeans?

      It does not require specifying the number of clusters $k$ in advance — the number is chosen by cutting the dendrogram. It is slower ($O(n^2)$ or $O(n^3)$ versus $O(nkd)$ for KMeans). It gives a hierarchical representation of the data rather than a flat partition.

    3. What is a dendrogram and how is the number of clusters chosen from it?

      A dendrogram is a tree showing the order of merges and the distances between the merged clusters. The number of clusters is chosen by "cutting" with a horizontal line: where the dendrogram shows a large jump in distance, there is a natural boundary.

    4. What is linkage and what variants exist?

      Linkage is the rule that defines the distance between two clusters. Single (by the closest pair of points) finds elongated clusters but suffers from the chaining effect; complete (by the farthest pair) gives compact clusters but splits elongated ones; average is a compromise; Ward merges the pair that minimally increases within-cluster variance and is used most often.

    5. What is the chaining effect in single linkage?

      Two dense but distinct clouds of points merge into one cluster if a chain of intermediate points happens to lie between them: the closest-pair distance is small at every step of such a chain. As a result, the clusters come out elongated and substantively wrong.

    6. Why isn't hierarchical clustering used on large data?

      A naive implementation requires the pairwise distance matrix: $O(n^2)$ memory and up to $O(n^3)$ time. For 100,000 objects the matrix alone takes tens of gigabytes. In addition, merges are irreversible — an early mistake is not corrected in later steps.

  58. Spectral

    Spectral clustering uses the spectral properties of the adjacency (or distance) matrix, applying SVD or the eigenvalues of a graph to project the data before clustering.

    In the simplest terms: if the data can't be separated where it lies, move it to where it can be. The method builds a "who is whose neighbor" graph and then finds a new space in which connected points end up close together and unconnected ones far apart. In this new space the clusters become ordinary blobs, and the task can be finished off with simple K-Means.

    Take the classic nested rings. K-Means is powerless here: the centers of both rings coincide, and any straight boundary will cut through both rings.

    Now look at the data as a graph: connect each point to its nearest neighbors. What do we get? Within one ring the points form a connected chain — you can get from any point to any other by stepping through neighbors. But there are no edges between the rings: they are separated by empty space.

    So in graph terms the task becomes trivial — we need to find the connected components. Spectral clustering does exactly that, only not crudely (connected / not connected) but softly, through the eigenvectors of the Laplacian, which lets it handle clusters that aren't fully separated as well.

    The key shift in thinking: what matters is not closeness in feature space but connectivity through a chain of neighbors. Two points on opposite sides of a ring are far from each other, yet they belong to the same cluster because there is a path between them.

    Spectral clustering is a clustering method based on the spectral properties of the similarity matrix of a graph, which is a set of data points connected to each other according to their similarity. The main idea is to transform the graph's similarity matrix into a Laplacian to obtain its eigenvectors, which are then used to project the data into a new lower-dimensional space for better separability, where another clustering method, such as K-means, is then applied.

    Algorithm:

    • Build the adjacency matrix $W$ (e.g., with an RBF kernel): $$W_{ij} = \exp\!\left(-\frac{\|x_i - x_j\|^2}{2\sigma^2}\right),$$ if $x_j$ is among the $k$ nearest neighbors of $x_i$.
    • Compute the Laplacian matrix (the Laplacian):
      • $D$ is the diagonal degree matrix: $D_{ii} = \sum_j W_{ij}$
      • $L = D - W$ (the unnormalized Laplacian)
      • or the normalized Laplacian: $L_{sym} = D^{-1/2} L D^{-1/2}$
    • Find the $k$ smallest eigenvectors of $L$ or $L_{sym}$ and form the matrix $U \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}$.
    • Cluster the rows of $U$ with K-Means.

    The Laplacian $L = D - W$ is a matrix that encodes the structure of the graph, and it has a remarkable property:

    • The number of zero eigenvalues equals the number of connected components of the graph. If the graph splits into three disconnected parts, $L$ will have exactly three zero eigenvalues, and the corresponding eigenvectors will be constant on each component — effectively, these are ready-made cluster labels.
    • When the components are weakly connected (rather than fully disconnected), the eigenvalues are not exactly zero but small, and the eigenvectors are nearly constant within groups. That's why we take the $k$ smallest eigenvectors.
    • The rows of the resulting matrix $U$ are the new coordinates of the objects. Points of the same group get nearly identical coordinates, and K-Means on them works trivially.

    Hence "spectral" in the name: the spectrum is the set of eigenvalues of a matrix.

    • Expensive. You need an $n \times n$ similarity matrix and an eigendecomposition: up to $O(n^3)$ in time and $O(n^2)$ in memory. The method is applicable to tens of thousands of objects, but not to millions.
    • The number of clusters is set in advance, as in K-Means. A hint is given by the eigengap — a noticeable gap between the $k$-th and $(k+1)$-th eigenvalues.
    • Sensitivity to $\sigma$ in the RBF kernel. Too small a value breaks the graph into many small pieces; too large connects everything to everything. This parameter matters more than the algorithm itself.
    • No predict for new points. Like DBSCAN, the method offers no ready-made way to assign a new object to a cluster without recomputing.

    It is used when clusters have a complex shape and are not separable in the original space, but become separable after the spectral transformation.

    There are various ways to build the similarity matrix; the most popular ones are:

    • Nearest_neighbors (by computing the nearest-neighbor graph);
    • RBF (using a radial basis function);
    • Precomputed, where the features are given as a similarity matrix or a nearest-neighbor graph;
    • Various kernels: linear, polynomial, RBF, and others (see SVM).

    1. What is spectral clustering?

      A method that uses the eigenvectors of the Laplacian matrix of a similarity graph. The data is projected into a new space (along the eigenvectors) where the clusters become linearly separable, and then KMeans is applied.

    2. What is the Laplacian matrix and how is it built?

      $L = D - W$, where $W$ is the similarity matrix (e.g., with an RBF kernel) and $D$ is the diagonal degree matrix ($D_{ii} = \sum_j W_{ij}$). The eigenvectors of $L$ corresponding to small eigenvalues encode the connectivity structure of the graph.

    3. For which data is spectral clustering better than KMeans?

      For clusters of complex, non-round shape (rings, half-moons, spirals) that aren't separable in the original space. KMeans assumes convex clusters — the spectral method handles arbitrary geometry through the similarity graph.

    4. What are the steps of the algorithm?

      1. Similarity matrix $W$ (RBF or kNN). 2. Laplacian $L = D - W$ (or normalized). 3. The first $k$ eigenvectors of $L$. 4. KMeans on the rows of the matrix $U \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times k}$.

    5. Why does spectral clustering handle rings and spirals while K-Means doesn't?

      K-Means relies on the distance to a center, so its boundaries are always straight and its clusters convex. The spectral method looks not at closeness in feature space but at connectivity through a chain of neighbors: two points on opposite sides of a ring are far apart but connected by a path, so they end up in the same cluster.

    6. Why are the smallest eigenvectors of the Laplacian used?

      The number of zero eigenvalues of the Laplacian equals the number of connected components of the graph, and the corresponding eigenvectors are constant on each component — effectively ready-made cluster labels. When groups are weakly connected rather than disconnected, the eigenvalues are small but nonzero, and the same vectors are nearly constant within groups.

    7. How do you choose the number of clusters in spectral clustering?

      Using the eigengap rule: sort the eigenvalues in ascending order and look for a noticeable gap between the $k$-th and $(k+1)$-th. The number of small eigenvalues before the gap indicates the number of weakly connected groups in the graph.

  59. Classification Algorithm: KNN (k-Nearest Neighbors)↗

    k-nearest neighbors is a lazy learning algorithm that classifies (or regresses) a new object based on the $k$ training examples closest to it.

    • In classification, the label of the new object is determined by the majority label of its neighbors.
    • In regression, by the mean of the neighbors' targets.
    • The distance between objects is usually measured with the Euclidean or Manhattan metric.

    In the simplest terms: "tell me who your neighbors are." To figure out whether a customer will repay a loan, find the five most similar customers from history and see what happened with them. No formula, no weights — just comparison with past experience.

    kNN has no training stage in the usual sense: fit simply memorizes the dataset. No parameters are fitted, no function is minimized.

    All the work is shifted to prediction time: to answer for a single object, you have to compute the distance to all training points and pick the $k$ closest.

    Hence its unusual cost profile, the exact opposite of other models:

    • training is instant, but the entire dataset must be kept in memory;
    • prediction is $O(n \cdot d)$ per object, where $n$ is the training set size and $d$ is the number of features.

    For comparison: a trained tree answers in microseconds regardless of how much data it was trained on. That's why kNN is a poor fit for production with large data and strict latency requirements, although structures like KD-tree and Ball-tree help somewhat in low dimensions.

    Given a new object $x$, the algorithm:

    1. Computes the distance to all points in the training set.
    2. Finds the $k$ closest.
    3. Predicts the class (or value) based on these neighbors.

    $k$ is the only truly important hyperparameter, and it directly governs the bias–variance trade-off:

    • $k = 1$: every training point creates its own region around itself. The boundary is jagged, the training error is zero (an object's nearest neighbor is itself), and a single noisy object spoils an entire region. Classic overfitting — high variance.
    • Large $k$: the decision is averaged over a wide neighborhood, and the boundary smooths out. In the limit $k = n$, the model always predicts the majority class of the whole dataset — that's underfitting, high bias.

    Practical guidelines: $k$ is tuned on validation; for binary classification, take an odd value so the vote never ends in a tie.

    • Scaling is mandatory. The algorithm is built entirely on distances, so a feature measured in thousands overwhelms a feature measured in units. Without normalization, kNN effectively looks only at the "largest" feature.
    • Curse of dimensionality. In high-dimensional space, the distances between all points become almost the same, and the notion of a "nearest neighbor" loses meaning. Quality noticeably degrades already at a few dozen features — prior dimensionality reduction helps.
    • Class imbalance. The majority class wins the vote simply because it has more objects. Distance weighting and rebalancing the dataset help.
    • Categorical features. The Euclidean distance between one-hot vectors is nearly uninformative; mixed data calls for special metrics (e.g., Gower distance).
    • Memory. The model stores the entire training set — that is its "weights."

    There's also weighted KNN, where each neighbor's contribution depends on distance. A closer neighbor can get a larger weight:

    $$w_i = \frac{1}{\rho(x, x_i)^2 + \varepsilon}$$

    A task on the platform

    1. How does the k-nearest neighbors algorithm work?

      To predict a new object, the distance to all points in the training set is computed, the $k$ closest are found, and the prediction is determined by majority vote (classification) or by the mean (regression).

    2. Why is KNN called a "lazy" algorithm?

      KNN doesn't build an explicit model during training — it just memorizes all the data. All the computational work happens at prediction time. This is the opposite of "eager" algorithms (e.g., trees), which spend time on training.

    3. How does the choice of $k$ affect KNN quality?

      Small $k$ (e.g., 1) — high variance, overfitting. Large $k$ — high bias, the model "averages" too much. The optimal $k$ is tuned on the validation set.

    4. What is weighted KNN and why is it needed?

      Each neighbor's contribution is inversely proportional to its distance: $w_i = 1/(\rho^2 + \varepsilon)$. Closer neighbors have more influence. This is useful when the data is unevenly distributed in feature space.

    5. Why is kNN training instant but prediction expensive?

      During training, the algorithm just memorizes the dataset — no parameters are fitted. All the work is done at prediction: you have to compute the distance to all $n$ training objects, i.e., $O(n \cdot d)$ per query. This is the opposite of trees and linear models, which take a long time to train but answer in microseconds.

    6. How does $k$ affect the bias–variance trade-off?

      At $k=1$, the boundary is jagged, the training error is zero, but a single noisy object spoils an entire region — high variance, overfitting. At large $k$, the decision is averaged over a wide neighborhood and smoothed; in the limit $k=n$, the model always predicts the majority class — high bias, underfitting.

    7. Why does kNN suffer especially from the curse of dimensionality?

      The method relies entirely on distances, and in high-dimensional space the distances between all pairs of points become almost the same. The notion of a "nearest neighbor" stops being meaningful: the closest point turns out to be almost as far as the farthest. Prior dimensionality reduction helps.

    8. Why is feature scaling mandatory for kNN?

      The distance sums up feature contributions in their own units, so a feature with a large numeric range (income in rubles) completely determines the result, while features measured in units (age, number of children) effectively don't factor in.

    60. Block 6

  60. Naive Bayes classifier↗

    The Naive Bayes classifier is a probabilistic classifier based on Bayes' theorem with a strong (naive) assumption that the features are independent of each other given the class.

    In the simplest terms: the model counts how many times each feature occurred in each class, and on a new object it multiplies these frequencies. The word "naive" refers to the assumption that the features are unrelated to each other — obviously false, but extremely convenient.

    The classic task for this method. During training, the model simply counts frequencies:

    • the word "prize" appears in 30% of spam emails and 0.5% of regular ones;
    • the word "report" — in 1% of spam and 15% of regular emails;
    • spam makes up 40% of all mail (this is the prior probability).

    An email arrives containing the words "prize" and "report". We compute the "scores" of both hypotheses:

    • spam: $0.4 \times 0.30 \times 0.01 = 0.0012$;
    • not spam: $0.6 \times 0.005 \times 0.15 = 0.00045$.

    Spam scores higher — the email is marked as spam. No optimization, no gradients: training boils down to counting frequencies in a single pass over the data. Hence the main advantage of the method — exceptional speed.

    The naive assumption that the features are independent of each other greatly simplifies the classification problem, because one-dimensional probability densities are estimated instead of a single multidimensional one.

    How much it simplifies is clear from numbers. Suppose we have 20 binary features.

    • The honest approach requires estimating $P(X_1, \ldots, X_{20} \mid y)$ — the joint distribution. That is $2^{20} \approx 10^6$ combinations per class, and a reliable estimate of each needs examples. You will never have that much data.
    • The naive approach estimates 20 separate probabilities $P(X_i \mid y)$ per class. That is forty numbers, computable from any reasonable sample.

    That is exactly why the assumption is introduced: without it, the problem is simply unsolvable on real data volumes. It is a classic trade-off: an obviously wrong model, but one that can be estimated.

    Words in text are related: "machine" and "learning" occur together far more often than separately. There is nothing close to independence — and yet naive Bayes held up for years as a strong baseline in text classification. Why?

    Because classification does not need accurate probabilities — it only needs the correct ordering of classes. The method systematically misestimates probabilities: with correlated features, it effectively counts the same evidence several times and outputs values like 0.9999 where the real probability is 0.7. But if the error shifts the estimates of all classes in the same direction, the argmax stays correct, and the label is predicted correctly.

    The practical consequence: naive Bayes gives good labels and bad probabilities. Its predict_proba cannot be used as a real probability (e.g., for computing expected losses) without calibration.

    Bayes' theorem reads as follows:

    $$P(A|B) = \frac{P(B|A)\, P(A)}{P(B)},$$

    where:

    • $P(A|B)$ is the posterior probability of event $A$ given that event $B$ occurred;
    • $P(B|A)$ is the conditional probability of event $B$ given that event $A$ occurred;
    • $P(A)$ and $P(B)$ are the prior probabilities of events $A$ and $B$, respectively.

    In the context of machine learning, Bayes' theorem takes the following form:

    $$P(y_k|X) = \frac{P(y_k)\,P(X|y_k)}{P(X)},$$

    where:

    • $P(y_k|X)$ is the posterior probability that the sample belongs to class $y_k$ given its features $X$;
    • $P(X|y_k)$ is the likelihood, i.e., the probability of the features $X$ given class $y_k$;
    • $P(y_k)$ is the prior probability that a randomly chosen observation belongs to class $y_k$;
    • $P(X)$ is the prior probability of the features $X$.

    If an object is described not by one but by several features $X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n$, the formula becomes:

    $$P(y_k|X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n) = \frac{\textcolor{red}{P(y_k)\prod_{i=1}^n P(X_i|y_k)}}{P(X_1, X_2, \ldots, X_n)}$$

    In practice, the numerator of this formula is of greatest interest, since the denominator depends only on the features, not on the class, and is therefore often omitted when comparing the probabilities of different classes. Ultimately, the classification rule amounts to choosing the class with the maximum posterior probability:

    $$y_k \propto \arg\max_{y_k}\, \textcolor{red}{P(y_k)\prod_{i=1}^n P(X_i|y_k)}$$

    To estimate the model parameters, i.e., the probabilities $P(y_k)$ and $P(X_i|y_k)$, maximum likelihood estimation is usually applied, which in this case is based on the frequencies of classes and features in the training set.

    Variants of naive Bayes

    Gaussian naive Bayes (GaussianNB):

    $$P(x_i|y) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\sigma_y^2}}\exp\!\left(-\frac{(x_i-\mu_y)^2}{2\sigma_y^2}\right),$$

    where $\mu_y$ and $\sigma_y$ are the mean and standard deviation of the feature in class $y$. These parameters are estimated from the training data using maximum likelihood estimation.

    GaussianNB is the variant for continuous features that follow a normal (Gaussian) distribution. The probability of a feature given the class is computed using the formula above.

    Bernoulli naive Bayes (BernoulliNB):

    $$P(x_i|y) = P(x_i = 1|y)\,x_i + (1-P(x_i = 1|y))(1-x_i),$$

    where $P(x_i = 1|y)$ is the probability that feature $i$ takes the value $1$ (true) given that the object belongs to class $y$; $x_i$ is the value of feature $i$ ($0$ or $1$).

    BernoulliNB is another variant, for discrete features that follow a Bernoulli distribution. In this case, the features are binary indicators of the presence or absence of certain properties in the object. For example, in a text classification task, this could be the presence or absence of certain words in the text. The probability of a feature given the class is computed using the formula above.

    • Laplace smoothing. If a word never occurred in a class in the training set, its probability is zero — and the entire product becomes zero, regardless of the other features. A single unfamiliar word can wipe out all the other evidence. This is fixed by adding one to the counts (add-one smoothing, the alpha parameter in sklearn), which is done by default.
    • Logarithms instead of a product. Multiplying hundreds of probabilities like $0.001$ instantly yields machine zero (arithmetic underflow). So in practice the sum of logarithms is computed: $\log P(y) + \sum_i \log P(X_i \mid y)$. The ordering of classes is preserved, since the logarithm is monotonic.
    • Text classification — spam filters, topic categorization. The method is especially appropriate here: there are tens of thousands of features, they are sparse, and training is linear in time.
    • A fast baseline. It trains in a single pass over the data, requires minimal memory, and has no hyperparameters other than smoothing. An excellent reference point to compare a complex model against.
    • Small samples. It estimates few parameters, so it does not fall apart where complex models overfit.
    • Online learning. Frequencies are easily updated as new data arrives, without full retraining.

    1. What is the essence of the "naive" assumption of the Bayes classifier?

      The assumption of conditional independence of features given the class: $P(X|y_k) = \prod_i P(X_i|y_k)$. In practice features are correlated, but the algorithm still works well, especially in text tasks.

    2. How is Bayes' theorem applied to classification?

      For each class $y_k$, compute $P(y_k) \prod_i P(X_i|y_k)$ and choose the class with the maximum value. The denominator $P(X)$ does not depend on the class, so it can be omitted when comparing classes.

    3. How does GaussianNB differ from BernoulliNB?

      GaussianNB assumes that continuous features are normally distributed within each class — it estimates $\mu_y$ and $\sigma_y^2$. BernoulliNB works with binary features (a word is present/absent in a document), estimating the probability $P(x_i=1|y)$.

    4. When is naive Bayes used in practice?

      Text classification (spam/not spam, document topic), medical diagnosis. Pros: fast training, good with small data, works in high dimensions. Con: violating the independence assumption reduces quality when correlations are strong.

    5. Why is the naive independence assumption needed if it is obviously false?

      Without it, you would have to estimate the joint feature distribution: for 20 binary features that is about a million combinations per class, and there is never enough data for that. The naive assumption reduces the problem to 20 one-dimensional distributions per class, i.e., a few dozen numbers that can be estimated from any reasonable sample.

    6. Why does the method give good labels but bad probabilities?

      With correlated features, it effectively counts the same evidence several times and outputs overconfident estimates like 0.9999 instead of 0.7. But for classification only the ordering of classes matters: if the estimates of all classes are shifted in the same direction, the argmax stays correct. predict_proba cannot be used as a real probability without calibration.

    7. What is Laplace smoothing and why is it needed?

      Adding one to the frequency counts. Without it, a feature that never occurred in a class gets zero probability, zeroing out the entire product — a single unfamiliar word wipes out all the other evidence. In sklearn, the alpha parameter controls this.

    8. Why is the sum of logarithms computed in practice instead of the product of probabilities?

      Multiplying hundreds of small probabilities leads to arithmetic underflow — the result becomes machine zero. The sum of logarithms is numerically stable, and since the logarithm is monotonic, the ordering of classes and hence the argmax result do not change.

  61. SVM (Support Vector Machine)↗

    The Support Vector Machine (SVM) is a supervised learning algorithm used for classification and regression. Its goal is to find a hyperplane that separates the classes as widely as possible in feature space.

    In the simplest terms: infinitely many separating lines can be drawn between two classes, and SVM chooses the one that runs exactly down the middle, as far as possible from both sides. It's like building a road between two villages not right up against one village's fence, but along the widest strip of open land.

    Logistic regression will settle on any line that separates the classes — it's enough for it that objects end up on the correct side. SVM demands more, and here's why that makes sense.

    The training set is just one particular example. A new object will lie slightly off from its training neighbors. If the boundary runs right up against the training points, the slightest shift will put the object on the wrong side. A wide margin creates a safety buffer: to be misclassified, an object has to deviate from its peers more strongly.

    Hence the support vectors — the objects lying exactly on the edge of the strip. They alone determine the whole solution: the other points can be removed from the dataset, and the hyperplane won't move a millimeter. This is a rare property with two consequences: the model is compact (it stores only the support vectors) and robust to objects far from the boundary.

    SVM aims not just to separate the classes but to do so with the maximum margin between them. This improves the model's generalization ability.

    The linear SVM problem is formulated as an optimization problem. I won't write out the formulas, because in the end it all comes down to the Karush-Kuhn-Tucker problem. The formulas are cumbersome, and that level of depth isn't needed right now and is unlikely to come in handy in practice! Link to the formulas

    Linear separability is when the classes can be separated by a linear hyperplane.

    Perfectly separable data almost never happens: classes overlap, and labeling errors occur. That's why a soft margin is used — a margin that allows some objects to intrude into the strip and even end up on the wrong side, but with a penalty.

    The size of the penalty is set by the hyperparameter C:
