    • small C — violations are cheap, the margin is wide, and individual objects are sacrificed. This is strong regularization; the model is simpler and more robust;
    • large C — every violation is expensive, the model tries to classify the entire training set correctly, and the margin narrows. This is weak regularization and a direct path to overfitting, especially on noisy data.

    Mnemonic: C is the "cost of an error". The higher it is, the harder the model chases a perfect separation of the training data.

    In real life, classes are rarely linearly separable. We have to draw a boundary between the two classes that is nonlinear, i.e., it can bend in any way we like.

    To solve nonlinear problems, SVM uses a clever trick that lets us do the following: if in the original feature space we can't easily draw a linear hyperplane, let's try to do it in a new space where the separating hyperplane will be linear. Once we've done that, we go back to the original space and use this hyperplane for classification. The beauty of it is that in the original space the constructed hyperplane will have a nontrivial, nonlinear shape that we would hardly have been able to build under the original conditions.

    In other words, the kernel trick is used:

    $$K(\mathbf{x}_i, \mathbf{x}_j) = \phi(\mathbf{x}_i)^\top \phi(\mathbf{x}_j)$$

    We move from the original space to a new, rectifying (feature) space, in which the dot product of vectors is computed via the kernel $K$.

    Popular kernels:

    • Linear: $K(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}'$
    • Polynomial: $K(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = (\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}' + c)^d$
    • RBF (Gaussian): $K(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = \exp\!\left(-\gamma \|\mathbf{x} - \mathbf{x}'\|^2\right)$

    Consider an example of moving to a rectifying space using the kernel trick.

    Let's try moving to a three-dimensional rectifying space, $\R^3$, i.e., increase the dimension by $1$. Define the following transformation:

    $$\phi(\mathbf{x}) = (x_1^2,\; x_2^2,\; \sqrt{2}x_1x_2)$$

    We have only 2 features initially. In the new space, let's square $x_1, x_2$ and construct the third feature as $\sqrt{2}x_1x_2$. Now our data looks like this:

    In this space the classes become linearly separable:

    Returning to the original space, we get the following nonlinear hyperplane:

    You might ask: so which kernel did we end up choosing here? The answer: we chose the polynomial kernel:

    $$K(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') = (\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}' + c)^d,$$

    in our case $c = 0$ and $d = 2$:

    $$\begin{aligned} K(\mathbf{x}, \mathbf{x}') &= (\mathbf{x}^\top \mathbf{x}')^2 =\\ &= (x_1x_1' + x_2x_2')^2 = x_1^2x_1'^2 + 2x_1x_1'x_2x_2' + x_2^2x_2'^2 =\\ &= (x_1^2,\; x_2^2,\; \sqrt{2}x_1x_2) \cdot (x_1'^2,\; x_2'^2,\; \sqrt{2}x_1'x_2') \end{aligned}$$

    The key trick is easy to miss: we never compute $\phi(x)$ explicitly.

    In the example above, the move to $\mathbb{R}^3$ looked harmless. But for the RBF kernel the corresponding space is infinite-dimensional — computing an object's coordinates in it is simply impossible.

    What saves us is that all of SVM's math is structured so that objects enter it only through dot products $\phi(x_i)^\top\phi(x_j)$. And the kernel $K(x_i, x_j)$ gives the value of this product directly, from the original coordinates, without computing the $\phi$ themselves.

    In other words: we don't need coordinates in the new space, only the pairwise "angles and distances" between objects — and the kernel gives us those right away. That's exactly why an infinite-dimensional space turns out to be computationally free.

    • Scaling is mandatory. The method is based on distances and dot products, so features in different units break both the margin and the RBF kernel.
    • Scales poorly with data size. Training complexity is roughly $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^3)$: a matrix of pairwise kernel values is needed. On datasets over 100,000 objects, kernel SVM is practically unusable — there people use LinearSVC or switch to boosting.
    • Two hyperparameters critical for quality: C (the cost of a margin violation) and gamma for RBF (an object's radius of influence). A large gamma means a narrow radius and an extremely jagged boundary — a sure path to overfitting. They are tuned together, on a log-scale grid.
    • SVM doesn't give probabilities. predict_proba in sklearn is obtained by separate calibration (Platt scaling) and requires additional cross-validation within training.
    • Poor interpretability with a nonlinear kernel. The coefficients live in the rectifying space and can't be explained in terms of the original features.

    Practice problem on the platform

    Practice problem on the platform

    1. What is the main idea of SVM and what is the margin?

      SVM looks for a hyperplane that separates the classes with the maximum margin — the distance to the nearest points of each class (the support vectors). Maximizing the margin improves the model's generalization ability.

    2. How does SVM solve problems that aren't linearly separable?

      Via the kernel trick: the data is implicitly mapped to a higher-dimensional space $\phi(x)$ where the classes become linearly separable. The dot product $\phi(x_i)^\top\phi(x_j)$ is replaced by the kernel $K(x_i, x_j)$ — without computing $\phi$ explicitly.

    3. Name three popular SVM kernels and where they are used.

      Linear — linearly separable data. Polynomial $(\mathbf{x}^\top\mathbf{x}' + c)^d$ — polynomial boundaries. RBF (Gaussian) $\exp(-\gamma\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{x}'\|^2)$ — a universal nonlinear kernel, a popular default.

    4. What is a "rectifying space" in the context of SVM?

      The space into which the data is mapped via $\phi$ and in which the classes become linearly separable. In the original space this corresponds to a nonlinear separating surface.

    5. What are support vectors and why are they so important?

      They are the objects lying exactly on the edge of the separating strip. They completely determine the position of the hyperplane: the other objects can be removed from the training set, and the solution won't change. Hence the model's compactness — it stores only the support vectors — and its robustness to points far from the boundary.

    6. What does the C parameter control in SVM?

      The cost of a margin violation with a soft margin. A small C makes violations cheap: the margin is wide and some objects are sacrificed — strong regularization. A large C forces the model to classify the entire training set correctly, narrowing the margin, which leads to overfitting on noisy data.

    7. Why doesn't the kernel trick require computing $\phi(x)$?

      All of SVM's math is structured so that objects enter it only through dot products $\phi(x_i)^\top\phi(x_j)$, and the kernel $K(x_i,x_j)$ computes this product directly from the original coordinates. That's why we can work even in the infinite-dimensional space of the RBF kernel, where an object's coordinates can't be computed.

    8. Why is SVM rarely applied to large datasets?

      Training requires a matrix of pairwise kernel values and has a complexity of roughly $O(n^2)$ to $O(n^3)$ in the number of objects. On datasets over 100,000 objects this becomes impractical, and instead of kernel SVM people use LinearSVC or gradient boosting.

  62. PCA

    PCA is Principal Component Analysis. The task is to replace the current feature set with another one that reduces the dimensionality of the problem.

    In the simplest terms: we rotate the coordinate system so that the data stretches out along the new axes, and keep only the axes along which the spread is large. Axes along which the data barely changes are thrown away — they carry little information.

    An everyday analogy: to photograph an elongated object, you walk around it and shoot from the side where it looks most "spread out". PCA does the same thing, only automatically and in a multidimensional space.

    • Visualization. You cannot plot 768-dimensional embeddings, but after PCA down to two components, you can.
    • Fighting multicollinearity. The new features are orthogonal by construction, i.e., uncorrelated. This cures the instability of linear model coefficients.
    • Speed and savings. Hundreds of features instead of thousands mean less memory and faster training.
    • Noise suppression. Components with low variance often contain mostly measurement noise, and discarding them can even improve quality.

    Let us introduce notation: $f_i(x),\; i=\overline{1,n}$ are the numerical features, $g_j(x),; j=\overline{1,m}$ are the new numerical features, $m \leq n$.

    We impose a requirement: the old numerical features must be linearly recoverable from the new ones:

    $$\hat{f}_j(x) = \sum_{s=1}^m g_s(x)\,u_{js},\; j=\overline{1,n}\;\forall x\in X,$$

    $\hat{f}_j(x)$ is the estimate of the feature $f_j(x)$. We want them to be recovered as accurately as possible on the training set $X: x_1,\dots,x_\ell$:

    $$\sum_{i=1}^\ell \sum_{j=1}^n (\hat{f}_j(x) - f_j(x))^2 \to \min_{\{g_s(x_i)\},\, \{u_{js}\}}$$

    We get least squares for constructing a transformation such that the recovered features are as close as possible to the original ones for any objects $x_i$ from the training set.

    Clearly, this is an unsupervised learning task: we have a set of features $f_1(x),\dots, f_n(x)$ from which we want to build new features (fewer of them), requiring that the original ones be well recoverable from the smaller number of features. Essentially, we want to compress information. It is a method for "archiving" the vectors $f_i(x)$.

    To draw an analogy with compressed image formats, this is JPEG — a lossy format, but one where the amount of information lost is a parameter. You can control how accurately the original information is recovered from its compressed representation. The same holds here. We move to a new, lower-dimensional feature space $g_j(x)$.

    Recovery need not be linear — it can also be done with (deep) neural networks, namely autoencoders.

    In matrix notation

    In matrix notation, the problem looks like this:

    $$F_{\ell \times n} = \begin{bmatrix} f_1(x_1) & \dots & f_n(x_1)\\ \vdots & & \vdots\\ f_1(x_\ell) & \dots & f_n(x_\ell) \end{bmatrix}$$

    is the object–feature matrix. Rows are objects, columns are features. Similarly,

    $$G_{\ell \times m} = \begin{bmatrix} g_1(x_1) & \dots & g_m(x_1)\\ \vdots & & \vdots\\ g_1(x_\ell) & \dots & g_m(x_\ell) \end{bmatrix}$$

    is the matrix of new features. As before, we want to find a linear transformation into the new feature space that reduces the dimensionality of the problem with minimal loss. This means finding a matrix of a linear operator (linear transformation)

    $$U_{n \times m} = \begin{bmatrix} u_{11} & \dots & u_{1m}\\ \vdots & & \vdots\\ u_{n1} & \dots & u_{nm}\\ \end{bmatrix},$$

    such that

    $$\hat{F} = GU^T \approx F.$$

    Thus we need to find both the new features $G$ and the transformation $U$:

    $$\sum_{i=1}^\ell \sum_{j=1}^n (\hat{f}_j(x) - f_j(x))^2 = \|GU^T - F\|^2 \to \min_{G,\, U}$$

    — this is the formula in matrix notation. It looks a bit simpler and more concise.

    PCA tells us that:

    • We should keep only the features that best explain the variance of the data.

    • The matrix $U$ should consist of the eigenvectors corresponding to the largest eigenvalues of the matrix $F^TF$.

    The logic is this: variance = information.

    If a feature takes almost the same value for all objects, it does not help tell them apart — nothing can be said from it. Conversely, a direction along which objects are widely spread separates them well.

    This criterion also has a second, equivalent reading: the direction of maximum variance is exactly the direction for which projecting onto it loses the least information. Maximizing the spread along an axis and minimizing the reconstruction error are the same problem written in two ways. That is why PCA is simultaneously "compression with minimal loss" and "a search for the most informative directions".

    The number $m$ is chosen by the explained variance ratio: each eigenvalue $\lambda_i$ shows how much variance falls on the corresponding component, and its ratio to the total sum is exactly that share.

    Practical approaches:

    • A threshold on the share. Keep as many components as needed to explain 90–95% of the variance in total. In sklearn this is done directly: PCA(n_components=0.95).
    • The scree plot. Eigenvalues sorted in descending order; look for the elbow after which the curve flattens out.
    • Task requirements. For visualization it is always 2 or 3, regardless of the explained variance.

    We take the largest eigenvalues precisely because we want the new features to explain as much of the original data's variance as possible. Moreover, there are fewer new features ($m < n$) than original ones, so we cannot take all the eigenvalues.

    • Scaling is mandatory. PCA maximizes variance, and variance depends on the units of measurement. An "income in rubles" feature has a variance in the billions and will become the first component simply because of its scale. Without StandardScaler, the result reflects the choice of units, not the structure of the data.
    • PCA is linear. It can only rotate and project. It will not unfold data lying on a curved manifold (a spiral, a ring) — for that you need t-SNE, UMAP, or autoencoders.
    • Components lose interpretability. The first component is a mixture like $0.3 \cdot \text{income} - 0.5 \cdot \text{age} + \ldots$. Explaining to the business what its increase means is usually impossible.
    • Large variance does not always mean usefulness for the task. PCA is an unsupervised method; it knows nothing about the target. It is quite possible that a feature informative for classification has low variance and will be discarded. When the relationship with the target matters, look toward LDA or feature selection.
    • Fit only on train. The transformation matrix consists of learned parameters, and fitting them on all the data means causing leakage.

    PCA looks for the best linear subspace. If you remove this restriction, you get an autoencoder:

    • Autoencoder — the same compression and reconstruction task, but with a nonlinear encoder and decoder.
    • Undercomplete autoencoder — a direct analog of PCA: without nonlinearities, it learns exactly the same subspace.
    • VAE — a step further: the latent space becomes probabilistic, and the model can generate new objects.

    1. What is PCA and what problem does it solve?

      PCA (principal component analysis) is a dimensionality reduction method: it replaces $n$ original features with $m < n$ new ones from which the original features can be linearly recovered with minimal error. The task is information compression with controlled loss.

    2. How can principal components be interpreted geometrically?

      The first principal component is the direction of maximum data variance. The second is the direction perpendicular to it with the maximum remaining variance. Each new component is orthogonal to the previous ones.

    3. Why does the matrix $U$ consist of eigenvectors of the matrix $F^TF$?

      Maximizing explained variance subject to $GU^T \approx F$ reduces to an eigenvalue problem. The eigenvectors with the largest eigenvalues correspond to the directions of maximum variance.

    4. Where is PCA used in ML in practice?

      Visualizing high-dimensional embeddings (2D/3D), preprocessing data before training (removing collinearity), dimensionality reduction to speed up training. Limitation: only linear dependencies — for nonlinear ones, autoencoders or t-SNE are used.

    5. Why does PCA maximize variance specifically?

      Variance here is a measure of informativeness: a feature that takes almost the same values for all objects does not help tell them apart. In addition, maximizing variance along an axis is equivalent to minimizing the reconstruction error when projecting — it is the same problem in two formulations.

    6. How do you choose the number of principal components?

      By the explained variance ratio: the eigenvalues show each component's contribution, and you keep as many as needed to explain 90–95% in total (PCA(n_components=0.95)). An alternative is a scree plot and looking for the elbow. For visualization, 2 or 3 components are always used regardless of the explained share.

    7. Why must features be scaled before PCA?

      PCA maximizes variance, and variance depends on the units of measurement. An "income in rubles" feature with a variance in the billions will become the first component simply because of its scale, not because of its informativeness. Without standardization, the result reflects the choice of units of measurement.

    8. Can PCA discard a feature that is important for classification?

      Yes. PCA is an unsupervised method; it knows nothing about the target and evaluates features only by their spread. A feature with low variance that clearly separates the classes may end up in the discarded components. When the relationship with the target matters, LDA or feature selection is used.

  63. MDS

    MDS (Multidimensional Scaling) is a method of multidimensional scaling. We have a finite set of points, and the distances between them are known. We want to place these points, for example, on a plane so that the Euclidean distances match the original ones as closely as possible.

    In the simplest terms: you are given a table of "who is how far from whom" and asked to draw it on a sheet of paper. There are no coordinates — only pairwise distances, and you have to place the points so that a ruler on your drawing shows roughly the same numbers.

    Take a table of distances between cities: Moscow–St. Petersburg 630 km, Moscow–Kazan 720 km, St. Petersburg–Kazan 1,200 km, and so on for twenty cities. We have no latitudes or longitudes — only numbers.

    MDS will place twenty points on a plane so that the distances measured with a ruler match the table as closely as possible. The result will be an almost real map of Russia — possibly rotated or mirrored, because distances do not change under rotations and reflections.

    That is the essence of the method: recover geometry from pairwise distances alone. And it works not only with kilometers: the distance can be the dissimilarity of products, the difference between DNA sequences, or the share of mismatched answers in a questionnaire.

    Consider a graph $G = \langle V, E \rangle$, where $V$ are the vertices, $E$ are the edges, and $(i,j) \in E$ are the observed distances $R_{ij}$ between vertices $i$ and $j$.

    The goal is to find vector representations $z_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ for each vertex such that the distances between them approximate $R_{ij}$.

    Formally, we solve the problem:

    $$\sum_{(i,j)\in E} w(R_{ij})\,(\|z_i - z_j\| - R_{ij})^2 \to \min_{Z\in \mathbb{R}^{|V| \times d}}$$

    Let's break the formula down:

    • $\|z_i - z_j\|$ — the distance in our picture (what we are building);
    • $R_{ij}$ — the distance in the original data (what we are given);
    • their squared difference — the penalty for the points ending up too close or too far apart in the picture;
    • the sum over all pairs — the overall "stress" of the configuration, which is what gets minimized.

    The weights $w(R_{ij})$ determine whose accuracy we sacrifice: $w = 1$ — all pairs are equally important; $w = 1/R_{ij}$ — emphasis on close pairs (we preserve the local structure, who is next to whom); $w = R_{ij}$ — emphasis on distant pairs (we preserve the overall shape of the cloud).

    Fitting arbitrary distances exactly onto a plane is usually impossible, and this is not a flaw of the algorithm but a matter of geometry.

    The simplest example: four cities whose pairwise distances are taken from the surface of a sphere. Such a configuration cannot be reproduced on a flat sheet without distortion — as every cartographer knows: any world map lies about either areas, angles, or distances.

    So MDS does not "solve" the problem; it minimizes stress — the total amount of distortion. This has two practical consequences:

    • The stress value is a quality metric for the embedding. High stress means the 2D picture is unreliable and no conclusions can be drawn from it; sometimes $d=3$ instead of $d=2$ helps.
    • The optimization is non-convex and is solved iteratively (the SMACOF algorithm), so the result depends on initialization — as with K-Means, several runs are made.
    • PCA looks for directions of maximum variance in feature space. If you have object coordinates and the distances are Euclidean, classical MDS gives the same result as PCA — this is a proven fact. The difference is that MDS does not require coordinates: a distance matrix is enough.
    • t-SNE and UMAP deliberately sacrifice long distances in order to carefully show local neighborhoods and separate clusters. MDS, on the contrary, honestly tries to preserve all distances, including large ones — so its picture shows clusters less well but reflects the overall geometry better.
    • Do not interpret the axes. An MDS projection has no "principal components": the configuration can be rotated or reflected without changing the stress. Only the mutual distances are meaningful, not "further right" or "higher up".
    • Always look at the stress. A beautiful picture with high stress is just a beautiful picture. Without this number, conclusions about the proximity of objects are unfounded.
    • The distance metric decides everything. MDS will faithfully display whatever matrix it is given. If distances are computed from unnormalized features, where salary in rubles sits next to age in years, the picture will reflect exactly that, not the structure of the data.

    1. What is MDS and what problem does it solve?

      MDS (Multidimensional Scaling) is a dimensionality reduction method that preserves distances between points. Given a distance matrix $R_{ij}$, find projections $z_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ such that $\|z_i - z_j\| \approx R_{ij}$.

    2. How does MDS differ from PCA?

      PCA works with the feature covariance matrix and maximizes explained variance. MDS works directly with the distance matrix and preserves geometry. Classical MDS with Euclidean distances is equivalent to PCA.

    3. How does the choice of weights $w(R_{ij})$ affect the result?

      $w = 1$ — all distances are equally important. $w = 1/R_{ij}$ — emphasis on close points (we preserve local structure). $w = R_{ij}$ — emphasis on distant points (we preserve global structure). The choice depends on the task.

    4. What is stress and why look at it?

      Stress is the value of the minimized sum $\sum w(R_{ij})(\|z_i - z_j\| - R_{ij})^2$, i.e., the total distortion of distances in the resulting projection. Fitting arbitrary distances exactly onto a plane is usually impossible, so stress serves as a measure of trust in the picture: with high stress, no conclusions about the relative positions of objects can be drawn.

    5. Why is it meaningless to interpret the axes of an MDS projection?

      The objective depends only on pairwise distances, and these do not change under rotations and reflections of the configuration. So any MDS picture can be rotated or reflected without making the solution worse — only the mutual distances between points are meaningful, not the directions of the axes.

    6. When is MDS preferable to PCA?

      When there are no object coordinates at all, only a matrix of pairwise distances or dissimilarities (Levenshtein distance between strings, the share of mismatched answers, expert similarity ratings). PCA requires a feature representation; MDS works directly with distances.

  64. SNE

    SNE (Stochastic Neighbor Embedding) is a probabilistic dimensionality reduction method in which distances are interpreted as probabilities of points being neighbors.

    In the simplest terms: instead of asking "how many centimeters apart are these points", the method asks "how likely is it that they are neighbors". It then lays the points out on a plane so that these neighbor probabilities are preserved — whoever was someone's neighbor in the original space should remain their neighbor in the picture.

    This switch gives two things that MDS doesn't have:

    • Locality. Distant points get a neighbor probability close to zero and barely affect the result. The method stops wasting effort on accurately reproducing large distances and focuses on what matters — who is next to whom.
    • Adaptivity to density. Each point has its own kernel width $\sigma_i$, so "neighborhood" in a dense region and in a sparse one is measured at different scales.

    Given: points $x_i \in \mathbb{R}^n$. We need to find a mapping $y_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ (where $d \ll n$) such that the distances between points in the new space preserve the structure of the data.

    SNE models the probability that $x_j$ is a neighbor of $x_i$ in the original space:

    $$p(j|i) = \frac{\exp\!\left(-\dfrac{\|x_i - x_j\|^2}{2\sigma_i^2}\right)}{\displaystyle\sum_{k \neq i} \exp\!\left(-\dfrac{\|x_i - x_k\|^2}{2\sigma_i^2}\right)}$$

    Similarly, in the projection space $\mathbb{R}^d$ we define:

    $$q(j|i) = \frac{\exp\!\left(-\|y_i - y_j\|^2\right)}{\displaystyle\sum_{k \neq i} \exp\!\left(-\|y_i - y_k\|^2\right)}$$

    Both formulas have the same structure — a softmax over negative squared distances:

    • the numerator $\exp(-\|x_i - x_j\|^2 / 2\sigma_i^2)$ is the "raw closeness": the farther $x_j$ is from $x_i$, the faster (exponentially) it falls off;
    • the denominator is the sum of such closenesses over all other points; it normalizes them into probabilities that sum to one;
    • $p(j|i)$ reads as "the probability that $i$ would pick $j$ as its neighbor".

    The only difference between $p$ and $q$ is where the distances are computed: $p$ in the original high-dimensional space, $q$ on our plane. The method's job is to move the points $y_i$ so that $q$ becomes similar to $p$.

    The kernel width $\sigma_i$ determines what counts as "nearby" for point $i$. Setting it by hand for each point is impossible, so instead we set one intuitive number — the perplexity — and $\sigma_i$ is tuned to match it automatically.

    Perplexity is easiest to understand as the effective number of neighbors: a perplexity of 30 means "each point has roughly 30 significant neighbors". In a dense region this requires a small $\sigma_i$, in a sparse one a large one, and the method finds it on its own by solving an equation for each point separately.

    Typical values range from 5 to 50. A small perplexity reveals fine local structure and many small groups; a large one reveals larger formations.

    The objective function is likelihood maximization:

    $$\sum_{i} \sum_{j \ne i} p(j|i)\log q(j|i) \to \max$$

    Density equalization is achieved by tuning $\sigma_i$ according to the perplexity $2^{H(i)}$, where

    $$H(i) = -\sum_j p(j|i) \log_2 p(j|i)$$

    is the entropy.

    Equivalently, this is minimizing the KL divergence $KL(P\|Q)$ between the original and the new neighbor probabilities.

    KL divergence is asymmetric, and in SNE this is a deliberate choice. In the sum $\sum p \log(p/q)$ the penalty is large where $p$ is large and $q$ is small, i.e., points that were close in the original are placed far apart in the picture — the method punishes this severely.

    The opposite error — distant points ending up close together — is barely penalized, because the factor $p$ is small.

    A practical conclusion that matters when reading any SNE/t-SNE plot: closeness in the projection is meaningful, but distance and the size of gaps between clusters are not. The distance between two clusters in a t-SNE plot does not mean they are equally different in reality.

    The main weakness of the original SNE, and the reason t-SNE appeared.

    In a high-dimensional space, vastly more equidistant neighbors fit around a point than on a plane: the volume of a ball grows as $r^d$. Trying to place all the neighbors at the right distance in 2D, the method is forced to herd them together — clusters stick together into a dense clump in the center, and the structure is lost.

    t-SNE solves this by replacing the Gaussian kernel in the projection space with a Student's t-distribution with one degree of freedom (the Cauchy distribution). It has heavy tails: moderately distant points don't need to move a huge distance to get a small neighbor probability, so the clusters get the chance to separate and the picture stops clumping.

    1. What is SNE and what problem does it solve?

      SNE (Stochastic Neighbor Embedding) is a probabilistic dimensionality reduction method. It models distances as neighbor probabilities and looks for a projection $y_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ in which the neighbor probabilities in the low-dimensional space match the original ones.

    2. How is the neighbor probability defined in SNE?

      $p(j|i)$ — the probability that $x_j$ is a neighbor of $x_i$ — is given by a Gaussian kernel with width $\sigma_i$. In the projection, $q(j|i)$ is defined similarly. The KL divergence between $p$ and $q$ is optimized.

    3. What is perplexity in SNE and why is it needed?

      The perplexity $2^{H(i)}$ sets the "effective number of neighbors" of point $x_i$. The parameter $\sigma_i$ is tuned individually for each point so that the perplexity matches the given value — this adapts the scale to the local density.

    4. What does t-SNE improve compared to SNE?

      t-SNE replaces the Gaussian kernel in the projection space with a Student's distribution (a $t$-distribution with 1 degree of freedom). This eliminates the "crowding in the center" problem (the crowding problem) and separates clusters better.

    5. What is perplexity in simple terms and how is it related to $\sigma_i$?

      Perplexity is the effective number of neighbors of a point. A single value is set for the whole dataset (usually 5–50), and the kernel width $\sigma_i$ is tuned individually for each point so that the entropy of its neighbor distribution matches the given perplexity. A dense region gets a small $\sigma_i$, a sparse one a large one.

    6. Why does SNE minimize specifically $KL(P\|Q)$ rather than a symmetric measure?

      The asymmetry is intentional: the penalty is large where $p$ is large and $q$ is small, i.e., when points close in the original are placed far apart in the projection. The opposite error is barely penalized. That's why in SNE/t-SNE plots the closeness of points is meaningful, but distances between clusters and the size of gaps can't be interpreted.

    7. What is the crowding problem?

      In a high-dimensional space, far more equidistant neighbors fit around a point than on a plane — volume grows as $r^d$. When projecting, all the neighbors have to be herded together, and clusters stick together. t-SNE solves this with the heavy tails of the Student's distribution in the projection space.

  65. t-SNE

    t-SNE is an improvement on SNE that fixes the problems with the tails of the distribution and the crowding of projections.

    In the simplest terms: it's the same SNE, but with a "softer spring" at long distances. In SNE, for a point to count as distant, it had to be moved very far away, and there wasn't enough room for everyone — clusters stuck together into a clump. t-SNE changes the distribution in the projection to one where a moderate distance is already enough, and the clusters get the chance to separate.

    Instead of a Gaussian distribution, a Student's t-distribution with one degree of freedom is used to define $q(i,j)$:

    $$q(i,j) = \frac{(1 + \|y_i - y_j\|^2)^{-1}}{\displaystyle\sum_{k \ne l}(1 + \|y_k - y_l\|^2)^{-1}}$$

    A symmetric distribution $p(i,j)$ is also introduced:

    $$p(i,j) = \frac{1}{2\ell}\,(p(j|i) + p(i|j))$$

    We optimize:

    $$\sum_{i \ne j} p(i,j)\log q(i,j) \to \max$$

    Let's compare how fast the "neighbor probability" falls off as the distance $r$ in the projection grows:

    • Gaussian kernel (SNE): $\exp(-r^2)$ — at distance 3 the value is already about $10^{-4}$;
    • Student's t-distribution (t-SNE): $(1 + r^2)^{-1}$ — at distance 3 the value is about $0.1$, i.e., a thousand times larger.

    What this changes in practice: for two points to "count as distant", SNE had to spread them a huge distance apart — but space on the plane is finite, and the points got crammed together. In t-SNE the same effect is achieved with a moderate distance, so the clusters separate easily and visible gaps appear between them.

    Hence the characteristic look of t-SNE plots: clear, separate islands instead of a single blurry cloud.

    In the original SNE the probabilities were conditional: $p(j|i)$ and $p(i|j)$ could differ greatly if the points had different local densities. A lone outlier point got tiny $p(j|i)$ to all its neighbors, barely affected the loss function, and therefore flew off to an arbitrary place in the projection.

    Symmetrization $p(i,j) = \frac{1}{2\ell}(p(j|i) + p(i|j))$ guarantees each point a minimum total weight in the objective function. Outliers stop being "invisible" to the optimization, and the gradients become noticeably simpler and more stable.

    • The Student's distribution "spreads out" the points and reduces crowding in the projection.
    • Symmetrizing the probabilities makes the method more stable.

    This is the most common source of false conclusions among beginners — almost everything that seems obvious in a t-SNE plot can't be interpreted:

    • The distance between clusters means nothing. Two clusters close together are no "more similar" than two far apart: the method optimizes only local neighborhoods, and the global geometry is unconstrained.
    • Cluster size means nothing. t-SNE equalizes densities, so a dense cluster and a sparse cloud can occupy the same area.
    • Gaps between clusters don't measure separation in the data. Their width is an optimization artifact.
    • Cluster shape is arbitrary. Elongation or curvature is a property of the layout, not of the data.
    • Small groups may be noise. At low perplexity, t-SNE draws "clusters" even on a uniform random cloud of points. Always look at the result for several perplexity values.

    Only one thing in t-SNE is meaningful: which points ended up in the same group. Everything else is decoration.

    1. Which problem of SNE does t-SNE solve?

      SNE suffers from the "crowding problem": in the low-dimensional space there isn't enough room to place the neighbors correctly, and the points crowd together in the center. t-SNE uses the Student's t-distribution instead of the Gaussian — its "heavy tails" give more room to separate the clusters.

    2. How is $q(i,j)$ defined in t-SNE and why does it matter?

      $q(i,j) = (1 + \|y_i - y_j\|^2)^{-1} / Z$ — a t-distribution with 1 degree of freedom. At moderate distances in the projection, $q(i,j)$ is much larger than the Gaussian — this "stretches" the clusters apart and separates them better.

    3. What is perplexity in t-SNE and how does it affect the result?

      Perplexity is a hyperparameter that sets the "effective number of neighbors" (usually 5–50). Low perplexity focuses on local structure, with many small clusters. High perplexity captures global structure, and clusters merge. The visualization is sensitive to this parameter.

    4. Why can't t-SNE be used to train a classifier?

      t-SNE doesn't provide a reproducible transformation for new points — it's a visualization method, not a preprocessing one. Each run gives a different result. The distances between clusters in the projection aren't interpretable. For feature extraction, PCA or UMAP are used.

    5. Why do the heavy tails of the t-distribution solve the crowding problem?

      The Gaussian kernel $\exp(-r^2)$ falls off extremely fast: for points to "count as distant", they must be spread very far apart, and there isn't enough room on the plane. For the Student's $(1+r^2)^{-1}$, the value at distance 3 is about a thousand times larger, so a moderate distance is enough, and the clusters get the chance to separate.

    6. Why are the probabilities $p(i,j)$ symmetrized in t-SNE?

      The conditional probabilities $p(j|i)$ and $p(i|j)$ differ greatly when local densities differ, and an outlier point with tiny probabilities barely affected the loss function, flying off to an arbitrary place. Symmetrization gives each point a guaranteed minimum weight in the objective function and simplifies the gradients.

    7. What can and can't be interpreted in a t-SNE plot?

      Only the composition of groups can be interpreted — which points ended up close together. You can't interpret the distances between clusters, their sizes, shapes, or the width of the gaps: all of these are optimization artifacts. At low perplexity the method draws "clusters" even on a uniform random cloud, so the result is examined at several perplexity values.

  66. UMAP↗

    UMAP (Uniform Manifold Approximation and Projection) is a dimensionality reduction method based on constructing and approximating the local topology of the data. Its main goal is to preserve both the local and the global structure of the data when projecting into a lower-dimensional space.

    In the simplest terms: first we build a "who is whose neighbor" network, and then we lay this network out on a plane so that neighbors stay close together. The difference from t-SNE is that UMAP also tries to preserve the relative arrangement of groups, not just the composition of neighborhoods within them.

    Behind the word manifold lies a simple idea: real data rarely fills the entire feature space.

    Take face photos of $100\times100$ pixels — formally that's 10,000 dimensions. But a random set of 10,000 numbers gives noise, not a face. Real faces occupy a tiny curved subset of this space, parameterized by a small number of factors: head rotation, lighting, expression.

    This subset is what's called a manifold. All methods like UMAP and t-SNE assume that the data lies on it and try to "unfold" it — roughly the way a geographic map unfolds the surface of a globe onto a plane.

    The idea of UMAP is based on the assumption that the data lies on a manifold. The method builds a nearest-neighbor (k-NN) graph in the original space and approximates its counterpart in the projection space.

    Steps:

    • Build a weighted neighbor graph in the input space: each point is connected to its $k$ nearest neighbors.
    • Define neighbor probabilities, taking distances and scale parameters into account.
    • Project the points $x_i \in \mathbb{R}^n$ to $y_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ (where $d \ll n$), minimizing the discrepancy between the graphs.

    Let $\|y_i - y_j\| = d_{ij}$. Then UMAP minimizes the discrepancy between the graphs using the cross-entropy between the adjacency distributions in the original and target spaces:

    $$\mathcal{L} = -\sum_{(i,j)} \bigl[w_{ij}\log\sigma(d_{ij}) + (1 - w_{ij})\log(1 - \sigma(d_{ij}))\bigr]$$

    where $\sigma$ is the sigmoid.

    In UMAP, the distance between vertices $i$ and $j$ with coordinates $\mathbf{y}_i$ and $\mathbf{y}_j$ is defined as follows:

    $$\frac{-2ab\,\|\mathbf{y}_i - \mathbf{y}_j\|_2^{2(b-1)}}{1 + \|\mathbf{y}_i - \mathbf{y}_j\|_2^2}\,w((x_i, x_j))\,(\mathbf{y}_i - \mathbf{y}_j),$$

    where $a, b$ are hyperparameters.

    • UMAP, unlike t-SNE, can better preserve the global structure of the data.
    • The parameters n_neighbors and min_dist control the balance between local and global structure.
    • UMAP is faster than t-SNE and scales to large datasets.

    UMAP has only two truly important hyperparameters, and they control different things:

    • n_neighbors — how many neighbors to consider when building the graph. This is the scale of view: a small value (5–15) makes the method look through a microscope and reveals small local groups; a large one (50–200) gives a bird's-eye view and preserves the overall structure, blurring fine details.
    • min_dist — the minimum distance between points in the projection. This is layout cosmetics: a small value (0.0–0.1) packs points into dense clumps, which is convenient for picking out clusters; a large one (0.5–0.9) spreads them more evenly, which better shows the internal structure of groups.

    Important: min_dist does not affect which points are considered neighbors — it only changes the appearance of the result. n_neighbors, on the other hand, changes the partition itself.

    Are there other methods?

    • There is a transform for new points — unlike t-SNE. So UMAP is formally usable as a preprocessing step inside a pipeline, not just for pictures.
    • But be careful using it for features. UMAP coordinates are nonlinear and unstable with respect to the random seed; including them in a pipeline requires fixing random_state and checking for leakage — UMAP must be fitted on train only.
    • Distances between clusters are more interpretable than in t-SNE, but still with a caveat. UMAP preserves global structure better, but the absolute sizes of gaps and cluster areas still have no direct meaning.
    • Clusters appear even on noise. Like t-SNE, the method will draw nice groups on a uniform random cloud. Before interpreting, it's worth checking the result at different n_neighbors values.
    • Scaling is required. The method relies on distances, so features in different units of measurement will produce a meaningless neighbor graph.

    Practice problem on the platform

    1. What is UMAP and how does it build a projection?

      UMAP builds a weighted k-nearest-neighbor graph in the original space, then looks for a projection $y_i \in \mathbb{R}^d$ by minimizing the cross-entropy between the neighbor graphs in the original and target spaces.

    2. How is UMAP better than t-SNE?

      UMAP is faster (scales to large datasets), better preserves the global structure of the data, and supports transforming new points. t-SNE is visualization only, and each run is unique.

    3. How do the parameters n_neighbors and min_dist affect the projection?

      n_neighbors: larger — emphasis on global structure, blurrier clusters. Smaller — local structure, crisp small clusters. min_dist: larger — points spread out evenly. Smaller — dense, well-separated clusters.

    4. When should you choose UMAP, t-SNE, or PCA?

      PCA is a fast linear method, good for data with linear structure. t-SNE gives the best cluster visualization but is slow. UMAP balances speed and quality, supports transforming new points, and is suitable for feature extraction and visualizing large datasets.

    5. What does the manifold assumption underlying UMAP mean?

      Real data doesn't fill the entire feature space: face photos formally lie in 10,000 dimensions, but a random set of numbers gives noise, not a face. Real objects occupy a small curved subset parameterized by a few factors. UMAP tries to unfold this subset onto a plane.

    6. How does n_neighbors differ from min_dist in its effect?

      n_neighbors sets the scale of view and affects the partition itself: small values reveal small local groups, large ones preserve the global structure. min_dist affects only the appearance of the layout — how tightly the points pack into clumps — and doesn't change which points are considered neighbors.

    7. What practical advantage does UMAP have over t-SNE?

      UMAP has a transform for new points: a fitted projection can be applied to data that wasn't present when it was built, so the method is usable as a preprocessing step in a pipeline. t-SNE doesn't offer this — each run builds the layout from scratch. In addition, UMAP is noticeably faster and better preserves global structure.
